Coming in 2027: Apple’s $1,000 home robot for FaceTime and switching on lights
2026-11-02

Coming in 2027: Apple’s $1,000 home robot for FaceTime and switching on lights

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Apple’s new CEO, John Ternus, is apparently pushing the tech company’s development of household robots—and the first to debut may be a home robot designed to autonomously roam around while supporting FaceTime calls and switching lights on whenever a person enters the room. People who worked on Apple’s robotic projects told The Information that the AI-driven home robot, code-named J595, could be named HomeView Ultra in line with Apple’s new HomeView stationary smart display. The company is reportedly aiming to launch the robotic product with a possible price tag of $1,000 or more by 2027, although development delays may push that back to 2028. The earliest prototypes of the home robot first came out in 2018 and received approval from former Apple CEO Tim Cook to continue the project. The Information describes Apple’s engineers as trying to give the robot more capabilities beyond being a “fancy way to make FaceTime calls” in order to justify a high price tag, even as the team has spent years refining the software and incorporating more AI technologies. However, the robot’s few reported features seem underwhelming so far when compared with existing smart home devices and systems, or even a run-of-the-mill robot vacuum with a clearer use case for households. Is there a market for a smart home device costing more than $1,000 that moves around while hosting FaceTime calls, automatically handling room lights, and occasionally ordering the family cat to get off the dining table? One of the Information’s sources also described Apple as having a very small team of about 10 “true roboticists,” with many members of the initial engineering team having departed over the years. The software side boasts a significantly larger team of approximately 300 people. Still, Ternus has been backing this home robot as well as a separate Apple project focused on developing robots for household tasks like folding clothes, according to The Information. The latter would presumably require robotic arms and manipulators, though it’s unclear if that means Apple has ambitions to develop a general-purpose humanoid robot for household use like robotics startups 1X and Figure AI. From a technical and safety standpoint, Apple’s initial focus on a limited-purpose home robot still makes much more sense right now than any attempt to develop a general-purpose autonomous robot that could pose greater risks when operating near humans. The question is whether Apple can find enough customers willing to pay whatever it charges for a limited-purpose home robot.

Claude’s new rules against game cloning won’t stop the AI copying boom
2026-11-02

Claude’s new rules against game cloning won’t stop the AI copying boom

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Last week, Anthropic introduced a new system prompt designed to stop its Claude models from re-creating specific, existing video games. While that move could help shield Anthropic from some liability, it’s unlikely to slow much of the massive wave of AI-assisted game porting and copying that has emerged in recent months. A social media user and AI decompilation enthusiast going by the name “chasm” was among the first to note the prompt change late last week, saying a new rule seemed to be added to the Claude Code prompt on or around October 27. That rule—included in a json file under the name “tengu_heron_brook”—reads as follows: Do build original games, games in a genre, and games from the user’s own designs and assets. Don’t build a recreation of a specific existing game, even if it is renamed or called original. XDA later independently verified that the new rule appears in version 2.1.314 of the Claude Code binary, which was released on October 30. XDA also reported that the “tengu_heron_brook” rule had been used for other purposes in earlier versions and that the Claude Opus 5.5 model specifically seemed to have its own distinct and independent instructions restricting game recreations as part of its system prompt bundle. Following chasm’s initial report, Anthropic software engineer Addy Osmani confirmed on social media that the company had “added two sentences to Claude Code’s prompt on Opus 5.5 to steer it away from recreating someone else’s specific game because using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy.” He added that the new prompt was designed to still allow “original games, genre games, and games built from your own designs and assets,” even as a handful of issue reports on Anthropic’s GitHub suggest the new prompt is getting in the way of some game development use cases. We added two sentences to Claude Code’s prompt on Opus 5.5 to steer it away from recreating someone else’s specific game because using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy. Original games, genre games, and games built from your own designs and assets are meant to keep working. We’ll continue to iterate to ensure we’re striking the right balance. In the meantime, if Claude turns down something that’s yours, send it my way. — Addy Osmani (@addyosmani) October 30, 2026 Anthropic’s new prompt comes as AI coding tools are powering a deluge of game projects based on existing work in one way or another. That includes thrown-together copies of game demos that are making some developers afraid to show their early work publicly, as well as thousands of AI-powered decompilation and porting projects for retro games. Thanks to AI tools, what used to be a time-consuming process requiring painstaking human effort is now orders of magnitude more efficient (though some veteran coders complain that these vibe-coded ports and decomp projects fail to clear the same quality bar as human-centered efforts). The trend has gotten so bad that decomp.dev—a years-old community devoted to the “brutally difficult, time-consuming task” of game decompilation—announced it was shutting down last month. Community maintainer Luke Street wrote that quickly improving AI tools had made it clear that “community aspect of decompilation has been broken” as newcomers “rac[e] to port games, especially utilizing decompilations that they did not contribute to.” “Decomp.dev was originally designed to gamify decompilation: watching the progress go up was a fun way to stay motivated when every fraction of a percent took real human effort,” Street wrote. “Today, that same idea has warped decompilation into a hollow numbers game, exacerbated by folks who just want to burn tokens to make number go up or ‘port game to PC,’ all else be damned. While Anthropic’s new prompt can be seen as a noble effort to push back against the flood of AI-powered, vibe-coded copying, it seems unlikely to have much impact overall. That’s primarily because eager game copiers can simply shift to any of the other frontier models that haven’t introduced such restrictions. And even if the AI industry as a whole began to bar this kind of use case, coders could switch to cheaper open-weight models that aren’t subject to these kinds of restrictions and are not all that far behind in capabilities. The prompt may have a more immediate benefit for Anthropic, helping protect the company from potential liability for copyright infringement in these projects. We’ve seen how AI companies face legal headaches if their models generate images based on copyrighted works, for instance. As Osmani noted in confirming the new anti-game copying prompt, “Using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy”—a policy that’s surely in place to protect the company legally. Decompilation efforts based on reverse engineering code generally pass legal muster as long as “expressive elements” like art and sound assets are not included in the project. But there’s no guarantee that individual vibe-coders using Claude would be so careful with their efforts, even with Anthropic guardrails against IP theft in place. On the other hand, there’s a risk that Anthropic’s efforts could prove too restrictive, limiting transformative game projects that build off existing work. Take, for instance, the increasingly popular trend of using AI to merge two different game concepts into one, adding Mario to Elden Ring or Spider-Man to Batman: Arkham Knight, for instance. Are these the kinds of “recreations of a specific existing game” that Claude’s new prompt should block? Or are they transformative works that are legally and creatively distinct from what inspired them? And how does the use of copyright-protected characters and images factor in? Beyond mashups, how should a model like Claude ensure there’s a difference between a direct re-creation of Tetris (ostensibly disallowed by the new prompt) and a mere game in the Tetris genre (ostensibly allowed by the prompt)? These are issues that even courts can have trouble adjudicating in the best circumstances. Asking an AI model to reliably draw the line between acceptable and unacceptable game clones is a tall order, even with a new two-sentence prompt in place.

China publicly discloses plan for a lunar landing within three years
2026-11-02

China publicly discloses plan for a lunar landing within three years

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Chinese space officials confirmed this weekend that the Asian nation plans to land its astronauts on the Moon within three years. At the Seventh Manned Spaceflight Conference in Beijing, officials, including Wang Xiang, chief designer for the lunar exploration program, laid out the country’s most publicly detailed plans yet for a series of missions leading up to a crewed lunar landing “before 2030.” China’s lunar landings will involve a lunar-orbit rendezvous similar to what NASA did in the 1960s and early 1970s with the Apollo program. However, lacking a rocket as powerful as the Saturn V vehicle, the crew and lunar lander will launch separately to the Moon. According to slides shared on social media, the current plan involves: The public release of this new information provides a timeline for gauging China’s technical progress in its efforts to land humans on the Moon before 2030 and beat NASA and the United States back to the lunar surface. Notably, the plan (for now, at least) omits a mission similar to Apollo 8 or Artemis II, which involved a crew flying out to the Moon and either entering orbit or passing close by before returning to Earth. The Mengzhou vehicle should still get a workout closer to Earth with humans on board as part of missions carrying astronauts to Tiangong, China’s space station in low-Earth orbit. NASA has set a target of landing humans on the Moon by 2028, a year or so ahead of China. The United States, which has been working on the Space Launch System rocket for 15 years and the Orion spacecraft even longer, has a more established rocket and crew vehicle. But there are major questions surrounding the development of a lunar lander, for which both SpaceX and Blue Origin have multibillion-dollar development contracts. Depending on how these projects go, a US lunar landing could be delayed to 2030 or even later. Although it is virtually impossible for outsiders to glean information about technical milestones achieved inside China’s space program, the country is clearly making credible progress toward reaching the Moon. For example, in August 2025, the China Manned Space Agency successfully tested a high-fidelity mock-up of its 26-ton Lanyue lunar lander. The test used giant tethers to simulate lunar gravity as the vehicle fired its main engines and fine-control thrusters to land on a cratered surface and take off from a location near Beijing. Recently, the Mengzhou spacecraft for the 2027 test atop a Long March 10A rocket arrived at the Wenchang Space Launch Site in Hainan, China, where the mission will launch. US government assessments of the Chinese space program are restricted, but lawmakers are clearly taking the threat seriously. In the last 12 months, the US Congress and White House have given broad latitude to NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman to streamline the Artemis Program to land humans on the Moon. He has moved swiftly to cancel the Lunar Gateway space station and planned upgrades to the Space Launch System rocket. And just last week, Isaacman announced plans to help Blue Origin develop a lunar lander by finding a way to boost the Orion spacecraft into low-lunar orbit. “We have the mandate, we have the resources, and the support from our stakeholders to be able to execute, and that’s what we intend to do,” he said at the time. For a couple of years, it has been clear that China has made a major commitment to landing astronauts on the Moon, preferably before the United States returns to the lunar surface, to score a major geopolitical win. Beyond this near-term competition, both NASA and China’s space program are building capabilities to establish a long-term presence at the Moon’s South Pole. As part of this second phase of the contest, Isaacman committed NASA to the development of a base on the surface of the Moon over the next decade.

F1 in Mexico City: Yet another Ferrari fiasco, but that’s not all
2026-11-02

F1 in Mexico City: Yet another Ferrari fiasco, but that’s not all

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Formula 1 has a new World Drivers’ Champion. Just a few months after turning 20, Kimi Antonelli sealed his first title this weekend at Mexico City, becoming the sport’s youngest-ever champion. Elsewhere, there was drama aplenty, perhaps a consequence of the thin air affecting decision-making in some cases. The race was Ferrari’s to win, but yet again, the team handed victory to a rival this season, in this case Red Bull. Further down the grid, we saw a last-minute driver change in the Haas team, synchronized retirements from both Aston Martins, and the wheels literally coming off Cadillac Racing. And there was I, expecting a dull weekend. It might not be as high as Pikes Peak, but at an elevation of 7,350 feet (2,240 m) above sea level, the Autódromo Hermanos Rodríguez provides a unique challenge on the F1 calendar. Wings that would create huge amounts of downforce at circuits like Monaco just generate Monza-like levels in Mexico, and there’s little slipstream effect when following another car. Cooling tends to be a problem, too, with insufficient airflow across radiators, and obviously engine power is reduced with less oxygen to burn. The track is very flat, with a rather low-grip surface and mostly medium- and slow-speed corners, including a processional route through a stadium that was built in the 1990s, plus a very long run from the start into turn 1. Friday’s first practice saw 10 rookie drivers substitute in for series regulars—each team must run a young or test driver in each of their two cars at least twice in a season. Make that six times in the case of Cadillac? The American startup team wants IndyCar star Colton Herta to race in F1, but Herta needs a total of 40 points over three years to get the requisite F1 super license. Sadly, his IndyCar record was too inconsistent—10th place in 2023, 2nd in 2024, but then 7th again in 2025—so he entered F2 this year, both to learn the circuits and to finish well enough in that championship to earn the remaining points. That hasn’t happened, but you also earn a point for completing 100 km in an FP1 session, and Herta stepped into the Cadillac in Barcelona, Italy, Hungary, and in Texas. His run in Italy was cut short by unreliability, so Cadillac put him in the car again this weekend, hoping it would be the final run. But it turns out they hadn’t added the numbers properly. Rather than pulling Herta’s IndyCar results from the official scoring, the team apparently relied on IndyCar’s erroneous website. Instead of starting the year with 36 super license points, Herta only had 35, and with four successful FP1 runs under his belt, he still needs one more point if the team wants to replace an underperforming Valteri Bottas next year, something that should happen with another run next time out in Brazil. It’s not the first time that paperwork has proved to be a problem for the American team, which almost lost a chance to enter the sport after FIA emails went to its spam folder in 2024. Friday’s second practice session also had more action than normal. Max Verstappen and Liam Lawson both sat out FP1 so rookies could drive their cars, which may have contributed to both being too eager to set a fast time in FP2. As Lawson’s Racing Bull got held up by Carlos Sainz’ slow Williams in front, Verstappen wanted past in the Red Bull, but both drivers were intransigent, and neither was prepared to yield. Paint was traded and cars were damaged, and both drivers were handed a one-place grid penalty for failing to behave themselves. FP2 also probably marked the end of Esteban Ocon’s F1 career, at least for now. The French driver was upset with his team about their choice of tires for his long run, which evidently escalated once he was out of the car. On Saturday, Ocon had been benched and replaced with Ryo Hirakawa, Haas’ reserve driver and two-time World Endurance Champion with Toyota. Haas had already announced that Ocon was not rejoining the team in 2027, opting instead for F1 driver Rafael Camara, who will probably race for the team in Brazil, as Hirakawa will be racing his Toyota that weekend. For most of this year, Ferrari has had a particular form. The team often tops the practice times on Friday but then fails to follow up on Saturday, often in the final qualifying session. That didn’t happen in Mexico, and Lewis Hamilton was able to claim his first pole position for Ferrari and his 105th in the sport. George Russell’s Mercedes qualified second, followed by Charles Leclerc’s Ferrari. Verstappen should have been fourth, but that Friday clash saw Antonelli moved into that spot. As it happens, the grid demotion put Verstappen on the clean side of the track, which he used to his advantage. Both Ferraris got off the line well, as did Verstappen and Antonelli. Russell’s hybrid system derated halfway down the straight, ruining his race well before the first corner. With Hamilton in the lead and Leclerc in second place, the plan should have been obvious: settle into a 1-2 and win the race. Unfortunately, the idea of supporting his teammate is utterly alien to Leclerc, who showed us why he has 27 pole positions but only nine wins in eight years. Once more this year, the Ferraris raced each other as if a championship were at stake, and once more they snatched defeat from the jaws of victory. Going into turn 4, both drivers went off track and rejoined. The race stewards had been extremely clear that leaving the track and gaining a permanent advantage at turns 1 or 4 on the first lap would result in a penalty unless the driver gave the place back. Hamilton did not gain a lasting advantage, but Leclerc did, staying in clean air up front and building enough of a lead that the paltry five-second penalty that was eventually handed down kept him ahead of Hamilton after their stops. But it didn’t keep him ahead of Verstappen, who stopped at the same time as Leclerc but emerged in the lead, a lead he would keep until the end. It wasn’t an easy drive to the checkered flag, however. We’re accustomed to Lance Stroll’s Aston Martin breaking down on track and triggering a safety car or a virtual safety car, and this race was no exception. It happened early, on lap 7, while the other Aston Martin, driven by Fernando Alonso, retired to its garage. Cadillac has also had plenty of reliability problems this year, usually with its front brakes overheating. It was even more serious in Mexico: On lap 44, Bottas’ front left suspension failed as he approached turn 1. The heavy crash forced a red flag to repair the barrier, bringing the race to a halt and necessitating a second standing start. Verstappen kept the lead, demonstrating the superior power output of the Red Bull power unit, even at altitude, over Ferrari’s. A furious Hamilton passed Leclerc, who compounded his original error by badly locking his front tires, wearing a flat spot down to the cord. The safety car came out again on lap 59, when Arvid Lindblad’s Racing Bull was taken out by McLaren’s Oscar Piastri. Up front, Hamilton followed Verstappen by a few tenths of a second but without ever having the speed to try an overtake. The Ferrari might not have had the legs to get past the Red Bull, but its tolerance for riding the curbs, plus the small turbo’s punch out of low-speed corners, was sufficient to give it the edge over 2025 champion Lando Norris’ McLaren, which finished in fifth behind Leclerc. After the race, Ferrari team principal Fred Vasseur said, “I always expect everyone to show respect for the brand, the team, our partners and, above all, the 2,000 people working for us back home. They put an incredible amount of effort into what we do, working day and night at the factory to improve the car and bring upgrades to the track. That’s why missing out on a win in this way is difficult to accept.” “The core thing to remember, which maybe comes with age, is that every decision you make in the car impacts all the people that work in the background for the whole team,” said a frustrated Hamilton after the race. “A part of our contract is we have to work as a team, which we could have easily done today. So it’s a shame to see us lose as we did. And that will continue unless action is taken, and a real deep, serious conversation is had, so it shouldn’t happen moving forward.” Hamilton pointed to Leclerc’s “do-or-die, either I win or none of us do” attitude as a problem, ruing the loss of what could have been the team’s best result all year. “It was just unnecessary,” he said. “I think we could have easily had that 1-2, which would have been huge for the team and for everyone back at the factory who’s worked so hard to bring us these upgrades.” Other teams have laid down clear rules to determine the way teammates race each other—at Mercedes, Toto Wolff began making Hamilton and Nico Rosberg pay for any damage they caused to the cars if they came together on track, and McLaren had its famous Papaya rules. At Ferrari, both drivers are free to race each other, and both are jockeying to be seen as team leader for next year. Now Vasseur has to try to get both Hamilton and Leclerc under control, because if Ferrari keeps giving away race wins like this, his position has to be increasingly untenable.

The world has a new plan to save nature, but it still needs the money
2026-11-02

The world has a new plan to save nature, but it still needs the money

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
The world is set to miss its 2030 conservation goals as finance and action have fallen short, leaders recognized at a UN biodiversity summit in Armenia’s capital, where they agreed to step up efforts after a final overnight session. Representatives from almost 200 countries gathered in Yerevan over the past two weeks to review progress on a landmark agreement set four years ago to protect about a third of the world’s land and seas by 2030. The COP17 talks on biodiversity concluded on Saturday with a recognition that conservation efforts were so far too slow to realistically achieve the goals of the 2022 Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework. A plan was agreed to accelerate progress ahead of the next summit in Ecuador in two years, dubbed the Yerevan Pathway. It would mean redoubling efforts to tackle subsidies and incentives that are harmful to nature, increasing financial, scientific, and technical support for developing countries, and strengthening monitoring and reporting mechanisms. Steven Guilbeault, former Canadian minister of environment and climate change, said that finance remained one of the biggest obstacles to achieving global biodiversity goals. The fact that a fund agreed at the last meeting—for companies profiting from genetic materials to voluntarily contribute to—stood largely empty was “especially disappointing.” “We need to mobilize substantially more public and private finance, reform subsidies that harm nature, and ensure that funding reaches the countries and communities on the front lines of the nature crisis,” he said. Catherine Weller, global policy director at the campaign group Fauna & Flora, said the Yerevan agreement was welcome but also noted that funding fell far short of a pledge that had been a key part of the 2022 deal. “In a world of trillionaires, we can certainly afford to spend $200 billion a year on protecting and restoring the very thing keeping us all alive: nature,” she said. Conference organizers said governments, philanthropists, and businesses had announced close to $2 billion in new financial commitments to coincide with the Armenian gathering. This included a commitment by the Netherlands to contribute $500 million to the Tropical Forest Forever Facility, a Brazilian initiative to pay countries to keep their forests standing. The pledge was announced in the Dutch parliament the day after Brazilian leader Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva was re-elected as president, to the relief of many attending the biodiversity summit. After the close of the conference, Carina Pimenta, Brazil’s national secretary for bioeconomy, highlighted Lula’s record of protecting the Amazon rainforest and spoke of hope. “At a time of growing global uncertainty, the Yerevan Declaration sends a powerful message: world leaders recognize that protecting biodiversity is essential to food security, livelihoods, and economic resilience,” she said. A growing number of countries to have drawn up national diversity plans, and the new pledge to the Brazilian fund, were “reasons for cautious optimism,” said Brian O’Donnell, director at Campaign for Nature. But international funding for protected and conserved areas has fallen in a number of countries over years of disruption from war and high energy prices, with a 27 percent decline in environmental funding for the 2026-30 period. © 2026 The Financial Times Ltd. Ars Technica - All content
This $48 e-paper display can be whatever you want it to be
2026-11-02

This $48 e-paper display can be whatever you want it to be

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
One of the most useful gadgets in my kitchen is now a screen that never asks me for anything. I recently bought a $48 DIY display kit from Seeed Studio (yes, that’s three E’s), developed in collaboration with TRMNL, that lets me display whatever I want, using whichever software ecosystem I prefer. I previously built something similar by modding a Lenovo ThinkSmart View, which was selling for around $30 on eBay during a liquidation. The Lenovo device was only ever intended for business-to-business video, but it does a decent job of running a fork of LineageOS. Unfortunately, its limited RAM makes it sluggish. So when I came across the TRMNL kit and its e-paper display, I was sold. I’d have an always-on display that shows me the information I want, with no subscription required. And since the screen populates automatically, I would never have to wrestle with a slow interface. The Seeed Studio kit and TRMNL ecosystem aren’t trying to compete with products like the recently announced Apple HomeView, a $299 home hub designed to get the whole family interacting with it. If anything, the TRMNL kit is the HomeView’s antithesis. It’s designed to be entirely passive: The e-paper display stays on, cycling through potentially useful information on your desk or in your kitchen, from calendars and weather to news and all sorts of ideas thought up by its community members. TRMNL, the platform company, was founded by indie hardware enthusiast Ryan Kulp in 2023. Its first e-paper device launched via crowdfunding in 2024. Seeed Studio provides the hardware. The established Shenzhen open hardware company is best known for its XIAO microcontroller boards. In March 2025, Seeed teased an XIAO e-paper panel on social media, prompting a community member to ask whether it could run TRMNL firmware. The question brought the two teams together, and in July 2025, they announced an official partnership to develop a DIY e-paper kit combining Seeed’s hardware designs with TRMNL’s firmware packages. Seeed designs and manufactures the hardware, while TRMNL maintains the firmware, plugins, and hosted service. But you’ll still need to make some decisions about how to set up the kit. There are three ways to get it running, depending on your budget and how comfortable you are with managing software yourself. The stock TRMNL setup connects you with the company’s “BYOD ecosystem” (bring your own device), letting you use trmnl.com as your information server with a 14-day free trial. After that, you’ll need to pay a one-time $50 license fee to keep your device connected to TRMNL servers. If you’d rather go your own way, Seeed offers software options, including Home Assistant compatibility, that don’t require a TRMNL connection or license. You can also access the SenseCraft Seeedash platform, which includes a plugin repository and design workspace. It’s worth checking out the options before committing to a $50 TRMNL license. The browser-based platform also includes a firmware-flashing tool Seeed describes as simple enough for anyone to use. If you already have a server up and running, you can point the device right at it. Terminus is TRMNL’s open source container for this setup. The Terminus GitHub page describes the BYOS as providing “privacy first functionality by empowering you to run your own private server, network, and devices. You own the stack and the data.” The e-paper display and control board simply ping an API to retrieve the information to display. You can even write plugins in HTML, which the screen renders as a bitmap. The trade-off is that your server needs to stay online, and maintaining it may bring some system-admin headaches some users may want to avoid. But hey, that’s why there are options. Two official case designs are available for 3D printing: a triangular prism and an L-shaped case. I went for the latter, and although I’d hoped assembly would be as simple as plugging everything in and slotting the pieces into place, it proved to be a bit more involved. The kit came with all the hardware I needed, with one slight hiccup: I’d forgotten to order a case or display stand. Fortunately, TRMNL is a proper DIY project, and there are print files available for recommended stand setups. A quick print at my local library’s Bambu printer and $8 solved the problem. I probably should have chosen something other than “dealer’s choice” for the PLA color, as I ended up with a bright canary yellow device case. At least it stands out on my kitchen countertop. I’d recommend trying the prism shell design over the L-style case I chose because the latter required me to cut a channel for the ribbon cable extension. It wasn’t a huge deal for a project like this, but you may need to do some extra work and fiddle with the fit. You’ll also need needle-nose pliers to remove the print’s support trees from the narrow space. Before trying to fit everything into the printed case, I wanted to make sure the hardware worked, so I connected the parts on my workbench. The case-less assembly couldn’t be simpler: Connect the battery or a USB-C power source, plug the display directly into the board, and you’re good to go. From there, connect your phone or tablet to the device’s Wi-Fi and enter your local Wi-Fi credentials. If you attempt this build, be aware that it involves some delicate parts. The FPC is extremely fragile and can break if you force it to bend in a direction it doesn’t want to go (say, around the sharp angle of an L-shaped case). Getting the cable to conform to the design was challenging; I tried using tweezers to coax the cable around the bend but got nowhere fast. Connecting and routing the ribbon cable extension was the most stressful part of the assembly. The case design just doesn’t accommodate the path the ribbon cable naturally wants to take. Following a helpful tip from a commenter on MakerWorld, I managed to snake the cable through the narrow passage by attaching a bit of paper tape to the end of the ribbon connector. After powering on the kit for the first time, you’ll see the TRMNL logo and a QR code on the e-paper display. Scanning the code redirects you to a “How to set up a new device” guide written by Kulp, the TRMNL creator. The guide had been updated just 15 minutes before I clicked the link—a promising sign of a living document that is constantly evolving. I wasn’t planning to use the trmnl.com service, but I wanted to get a sense of what it offered for its $50 access fee. The 14-day “free” trial is a misnomer, though. Before you can register your device, you’ll need to enter your credit card information and pay $1 for access. After plopping down a buck, the next screen welcomed me to my trial. After registering your kit’s MAC address with trmnl.com, you’ll receive what the service calls a “friendly code,” a much shorter identifier that’s easier to remember than an entire MAC address. From there, you can install plugins to display content on a schedule throughout the day. The options are vast. The marketplace contains standard fare like Google Calendar, weather, and stock quotes, alongside more niche offerings like rotating factoids about the Ferengi Rules of Acquisition in Star Trek. If you’re looking for a hassle-free solution that “just works,” it’s hard to fault the package trmnl.com has put together. I’m a fan of any service that offers a one-time license fee over yet another forever subscription, and I think $50 is a fair price for the people behind the platform to be compensated for their work. After all, it handles all the rendering; the device simply displays information pulled from the APIs. On the Seeed side, there’s SenseCraft, a similar marketplace to trmnl.com’s but without the license fee. The website offers a browser-based flashing tool that promises to “flash official firmware from the browser. No coding. No terminal. Just clicks.” The process was as simple as promised—once I figured out that I needed a USB-C to USB-C cable. I connected the board to my PC via the onboard USB-C port, opened the website in a Chromium-based browser (sorry, Firefox), selected my device from a drop-down menu, and clicked “Flash.” Once the device rebooted, setup followed essentially the same process as it did with TRMNL, minus the credit card. The tradeoff for free access is that Seeed is based in China and operates under its own privacy policy. Your call, but it’s certainly nice to have options. After registering a Seeed account, I entered a code displayed on the device, just as I had with TRMNL. SenseCraft’s marketplace is similarly packed with community contributions, but I had trouble determining which plugins were intended for my device and which might not be compatible. I tried publishing several different feeds to the display, but I couldn’t get them to render. SenseCraft leans heavily into AI-powered custom designs. While the platform offers a bit of everything, much of its content is geared toward the Chinese market, with English-language apps sprinkled throughout. I can see why US users reached out to ask about TRMNL compatibility. As TRMNL puts it, “the goal isn’t for BYOS to match parity with our hosted solution but to provide enough of a pleasant solution for your own customized experience.” Even a modest homelab server should have no trouble running the Terminus package, which acts as an API point that takes in information, renders the designs, and sends them to your e-paper display. The process shouldn’t put meaningful strain on most systems. It’s great that there’s an “easy” version for a fee, but homelabbing is one of my favorite hobbies, and I love self-hosting projects wherever possible. When I first saw the DIY TRMNL/Seeed package, I was hoping I’d be able to control the feed myself. For now, getting Terminus running on your server isn’t as simple as heading to your OS’s Docker app store and downloading a host service. TRMNL has a great guide to walk you through the setup, but if you’re not comfortable working with docker compose files, you may want to pay the one-time license fee and leave the hosting to TRMNL. I won’t lie: I spent some late nights banging my head against the desk trying to get everything running. I’d initially tried hosting it on my main Unraid machine, but I kept running into bridge networking issues. The Terminus package is a multi-container compose stack with pieces like Sidekiq worker, Postgres, Valkey, and a certificate initializer. If those containers can’t communicate with each other, nothing works. Thankfully, I’m a weird nerd who also runs a CasaOS deployment on another machine. After I executed the bin/setup script there, everything worked as it should. The next issue was flashing back to the latest TRMNL firmware package. My unit arrived with FW 1.5.12, well behind the current 1.8.18 release. Flashing proved to be an annoyance again because I struggled to keep the unit in flashing mode and get the web flasher to communicate with the serial port on my Fedora PC. After about 30 minutes of troubleshooting without making progress, I switched to my MacBook Pro, where the process worked on the first try. Go figure. Once you’ve updated the display to the proper firmware and pointed its connections at your server, you can write and design your own display templates. If starting from scratch isn’t your thing, TRMNL’s own plugins repo and a community-maintained catalog are available. A new Extensions Gallery in the Terminus web UI lets you import certain plugins for local hosting. The options are almost overwhelming, and I imagine it will take some time to get everything dialed in exactly as I want it. I still plan to stick with the BYOS route, but the process isn’t as straightforward as I had hoped. For example, the setting for pointing the display at your local server is hidden under the “advanced” settings page during the web portal login. Once I deployed the display in my kitchen, I could get a better sense of how it would fit into my day-to-day life. The 7.5-inch e-paper display is large enough to read from across the room, and all the plugins I had running looked crisp and legible. My son was enamored with the lunar cycles display and commented that “now I’ll always know what moon we have.” That’s where the kit shines: showing little rotating bursts of useful information that you might never have thought to look up before. Seeed Studio says the display can run for three months on battery power. I haven’t had the device long enough to verify that claim, but I’m not sure I need the battery for much more than backup. The device powers via USB just fine, so I’ll probably leave it connected to a nearby hub on the counter. The three-month estimate assumes that the display will update once every six hours. I prefer refreshes closer to once an hour, which would cut battery life significantly. Either way, the battery becomes largely irrelevant if you use a USB power source. The official TRMNL route is starting to look mighty tempting if it means getting the type of headache-free, set-it-and-forget-it Ron Popeil experience I was expecting from this setup. I’ll keep tinkering with the BYOS solutions, but since those still run on the official TRMNL firmware, I may switch back and use the rest of my 14-day trial to see what best suits my needs. The $50 license fee would push my total project cost over $100. That’s not a huge deal, but it’s double what I was expecting. The TRMNL/Seeed Studio collaboration has produced a true DIY device. It will never compete with a home assistant device from Amazon or Apple, but it’s not meant to, and that’s a good thing. Not everyone wants a digital butler waiting for their next command. Sometimes you just want information that’s relevant to you and your life, displayed in a central place in your home.

The AI boom is facing another supply crunch. This time it’s the amount of capital available to feed Silicon Valley’s endless need for cash
2026-11-02

The AI boom is facing another supply crunch. This time it’s the amount of capital available to feed Silicon Valley’s endless need for cash

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
A new supply crunch is closing in on the AI boom as Wall Street starts to reach the limits of how much capital it’s willing to hand over to Silicon Valley. Hyperscalers have already been grappling with shortages of chips, materials and other physical inputs needed for AI infrastructure. They are also running out of political capital as voters push back on the aggressive data center build-out. Now the spigot of money is starting to dry up. OpenAI slashed the target on its latest funding round to $20 billion from $30 billion, sources told the Financial Times. That’s after securing about $11 billion in commitments, less than half of its original goal, according to the Wall Street Journal. A top credit rating agency also dented Silicon Valley’s appeal to debt markets. S&P Global cut its credit outlook on Meta to “negative” from “stable” late Friday, citing negative cash flow and uncertainty around capital expenditure ​plans. Meanwhile, the head of technology, media and telecom lending at a large European bank told Bloomberg last week that the bank is approaching the limit of what it can lend to the sector given its sizable exposure. But the latest earnings reports indicate the sector still plans to spend heavily, sending their stocks tumbling. A handful of hyperscalers are expected to invest $800 billion this year and more than $1 trillion next year. “Now, another concern is moving to the forefront: Does enough capital even exist to finance everyone’s ambitious AI spending plans?” Wall Street veteran Ed Yardeni asked last week. In a note last week, he pointed to a series of red flags: Broadcom CEO Hock Tan warned capital could become the biggest constraint on AI; BlackRock CEO Larry Fink said that competition for capital was the fiercest he had witnessed in his career; and Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang observed that neocloud demand is “off the charts” but capital is scarce. Trillions more in financing will be in demand as AI businesses fall short on self-sufficiency. Market research firm Gartner estimated data center owners need $8.5 trillion in cumulative revenue by the end of the decade to justify their investments. But the data centers are expected to generate no more than $3.6 trillion of enterprise and consumer AI revenue by then. As a result, tech companies keep going to the well. On Monday, Oracle said it plans to raise $20 billion by issuing stocks and bonds. That’s after Elon Musk said SpaceX closed $40 billion in debt financing, Meta announced plans to raise $95 billion in equity, and Microsoft raised $25 billion in debt. For its part, the sector recognizes that the availability of money is tighter. Meta nodded to the “increasingly challenging environment for raising capital” in a recent filing and suggested it may have to speed up fundraising instead of slowing it down. “The Special Committee considered that, while the Company presently has sufficient capital resources for its current needs, in order to finance its funding needs going forward it would be prudent for the Company to do so before even more companies have absorbed available capital in the market,” it said. To be sure, Wall Street may also turn more selective about which AI companies to fund, allowing well qualified borrowers to obtain financing while shutting out shakier ones. Analysts at Bank of America said last week capital remains ample for investment grade firms and projects backstopped by suppliers, though such “circular” deals have also drawn more scrutiny. Still, BofA sees positive leading indicators for AI chipmakers into next year. But tech companies aren’t the only ones tapping Wall Street for cash. The federal government ran a deficit of nearly $2 trillion last fiscal year with no end in sight to its profligacy, putting more pressure on the bond market. “All those things combined basically means that at the moment we have a shortage of capital, and we have an abundance of good ideas that we need to invest in,” Apollo chief economist Torsten Slok told CNBC last month. Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune, where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. A prank meant to humiliate a Red Baron worker backfired—now strangers have given the dad of 4 more than $760,000
2026-11-02

A prank meant to humiliate a Red Baron worker backfired—now strangers have given the dad of 4 more than $760,000

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
The internet has a well-worn genre: Film a stranger at work, needle him until he snaps, post the meltdown for views. This time, the genre flipped. A video of a prankster faking an Apple Pay theft from a Red Baron Pizza worker as he stocked a grocery store freezer has made the prankster an online pariah. It has also turned the worker, a Michigan father of four identified only as Walt Z., into the beneficiary of a GoFundMe campaign that had raised $761,514 as of publication. Among the donors: Vivek Ramaswamy, the former Republican presidential candidate and GOP nominee for Ohio governor, who gave $38,000. The clip opens with the cameraman asking the worker, who is wearing a Red Baron shirt and loading frozen pizzas into a case, what time it is. When Walt pulls out his phone, the prankster holds up his own device, plays a sound mimicking an Apple Pay transaction, and claims he just took $10, NBC Chicago reported. No money actually changed hands. “Listen dude, I’m not in the mood for this crap right now,” Walt says. “You think this is funny, don’t you?” He tells the prankster he’s going to report him and walks away. The footage then shows him speaking with what appear to be store employees. When the cameraman approaches again, this time thanking him “for the $20,” Walt’s composure gives way. “It’s not a joke to me,” he says. “I don’t have time for this crap in my life. My life is hard enough as it is.” He explains that his kids went to bed without him, that he was supposed to be home an hour earlier, and that he has to be up at 5 a.m. to get to 10 more stores. When the prankster apologizes, Walt rejects it, wearing a smug look. Then comes the line that has been quoted across the internet: “You’re probably making money off this! I can’t even pay my goddamn mortgage! And I got four kids at home! So not funny!” Walt also worries aloud that the confrontation could get him kicked out of the store. Instead, the person behind the camera is the one who disappeared: The Instagram account that posted the clip has since been deleted, and the video now lives on through reposts on X and Reddit. Schwan’s Company, Red Baron’s parent, told the New York Post that the encounter actually took place in August, months before the video went viral, and that the company had “connected directly with our team member to check on his well-being, and we continue to offer our full support.” On Oct. 29, the brand’s X account went a step further. “He’s still here. He’s got a fan club. Including us,” Red Baron posted, alongside a graphic declaring that employee-of-the-month nominations were closed. “You know why.” The GoFundMe, titled “Support a Harassed Red Baron Worker,” was launched Oct. 27 by Luke Oakenshield, who had never met Walt and set out to track him down. The campaign had raised about $5,500 by the next day. It crossed $100,000 a day after that, then $500,000 by Oct. 30, as conservative commentators including Benny Johnson and Collin Rugg amplified it. As donations poured in, supporters learned that Oakenshield, a military veteran, says he is homeless and living out of his car. A friend has since launched a separate fundraiser for him. “I started all of this because I know struggle, and never thought it would explode like it did,” Oakenshield wrote. He has said GoFundMe is overseeing the campaign so the money goes directly to Walt. The displayed total reflects pledges, not confirmed payments. The outpouring says as much about the economy as about prank culture. Walt is a full-time worker, still on the clock near midnight, who says he can’t cover his mortgage. Millions of Americans recognize that story. Some 63% of Americans say they are living paycheck to paycheck, according to a July 2026 CNBC|SurveyMonkey poll. As Fortune has reported, even six-figure earners increasingly say the same. Walt has stayed out of the spotlight. In an update on the GoFundMe page, he thanked donors and said the kindness shown to him and his family “meant more than I can put into words.” He added that he’s been keeping things private to protect his family, and that while being in the spotlight “isn’t something I would normally seek out,” he is considering sharing his story. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. The Pentagon and Britain are bracing for the power to go out. The ultrawealthy got there first, with backup generators, escape homes, and stockpiles
2026-10-30

The Pentagon and Britain are bracing for the power to go out. The ultrawealthy got there first, with backup generators, escape homes, and stockpiles

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
The Pentagon is planning for the possibility that the lights go out and stay out. Under a requirement now in development, each major base in the 48 contiguous states would need to function on its own for at least 90 days if a war cuts off the grid and supply chains. Fox News Digital first reported the news after reviewing internal Pentagon documents. The Pentagon hasn’t said which adversary it has in mind, but in 2024 U.S. cybersecurity, intelligence, and law enforcement agencies warned that Chinese state-backed hackers had broken into American energy, water, and communications networks, possibly to disrupt them during a future conflict.  Under the program known as FORTRESS, the Pentagon’s independence would cover water, fuel, communications, and power. Meanwhile, British households are hearing a scaled-down version of the same warning. The U.K. government’s Be Ready campaign, which was launched Oct. 22, urges families to keep enough water, shelf-stable food, and cash on hand to manage for three days if essential services fail. These two warnings have come around the same time, but the ultrawealthy have been planning for these conditions for years. Many already plan for outages at home with backup generators, escape homes, and stockpiles of supplies, advisors and real estate agents who work with them told Fortune. Take Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg, as an example. In December 2023, Wired reported he was developing a compound of about 1,400 acres on Kauai, Hawaii, with an estimated price tag of roughly $270 million. Plans for the property Wired reviewed showed an underground shelter larger than 5,000 square feet, equipped with its own energy and food supplies. To be sure, Zuckerberg disputes it’s a doomsday bunker: “No, I think that’s just like a little shelter,” he told Bloomberg. “It’s like a basement.” But some of his peers have spoken more freely about preparing for disaster. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman listed his own preparations in a 2016 New Yorker profile: “guns, gold, potassium iodide, antibiotics, batteries, water, gas masks from the Israeli Defense Force, and a big patch of land in Big Sur I can fly to.” In 2017, LinkedIn cofounder Reid Hoffman told the same magazine he believed more than half of Silicon Valley’s billionaires had invested in what those circles call “apocalypse insurance,” typically a retreat somewhere in the U.S. or overseas. “Human motivation is complex, and I think people can say, ‘I now have a safety blanket for this thing that scares me,’” Hoffman told The New Yorker. Still, many of the ultrawealthy have been more on the down low about preparing for a doomsday scenario. But the people who staff their homes, advise them on risk, and sell them real estate say the planning has picked up in recent years. Brian Daniel founded The Celebrity Personal Assistant Network in 2007 to place assistants and household staff with wealthy families. He told Fortune his clients have grown more focused on safety, and the shift has sped up over the past five years. “At the very minimum, my clients are installing back-up power systems and stocking up on supplies that would last months,” he said. Others are spreading their bets across multiple properties—at home and abroad—and often away from major cities, he said. Some are “even purchasing luxury underground bunkers that cost millions.” Clients worth $100 million or more keep security staff on the payroll full time, he added. “They want peace of mind, and they will spend what it takes to get it,” Daniel said. That emphasis on protection shows up in other wealthy enclaves, too. In Palm Beach, Fla., the stretch of South Ocean Boulevard that passes Mar-a-Lago, was shut down earlier this year over security concerns tied to the Iran war. It cut through a strip of oceanfront mansions known as Billionaires’ Beach. But local agents said buyers treated the closure as a selling point, not a nuisance. “What used to be a temporary inconvenience has now become part of the infrastructure of doing business here,” Palm Beach–based luxury agent Jessica Julian told Fortune in April. Philip Farina, chief advisor at Risky Business, a firm that counsels companies and high-net-worth families on getting ready for disasters and emergencies, told Fortune the latest wave of government preparedness campaigns has brought him more client inquiries. During the pandemic, he said, some clients left places with tighter COVID rules for second homes and “escape homes” in more remote areas. “Many clients are looking for the complete package: real estate coupled with self-sufficient equipment, food and beverage stockpiles, Go-Bags, weapons training and communication plans for all family members,” he said. For buyers in Miami, one of the main worries is the power grid, said Samuel Strauch, principal at Metrik Real Estate. Because the city is surrounded by water and exposed to hurricanes, he said, the topic comes up almost as soon as a wealthy buyer reaches out. Clients also ask about how high a property sits and whether it has a generator or room to install one, he said. “Impact glass and windproof construction are not add-ons here. They are expected features,” he added. “Serious, sophisticated buyers know exactly what happens in an outage. They want their fallback plans perfectly in place from day one.” Sydney Lake is an associate editor at Fortune, where she writes and edits news for the publication's global news desk. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Fortune | FORTUNE
Welcome to the school where Gen Z learns philanthropy as billionaire wealth explodes—with a 100% job placement rate
2026-10-31

Welcome to the school where Gen Z learns philanthropy as billionaire wealth explodes—with a 100% job placement rate

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
When Ella Traina, a sophomore at Indiana University, thinks about her future, she isn’t focused on becoming a CEO or making a fortune. She wants to figure out how to make the world a little better. That might sound like a lofty ambition for a 19-year-old, but Traina is taking a decidedly practical approach: She’s studying philanthropy at Indiana University’s Lilly Family School of Philanthropy, the first school of its kind in the world. She’s part of a growing cohort of students doing the same. Over the past decade, enrollment at the school has grown more than 300% from about 50 students to roughly 230, as students explore careers in nonprofits, foundations, corporations, and government. The school says it has had a 100% job placement rate for the past three years—a higher rate than the university’s business school students. The growth comes as more young people look for careers that offer something beyond a paycheck and a chance to make a tangible difference. And in a world where wealth is increasingly concentrated among the richest Americans, philanthropy is becoming an important part of the conversation about how money and resources can be put to work for the public good. Meanwhile, the $124 trillion Great Wealth Transfer—the passing down of baby boomer fortunes to younger generations—begs the question of how philanthropy will look in the future. Will millennials and Gen Z carry on the traditions of their family fortunes, or will they chart their own path? These are the types of questions a philanthropy education can explore, as well as how the wealthy are filling the void left by the federal government, which has pulled back funding for nonprofits and higher education institutions. Indiana University’s roots in philanthropy education go back decades. The university formally organized its fundraising teaching and research into an academic center in 1987. A quarter-century later, in 2012, it was established into an official philanthropy-focused school, making it the first place in the world dedicated exclusively to the study and teaching of the subject. Today, it is one of the few places where students can earn a bachelor’s, master’s, or Ph.D. specifically focused on philanthropy. The school’s development has been supported by the Lilly Endowment—established in 1937 by the family of pharmaceutical executive Eli Lilly—since 1988. It views philanthropy and the nonprofit sector as important complements to government and business in promoting the public good. “We hope that as these students prepare for careers in philanthropy, they are also discovering what it means for them to live lives of meaning and purpose and how they can help others do so as well,” an endowment spokesperson told Fortune. That philosophy carries into the classroom. Traina’s classes range from the history of philanthropy to storytelling for social change. In one course, she and six classmates decided how to distribute a real $10,000 grant. “When I wake up every day and I want to help achieve impact… I want to keep moving that needle to where there are less people suffering and without basic human needs,” Traina said. After graduation, she hopes to work in impact evaluation, helping nonprofits measure whether their programs are making a difference. Traina’s ambitions reflect a broader sentiment among Gen Z. A survey released this summer by the Walton Family Foundation, established by Walmart founders Sam Walton and his wife Helen, found that 79% of Gen Z hope to find a future job primarily focused on helping others or making a positive difference in people’s lives. That desire is not only shaping what young people study but also where they work—and where they don’t. Forty-four percent of Gen Z said they had left a job they felt lacked purpose, while around 40% had rejected a potential employer for similar reasons, according to a 2025 Deloitte survey. And there are plenty of opportunities to pursue that purpose. Nonprofits employed about 12.8 million workers in 2022, accounting for nearly 10% of all private-sector jobs, according to an analysis by the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics. Still, Amir Pasic, dean of IU’s Lilly Family School of Philanthropy, said the sector faces a persistent misbelief: that meaningful work comes at the expense of a viable career. “There’s still this misconception that if you go into philanthropy or the nonprofit sector that you are going to be offering to work for free, basically… ‘Aren’t you sweet to have such a warm heart?’” Pasic said. The reality is more complicated. Nearly two-thirds of nonprofit workers earn more than $75,000 a year—about 15% more than the average U.S. salary—and philanthropy-related roles at some Fortune 500 companies can reach well into six-figures. Walmart, for instance, has advertised a grant manager role in Arkansas paying up to $180,000, while a Microsoft job has offered compensation of up to $275,000 for a recent community affairs role. For Traina, the potential payoff of her studies goes beyond a paycheck: “If you regret the degree, the worst you’re gonna have is you’re gonna know how to help people and how to impact society and create change.” Patrick Lindenmeyer took a less traditional route into the field. He spent nearly 17 years in financial services helping clients grow and structure their wealth. Many of them wanted to be generous with their money, he told Fortune. Then the pandemic hit. It pushed him to rethink what he wanted from the second half of his career, and the part of his job he’d always enjoyed most was helping clients figure out how to direct their money toward causes they cared about. “The financial services career really gave me the technical ability [of asking] how, like how do we best structure assets? How do we best leverage impact?” Lindenmeyer said. “But the school of philanthropy really helped me with the why behind why individuals want to make this impact, and what does that look like.” He enrolled in the school’s online master’s program in philanthropic studies, taking one course per semester while working full time. Midway through the program, he landed what he called his dream job: gift planning officer at the University of Notre Dame, where he helps families build giving into their estate plans through tools like donor-advised funds and charitable remainder trusts. Several of his Notre Dame colleagues have also completed the IU degree or are working on it, he said. Lindenmeyer is now also pursuing a master’s in nonprofit administration at Notre Dame, which he expects to finish in 2028. Plenty of universities teach the business side of running a nonprofit, he said, but IU’s program focuses on what drives donors to give. Overall, he encourages others at any point in their career to explore studying philanthropy. “People that are looking for true, meaningful work, work that creates an impact and something that they can truly see, I think that this is something worth exploring,” he said. “That certainly wasn’t on my radar … 25 years ago when I entered college as an undergrad.” From a wealth and giving standpoint, philanthropy could be on the cusp of a major expansion. In 2025, billionaire wealth surged more than 16%—three times faster than its average growth over the previous five years—and reached a record $18.3 trillion, according to Oxfam. Charitable giving also reached a record $617.2 billion in 2025, but rose just 3% after adjusting for inflation, according to Giving USA. The gap between the wealth billionaires have grown and what they’re giving has fueled criticism that the ultrawealthy are giving too slowly. MacKenzie Scott remains a notable exception: She has given away more than $26 billion, and alone accounted for one-third of America’s megagifts last year. Since then, the fortunes of many of the world’s wealthiest people have continued to grow, fueled in part by soaring tech stocks and optimism around AI. Take Jensen Huang, for example. The billionaire Nvidia CEO donated $75 million to Vanderbilt University. For Pasic, that presents an opportunity for students at Indiana University to help shape how such wealth is put to work, particularly as younger heirs push their families to give away more money, sooner. “The anticipated large windfalls that will come through the AI industry,” Pasic said. “There’s this problem of money on the sidelines, to which more is going to be added pretty soon.” And while the $124 trillion Great Wealth Transfer has the power to change philanthropy as we know it, that handoff isn’t completely solidified. Wealthy Americans are growing less confident their heirs will carry on their family’s charitable giving, according to a 2026 Bank of America Private Bank study. But perhaps that’s where philanthropic education will play a larger role. Lindenmeyer sees firsthand how aging baby boomers are deciding where their wealth goes next. “We had always talked about the great wealth transfer,” he said. “First it was … the retirement boom for the baby boomers and what that was going to look like, and then now as they’re aging and passing away, what does this transfer of wealth look like?” Preston Fore is a reporter on Fortune's Success team. Sydney Lake is an associate editor at Fortune, where she writes and edits news for the publication's global news desk. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Fortune | FORTUNE
As Sergey Brin, Larry Page and the ultra-rich flee California, one 34-year-old tech billionaire is ‘not running’—he ‘wants to fight’ for the state
2026-11-02

As Sergey Brin, Larry Page and the ultra-rich flee California, one 34-year-old tech billionaire is ‘not running’—he ‘wants to fight’ for the state

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
California’s proposed billionaire tax has sent some ultra-rich residents flocking to other states—but tech entrepreneur Palmer Luckey is opting to stay put. While the 34-year-old cofounder of Oculus VR and Anduril opposes the bill, he still believes it’s worth it to stay. “I think that this tax act, if you can call it a tax, is going to cause a lot of problems,” Luckey said during a press conference last week, reported by TechCrunch. “It’s causing a lot of people to run who I don’t want to be running. It’s going to cause companies that I work with to leave the state, which is a problem for me.” “As of right now, though, I’m not running. I am staying,” he continued. “I think California is something special. I want to fight for California.” The entrepreneur raised in Long Beach has nurtured all of his biggest career wins in the Golden State. He founded his first company Oculus in Irvine, California back in 2012, joining the billionaire club just two years later when Facebook bought the virtual reality business in a deal estimated to be worth $2 billion. And one of his newer ventures, defense tech firm Anduril headquartered in Costa Mesa, California, was valued at $90 billion earlier this month following an employee tender offer. Now, Luckey has a net worth of around $5.9 billion, according to Forbes—but he isn’t fleeing his home state to protect his fortune. Luckey said that he doesn’t mind the idea of paying more in taxes, but takes issue with the bill’s sponsor, a union under United Healthcare Workers, wanting to leverage the tax money “for their uses,” and questions how the bill would assess what is owed. Prop 40’s sponsors have stated that the money will be used to improve California’s “health care, education, and food assistance.” He is also concerned about illiquid and unsold assets being grouped into his fortune, likening it to a “seizure of future wealth.” The tax hike, Proposition 40, has caused quite a stir; while many successful peers like Google cofounder Sergey Brin and former CEO Eric Schmidt have flocked to tax-friendly states, others like Luckey still believe that staying in California is worth the hit. “This has been a point of friction between me and some of my other billionaire friends,” the millennial founder said. “They’ve said things like ‘Palmer, you have to have solidarity for us. You can’t enable these really bad policies. If you stay and if you don’t leave, it’s going to show them that there’s no consequences.’ I empathize with that argument, but it’s not one that I’m going to participate in right now because I still believe that we can fix things.” Fortune reached out to Anduril for comment. Notable business figures in California have been waging war over Proposition 40: a one-time 5% tax on residents with assets over $1 billion. The coastal state hopes this will increase temporary revenue by tens of billions spread over several years to fund health care and education—and California is home to the highest number of billionaires in the U.S. The fate of Proposition 40 will be decided by voters tomorrow during America’s midterm elections. And some of California’s ultrarich have spent sizable fortunes to strike it down. Tech billionaires have raised more than $340 million and spent more than $1 million daily in a campaign to defeat Prop 40, according to The Guardian; Palantir cofounder Peter Thiel, ex Google CEO Eric Schmidt, DoorDash leader Tony Xu, Stripe chief exec Patrick Collison all put money towards the opposition campaign. Before Prop 40’s January 1 2026 residency cutoff, a slew of billionaires also fled the state. Google cofounders Larry Page and Brin moved their business entities to Florida and Nevada, and Travis Kalanick, the former CEO of Uber, also changed addresses to Austin. Others like Palantir’s Peter Thiel and Meta’s Mark Zuckerberg have already relocated from California to more tax-friendly areas. While Prop 40 has emboldened some to leave, there’s a vocal cohort of billionaires like Luckey choosing to stay. Jensen Huang, the world’s ninth-richest person with an eye-watering $180 billion net worth, recently said that “the more you make, the more taxes you pay. And I’m okay with that.” The leader of the world’s largest chip company reasoned that he’s the “American Dream,” and success wouldn’t have been possible without the opportunities the U.S. afforded him. He was able to pursue a high-level education, create a company, pursue his dreams, and meet the people that “made Nvidia possible.” “The fact that I can afford to pay $8 billion in taxes over five years is a privilege,” Huang told CBS Sunday Morning last month. “I’m not afraid of paying taxes. I’m just afraid of being poor.” Emma Burleigh is a reporter at Fortune, covering success, careers, entrepreneurship, and personal finance. Before joining the Success desk, she co-authored Fortune’s CHRO Daily newsletter, extensively covering the workplace and the future of jobs. Emma has also written for publications including the Observer and The China Project, publishing long-form stories on culture, entertainment, and geopolitics. She has a joint-master’s degree from New York University in Global Journalism and East Asian Studies. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Billionaire Sergey Brin is building political infrastructure that will last beyond his $102 million fight against Prop 40
2026-11-02

Billionaire Sergey Brin is building political infrastructure that will last beyond his $102 million fight against Prop 40

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Google cofounder Sergey Brin spent much of his career on the sidelines of electoral politics. But a proposed California wealth tax has turned the billionaire into one of the state’s most powerful political financiers, prompting him to build an organization that can continue shaping state policy long after voters decide on the measure that initially mobilized him. Brin has contributed $102 million to Building a Better California, a political organization that has become the central vehicle for Silicon Valley billionaires seeking to influence California’s political direction. The group has raised hundreds of millions of dollars from wealthy tech executives, venture capitalists, and business leaders—channeling money into ballot measures that extend beyond the immediate fight against Proposition 40. Through Oct. 20, Building a Better California has spent more than $304 million supporting efforts to defeat Proposition 40 and advance Propositions 41 and 42—direct counters to the wealth tax. Of that amount, approximately $118.5 million went toward opposing Proposition 40, while the other $186 million supported Propositions 41 and 42, according to Capital & Main. Proposition 41 would bar the state from exempting new taxes from its spending limit and require state audits of programs funded by new special taxes, making it harder for the state to use funds raised by the billionaire tax. Proposition 42 would outlaw retroactive taxation, directly countering Proposition 40. The strategy gives the ultrawealthy opponents multiple opportunities to defeat the measure: If either one gets more “yes” votes than Proposition 40, the Legislative Analyst’s Office says, the wealth tax could be stopped even if a majority of voters approve it. And according to an analysis by Politico, more than 70 current and former California billionaires had directed a record $612 million into state elections during the 2026 cycle. Nearly half of that money went toward groups working to defeat Proposition 40, and at least 30 billionaires had contributed more than $293 million specifically to the wealth tax fight, according to the analysis. Building a Better California also endorsed Propositions 1, 3, 37, 41, and 42, according to its policy platform. It also put $10 million behind Proposition 45, a separate measure addressing California’s environmental review process, and $6 million behind Proposition 37, which would create a $25 billion homebuyer loan fund, according to Capital & Main. The immediate catalyst for the ultrawealthy movement was Proposition 40, a proposed one-time tax on the wealth of California billionaires that will appear on the Nov. 3 ballot. The measure would impose a 5% tax on the assets of individuals and certain trusts worth more than $1 billion who were California residents on Jan. 1, 2026. The proposal is designed to raise money to offset federal health care funding cuts, with 90% of the proceeds allocated to health care and the remaining 10% directed toward education and food assistance. According to the Legislative Analyst’s Office, the state probably would collect tens of billions of dollars from the tax, spread across several years. The proposal was sponsored by the Service Employees International Union-United Healthcare Workers West, which argued that California needs additional funding to protect its health care system as federal cuts threaten coverage for residents. The ballot measure is also backed by progressive leaders Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-Vt.) and Rep. Ro Khanna (D-Calif.). For Brin, whose fortune is estimated at $231 billion by Bloomberg, the potential financial exposure is substantial—and he has been particularly vocal about his opposition. In an interview with the New York Times in April, he compared the proposal to the socialism he experienced growing up in the Soviet Union—saying he did not want California to end up in the same place. He also relocated to Nevada in December, ahead of the Jan. 1 cutoff date that determines which billionaires would be subject to the proposed tax. But rather than leave California’s political fight behind, Brin organized a coordinated campaign to prevent the measure from becoming law. Sergey Brin did not immediately respond to a request for comment from Fortune. In January, Brin established Building a Better California—contributing an initial $20 million. By August, his contributions had reached $102 million, according to campaign finance filings. The organization has since become a principal coordinating point for wealthy tech executives. Other major contributors include Kleiner Perkins chairman John Doerr, former Google CEO Eric Schmidt, Stripe CEO Patrick Collison, and former Sequoia Capital partner Michael Moritz. Even Mark Cuban, a self-identified “libertarian at heart” and a billionaire closely affiliated with Democrats in recent years—such as being a high-profile surrogate for Kamala Harris in the 2024 presidential race—has found himself on the side of his fellow wealthy compatriots. Cuban got into a public feud with Khanna on X over the proposal, eventually calling the politician “insane.” “This is the biggest f*** you in the history of entrepreneurship,” Cuban wrote on X. “Ever.” But not everyone shares Brin’s broad political goal. His political organization has seen pushback from at least one major donor over how broadly to expand its political agenda. Former Google CEO Eric Schmidt was one of the ultrawealthy sponsors that had issues with Brin’s broader focus on politics. The New York Post initially reported in August that Schmidt had grown frustrated with the anti-wealth-tax coalition led by Brin after the group failed to prevent California Democrats from endorsing the measure. But a close advisor to Schmidt told Fortune in August the former Google CEO was not “irate” about the endorsement outcome and that Schmidt’s “only current involvement in politics is his contributions to defeating the billionaires tax”—meaning Schmidt and Brin diverged over the group’s money going to other measures, such as Proposition 45. Joshua Hong is a News Fellow at Fortune covering data, AI, cybersecurity, energy, and retail. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved.
