Silicon Republic
The growing threat of wildfires across Europe and Ireland
2026-11-02

The growing threat of wildfires across Europe and Ireland

Silicon Republic | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Smoke from the Trévillach wildfire rises behind Mediterranean hills and cypress trees near Perpignan during a major wildfire in southern France in July 2026. Image: © Bruno/Stock.adobe.com Reflecting on the wildfires that scourged Ireland and Europe this past summer, Eoin Murphy discusses the changing weather patterns and how nature itself may offer protection in the future. For many, last summer will be remembered for the news footage of wildfires spreading across Europe. Devastating scenes of fires approaching populated areas as firefighters did everything in their power to slow the spread of the blazing infernos in the hope that the wind would change direction or a small amount of rain would fall. Two of the countries which were most severely hit by the fires this summer were France and Spain. France experienced its worst year for wildfires in the past 15 years and in Spain the area burned was double the 15-year average. These countries together accounted for over 50pc of the area burned across the EU. In Ireland, the summer of 2026 was the hottest on record. The heat and dry weather led to a partial drought in Oak Park, Co. Carlow lasting for 66 days, breaking the previous record of 62 days stretching back to 1983. Consequently, wildfires broke out in locations across Ireland at levels never seen before. Slievenamon Mountain viewed from the Copernicus Sentinel 2 satellite in August 2025 compared to August 2026. The consequences of extreme drought and wildfire clearly visible on the landscape. Image: Copernicus Sentinel data (2026) However, when the data as a whole is examined, there was in fact much less area burned across the European Union in 2026 when compared to the previous summer. Why then does it feel worse? The answer may lie in the fact that wildfires are now threatening densely populated areas previously considered safe, as well as tourist locations. Living in Co Clare, my house wasn’t near any of the locations around Ireland seen to be at risk. But in early August, I travelled to the south-west of France on a family holiday. I knew that we were far enough north that the fires which had devastated the Bordeaux region were not a risk. Then just before arriving at our campsite, I saw the remains of a scorched field and hedgerow. I subsequently learned that friends of ours who arrived two days earlier had in fact witnessed the fire. Apart from the initial fear which came with seeing the burning field, they like me had felt the immediate instinct to draw their children’s attention away from the fire. Perhaps this is a natural reaction to something frightening and to a certain degree beyond our control. But if extended drought periods and the accompanying risk of fire are to become a more regular occurrence, will that begin to change how we view the consequences of climate change? Climate scientist and meteorologist Cathal Nolan also saw first-hand the consequences of last summer’s drought across Europe. In August, Nolan travelled from his home in Offaly to France by car and boat, continuing on to the Dolomites before returning home via Lake Garda and Chamonix. The drought across Europe was evident all along the journey. Nolan recalls seeing “the trees in their full autumn shade resembled something more like mid-October”, as if the trees had been “going into shut down mode to preserve themselves”. Cathal Nolan, climate scientist and founder of Ireland’s Weather Channel. Image: Cathal Nolan Unfortunately, he doesn’t believe that the past summer was an outlier, moreover it may be a sign of what is to come. In relation to the climate data, Nolan says “the general trend and the move at the moment is for parts of the midlands, east and south of the country to become dryer during the summer months and as a consequence see an increase in drought conditions, an increase in wildfires and higher temperatures”. One of the most significant changes in the past two decades has been the number of satellites in the sky monitoring the different aspects of both real-time weather data and the longer-term impacts on climate. Since the launch of Sentinel-1A in 2014, through the Copernicus Programme, the EU has been working on placing more than 20 satellites in space before the end of the decade. Our ever-developing satellite system will be crucial in combatting the risk factors driving wildfires. According to Nolan, “now we can track in real time the development of severe droughts, we can see the areas which are going to be the most impacted”. With the data from the vast number of satellites now feeding data in real-time into AI systems, weather forecasting has been transformed in the space of a few years. Commenting on the role of AI in weather prediction systems, Nolan says “it looks and assesses back in time through every available synoptic chart that’s in existence and through that then transgresses forward and shows us what’s likely to happen over the course of the next seven to 10 days”. AI has brought about unprecedented improvements in weather prediction models. In June of this year, Prof Andrew Parnell, Met Éireann professor of data science for climate and weather, told an Oireachtas committee that AI “beats the latest weather forecasting systems by 10 to 15pc”. There are many opinions and beliefs as to why Ireland’s woodlands and countryside have become so vulnerable to the threat of wildfires. For example, improper land management, monoculture forests and reckless individual behaviour. But regardless of where you stand on this argument, it seems clear that climate change is exposing weaknesses within our systems. In the face of these challenges, some have been turning to nature-based solutions. One such group, Hometree, works to establish and conserve native woodlands across Ireland. Their work may prove to be a powerful asset, as around the world native woodlands have been shown to have an increased capacity to resist fire. Ray Ó Foghlú, head of partnerships and development at Hometree says “generally these trees are wetter and the roots are deeper” and “they can get scorched but they won’t burn themselves”. Ray Ó Foghlú of Hometree pictured near his home in Miltown Malbay, Co Clare. Image: Philip Doyle A case in point is the wildfire which spread through Killarney National Park last summer. The blaze, which started due to the illegal use of a barbeque, resulted in the burning of over 20 hectares of the parks vegetation. As tragic as this loss was, it could have been worse. Commenting on the presence of the more mature, healthy oak trees, Ó Foghlú says “the fire hit the edge of the trees and stopped”. As promising as it was to see the healthy oak trees providing a level of fire resilience within the landscape, Ó Foghlú acknowledged that “there will be a stage on the journey there that’s actually quite fire prone”. This is due to the preparatory steps taken when preparing land for new trees. Ó Foghlú tells me that the “first rule for growing trees is excluding herbivores in the early stages” and as a result “you are building up a substantial load of flammable material”. Conscious of the risks, Hometree is actively integrating steps into its plans to mitigate the likelihood that it would impact on others. One such innovative approach has been used in the past to maintain firebreaks on Howth Head. By introducing goats wearing the Nofence collar system, a herd can be directed to selected strips of land in such a way as to create a buffer zone between the young trees which may pose a risk to the surrounding farmland or commercial forestry. The commitment and belief shown by groups such as Hometree that nature-based solutions like restoring native woodlands can play in reducing the risks of climate change is clear for all to see. Nevertheless, Ó Foghlú acknowledges that “anyone who works close to the land feels a level of concern and anxiety about what happens next”, but perhaps “fear might lead to action”. If we are to see more urgent action in creating woodlands and countryside which are resilient to the risk of extreme drought and wildfires, it will require a greater focus and investment from government departments. For this to become a reality, it is likely that a greater proportion of society will need to recognise the importance of this investment. In the 2024 report, Climate Change in the Irish Mind Wave 2 published by the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA), 69pc of people in Ireland reported being “not very worried” or “not at all worried” about wildfire causing harm to their local area. However, with the EPA due to publish Wave 3 of this study in early 2027 based on opinions and beliefs collected between mid-July and mid-August 2026, the results may prove to be different. Could we see a change in attitude towards the risks posed by wildfires in Ireland? If so, would this lead to the action many believe is now essential? Time will tell. Eoin Murphy is a biochemist, educator and award-winning science communicator. He holds a degree in biotechnology as well as master’s degrees in both biochemistry and science and health communication. Murphy has delivered a number of science communication workshops, designed programmes for secondary schools, guest lectured on podcast production as well as featuring regularly on Newstalk’s Science show Futureproof. He currently holds the role of social media manager for The Mary Mulvihill Association and divides his time between education and working on different science communication projects. How might women be reintroduced into the science community they helped create?
2026-11-02

How might women be reintroduced into the science community they helped create?

Silicon Republic | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Stephanie Buttigieg and Gregory Cooke of the University of Cambridge and Skyla White of the University of Bern discuss how the women who changed the scientific landscape should be recognised for their work. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) Cast your mind back to your secondary school science classroom: the fiery glow of Bunsen burners, scratched safety goggles that never quite fit, and a giant periodic table pinned to the wall. When you learned about the breakthroughs that shaped our world and the people who made them, whose names were attached? From Newton’s laws to Darwin’s theory of natural selection, the ones you remember are almost certainly male. That experience was not just down to individual teachers. It was and still is written into official GCSE and A-level course specifications. Because these early stages form the foundation of our science education, their omissions stick with us for life. Half of the British population can’t name one woman scientist. Leaving women out of national specifications sends an implicit message about who science is really for. In our new study, led by researchers at the University of Cambridge alongside IncludeHer, a collaborative project working to improve the representation of women in school science education, we analysed over 40 science specifications: the knowledge required to pass exams. We examined the specifications for six main exam boards for Key Stage Four (GCSEs) and Key Stage Five (A-levels), and for Scotland’s National Fives and Highers. Our work builds on an earlier Australian study led by astrophysicist Dr Kat Ross, which uncovered a striking lack of female scientists in high school curricula. We set out to investigate whether UK specifications have similar omissions. Across 224 unique scientists referenced in UK specifications, discoveries are taught almost exclusively through concepts named after men, with only three mentioning women in total. Inge Lehmann, who discovered that the Earth has a solid inner core surrounded by a liquid outer core, is cited in A-level geology via the ‘Lehmann discontinuity’. Marie Tharp, who mapped the ocean floor and identified the Mid-Atlantic Ridge rift valley, is also mentioned in A-level geology. She provided vital evidence for continental drift, despite being prohibited from boarding research vessels for years and having her ideas dismissed as ‘girl talk‘. Finally, Rosalind Franklin is included in two biology specifications for her x-ray crystallography work revealing the double-helix structure of DNA. Her essential data was used without attribution during her lifetime. At GCSE level, when science is compulsory for all students, Franklin is the only woman mentioned. At A-level, Lehmann and Tharp appear solely in geology, a subject taken by less than 2pc of science students. For centuries, social, economic, and institutional barriers limited women’s formal participation in science. Despite this, women made numerous key breakthroughs, and these are already taught to UK 14 to 18-year-olds. But the names of the women behind them did not make it onto the page. Diversity within scientific fields drives creativity and innovation, benefiting all of society. Yet women remain underrepresented. They make up only around one third of the global scientific community, with similar figures seen within the UK workforce. This disparity starts early on in schools: although science uptake is balanced at Key Stage Four, just two years later at Key Stage Five, physics, mathematics and further mathematics only see participation from girls at 24.1pc, 37.3pc and 26.9pc respectively in 2025. This imbalance continues into university, where women make up roughly 25pc of physics undergraduate students in the UK. Introducing relatable female role models into the classroom directly boosts girls’ confidence and fosters positive attitudes towards scientific careers. But because specifications dictate mandatory exam content, teachers currently have to go out of their way to add female figures on top of busy workloads. By making small, targeted changes directly to national specifications, we can make inclusion standard practice across all schools. We propose inserting one female scientist per subject into topics that are already compulsory, but where the woman’s name is missing. This means introducing Marie Skłodowska-Curie, who coined the term radioactivity and discovered polonium and radium, when teaching atomic structure and radioactivity, and Lise Meitner, who provided the first theoretical explanation of nuclear fission, when discussing this concept. GCSE astronomy should include Jocelyn Bell Burnell, who discovered pulsars: rotating neutron stars used as cosmic clocks. Biology teaching can include Dorothy Hodgkin on protein structure, the only British female Nobel laureate in science, and Tu Youyou, a Nobel laureate whose work led to the development of an antimalarial drug. In chemistry, schools can teach about Stephanie Kwolek, the inventor of Kevlar, when covering polymers. For physics, they can include Annie Jump Cannon when describing the classification of stars. And in environmental science, classes can discuss the pivotal work of Susan Solomon for understanding Antarctic ozone depletion. By making these simple additions to science specifications, we can change the message we send to the next generation. Whoever they are, every student sitting in a classroom deserves to know that the story of science belongs to them too. By Stephanie Buttigieg, Gregory Cooke and Skyla White Stephanie Buttigieg is a PhD candidate in astronomy at the University of Cambridge. She researches supermassive black holes, how they formed, evolved and merged with each other and what we can learn about them from observations with light and gravitational waves. She is also passionate about improving women’s representation in science and is a big advocate for girls being exposed to relatable role models. Gregory Cooke currently works on theoretical explorations of exoplanet climates and astrobiology as a postdoctoral researcher at the Institute of Astronomy at the University of Cambridge. He has worked on analysing the representation of women in UK science specifications for 14 to 18 year olds. Skyla White is a research fellow at the Centre for Space and Habitability at the University of Bern, where she is specialising in planetary chemistry. She holds a PhD in physics from the University of Cambridge, a first class degree in geochemistry from the University of St Andrews and a first class honours degree in biochemistry from the University of Warwick. Her interests lie in the origin of life on Earth and beyond. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. Related: equality, The Conversation, higher education, Opinion, science, education, Guest Column

13 SuperValu stores hit in suspected cyberattack
2026-11-02

13 SuperValu stores hit in suspected cyberattack

Silicon Republic | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Supermarkets across Europe have been affected in another wave of attacks on fridges and freezers. 13 SuperValu stores across Ireland have been hit by a suspected cyberattack on their refrigerators. In a statement to news publications, the grocery chain’s owner Musgrave said that the incident was limited to a specific model of refrigerator. “As a precaution, chilled and frozen products in the affected stores have been removed from sale, and all other products remain available as normal,” Musgrave said in a statement. “Customer safety is our first priority, and customers can be confident that products on sale in our stores have been stored at safe temperatures.” It is understood that affected locations include SuperValu stores in Waterford, Lucan, Blanchardstown and Bray. SiliconRepublic.com has reached out to Musgrave for further comments. The incident comes after hundreds of supermarkets in France fell victim to a cyberattack early last month after hackers turned up temperatures of remotely controlled refrigerators as high as 50 degrees Celsius. The large-scale hack caused fires across a number of stores. Integrity360 CTO Richard Ford told SiliconRepublic.com that while it’s too early to establish whether the previous incident in France is related to the SuperValu disruption, “they demonstrate how cyberattacks can have immediate consequences beyond traditional IT systems”. “For retailers, refrigeration equipment is essential to daily operations, yet connected systems can introduce risks that are easily overlooked. Equipment may be remotely managed by third-party suppliers, run outdated software or depend on access arrangements that are not subject to the same scrutiny as corporate IT systems,” he said. “The priority for retailers should be understanding which operational systems are connected, who can access them and whether appropriate security updates and controls are in place. They also need to test how they would respond if critical equipment became unavailable. “The disruption at SuperValu is another reminder that cyber resilience is not simply about protecting data. It is about keeping stores operational and protecting customers when essential systems are affected.” Meanwhile, supermarkets in Sweden, Belgium, Luxembourg and more were also hit in a suspected attack, after chains said today (2 November) that the temperatures of their refrigerators and freezers were turned up overnight. Sweeden’s Coop chain and retail franchise ICA, Dutch retailer Ahold Delhaize and “dozens” of locations overall have been reportedly affected in the incident. The reported losses run to hundreds of thousands of euros in some cases. The UK’s National Audit Office named cyberattacks as one of the major threats facing the country’s food supply chain earlier this year. Experts, meanwhile, have warned that attackers are increasingly targeting remote management systems, especially those that lie between IT and connected physical infrastructure. Sophisticated cyberattacks are threatening key global infrastructure as security professionals flock to secure their systems. Last week, North Korean hackers pulled off what is widely thought to be the biggest bank heist in history, after making off with $6.3bn in stolen cryptocurrency assets from Binance. Updated, 4.47pm, 2 November 2026: This article was amended to include a comment from Integrity360 CTO Richard Ford. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. The software engineer who found her place in financial services
2026-11-02

The software engineer who found her place in financial services

Silicon Republic | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Fidelity Investments’ Agata Duda explores how a graduate programme can be an ideal segue into a long-term career. Agata Duda, a full-stack engineer at Fidelity Investments, first pursued her studies at Technological University of the Shannon (TUS), in Athlone, where she undertook a level-seven bachelor’s degree in software design specialising in mobile applications and connected devices. She soon progressed to a level-eight qualification in the same area and spent an entire year working and gaining experience within the industry, before returning to complete a master’s degree in software engineering. Duda told SiliconRepublic.com, “I love what I do. While I was in college, it never crossed my mind that I could build a career in software engineering within a financial services company like Fidelity Investments. “At the time, I associated software engineering mostly with technology companies, but I’ve since discovered that financial services is an incredibly innovative space, with opportunities to work on large-scale systems, solve complex problems and create solutions that have a real impact on customers every day.” As a graduate, I was looking for a company where I could build a long-term career. I was also drawn to the opportunity to work on technology that has a real-world impact, helping people work towards their financial goals. Beyond the work itself, I was impressed by Fidelity’s commitment to giving back to local communities through initiatives such as its laptop donation programmes and Fidelity Cares Month, where associates volunteer for the community, organisations and local schools. When I applied for the programme, it was clear that it was designed to provide a well-rounded introduction to both the company and the role, which immediately put me at ease. Starting your first job can be overwhelming. You’re trying to get your foot in the door while also wondering how much of what you learned in college you’ll actually remember and use. What stood out about the Fidelity Leap programme was how structured and supportive it was. It guided us through everything we needed to know, from technical concepts and tools to the ways of working within the organisation. In many ways, it felt like being back in a classroom, but with a more professional atmosphere and training tailored specifically to the technologies, processes and skills we would be using every day at Fidelity. That made the transition from university to the workplace much smoother and gave me confidence that I was set up for success from day one. Initially, my responsibilities centred around taking ownership of my onboarding and learning. This included setting up my workspace, ensuring I had access to the necessary tools, technologies and permissions, and becoming familiar with the company’s processes and ways of working. As the programme progressed, I was responsible for managing my schedule, attending training sessions, completing learning objectives and actively participating in classroom-based exercises. We were also assigned group projects, which required us to organise regular meetings, divide tasks, collaborate effectively and keep the project on track – similar to responsibilities in college but with added professionalism in a different environment. Alongside the technical responsibilities, I learned the day-to-day aspects of working in a professional environment, such as managing calendars, booking meeting rooms and submitting timesheets. These may seem extremely simple now but as a new graduate entering a new environment everything helps you gain confidence. I’ve been part of my team for just under two years now and many of the core responsibilities I was introduced to during the Fidelity Leap programme are still a significant part of my role today. One of the key lessons from the programme was taking ownership of my own learning and that’s something that continues to be important. Technology is constantly evolving, so continuous learning is essential to staying current and developing as a software engineer. Day-to-day, I work on stories and tasks within each sprint, managing my workload and working towards delivery deadlines. My responsibilities include designing and implementing solutions, reviewing pull requests, collaborating with teammates on technical approaches and helping to make decisions on how best to deliver new features and enhancements, which, of course, requires plenty of problem solving and collaboration. I’m involved throughout the software development lifecycle, from development and testing on my local machine through to deploying and validating changes in higher environments. The project experience I gained during the Fidelity Leap programme provided a solid foundation for working within a delivery team and contributing effectively from an early stage. Beyond my core engineering responsibilities, I also enjoy getting involved in activities outside of my immediate role. I’m a member of the Amplify Community of Practice – one of the many Fidelity Ireland communities of practice focused on supporting and developing associates – and I regularly participate in volunteering initiatives such as career fairs and Fidelity Cares Month. I’ve also had the opportunity to mentor new Leap associates and interns, which has been particularly rewarding as it allows me to support others as they begin the same journey I started just a few years ago. The main difference is the level of experience and scope of ownership. More experienced engineers have encountered a wider range of technical challenges and can draw on that experience when making architectural decisions, troubleshooting complex issues or mentoring other team members. They often take the lead on larger initiatives, help shape the long-term direction of our applications, and are involved in production deployments and production support. One thing I really appreciate about my team is that everyone is encouraged to contribute ideas and opinions, regardless of tenure. I’ve had opportunities to influence technical decisions, collaborate with experienced engineers and learn from their expertise, while also taking ownership of my own work and deliverables. Working alongside more senior colleagues has been one of the most valuable parts of my growth as a software engineer. They have always been approachable, willing to answer questions and generous with sharing their knowledge and experience. Their guidance has been invaluable in helping me build confidence, develop my technical skills and steadily take on more responsibility over time. Definitely. The Leap programme was tailored to Fidelity’s technology stack, tools and ways of working, which made the transition into my business unit team much smoother after the programme. Rather than starting from scratch, I already had experience using many of the technologies and processes that my team worked with daily. Beyond the technical knowledge, the programme also gave me a solid foundation in collaborative ways of working, stakeholder communication and professional expectations. By the time I joined my business unit team, I felt confident contributing to discussions, working on sprint deliverables and taking ownership of my tasks. Looking back, the programme provided a safe environment to learn, ask questions and build confidence before moving into a permanent role. It significantly reduced the learning curve and helped me feel well-prepared for both the technical and professional aspects of working life. If you’re looking for a graduate programme that gives you real responsibility while also providing plenty of support, I would highly recommend the Fidelity Leap programme. It strikes a great balance between self-paced learning and instructor-led training, allowing you to take ownership of your development without ever feeling like you’re on your own. The programme is designed to bridge the gap between college and the professional world, helping you build both technical and workplace skills in a supportive environment. German agentic AI start-up Marble Cognition raises $3.75m
2026-11-02

German agentic AI start-up Marble Cognition raises $3.75m

Silicon Republic | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
From left: Felix Rösner and Christophe Schmit. Image: Marble Cognition The new funding will be used to scale inference, grow Marble’s engineering team in Munich and launch in the US. German AI start-up Marble Cognition has raised $3.75m in pre-seed funding for its agentic AI software platform. The Munich-based company, founded in 2025, aims to capture operational processes within companies and convert them “into systems AI can read” before deploying agentic AI ‘coworkers’ for assistance with “manual white-collar work”. The new funding will be used to scale inference, grow Marble’s engineering team in Munich and launch in the US, according to the company. The funding round was co-led by Heartcore Capital and Seedcamp, with participation from angels including a board member of Datadog, a founder of Personio, and executives from McKinsey, Mistral AI and Lovable, Marble said. “Every company we talk to wants AI to take over real work. But nobody can tell the AI how that work is actually done,” said Felix Rösner, co-founder of Marble. “Our ‘coworkers’ watch how the work happens before they take it over.” Rösner is also the company’s CEO; its CTO Christophe Schmit is the other co-founder. Marble works with tech unicorns, private equity-backed companies and AI roll-ups, including EGYM, Mutares and Enam, it said. “Most companies want to automate more, but the hard part has always been mapping out how the work gets done. Marble fills that gap, giving companies vital context for AI transformations that could define their future,” said Reshma Sohoni, general partner at Seedcamp, a European seed fund operating since 2007. According to Marble, its platform “observes how teams work, turning every process into a structured map that AI can read like code”, before creating agentic coworkers that run each process end to end in the tools teams already use, with humans kept “in the loop where judgement matters”. Max Niederhofer, partner at Heartcore, said: “Marble learns a whole company’s processes, anonymises the data, derives SOPs and deploys agentic AI against them. “What Felix and Christophe are building is a key technology for the AI-enabled workplace.” Everything the platform captures is anonymised before any person or AI model sees it, according to Marble. Its desktop app “records the screen, clicks and keystrokes in the business apps people use at work”, but “never [in] private apps, private tabs or anything outside working hours”. Employees can pause the process at any time and data is only analysed in aggregate, not based on individuals, the company said. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Futurism
Meta Is Refusing to Remove AI-Generated Videos of Coaches Inappropriately Touching Young Athletes When Users Report Them
2026-11-02

Meta Is Refusing to Remove AI-Generated Videos of Coaches Inappropriately Touching Young Athletes When Users Report Them

Futurism | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Content warning: this story includes descriptions and censored AI-generated images of sexual abuse. Facebook is hosting a large number of accounts dedicated to AI-generated content depicting coaches inappropriately touching young female athletes — and Meta isn’t taking them down, even when they’re directly reported by users. In the videos the accounts share, the coaches are older men. They’re shown in gyms and dance studios, with their hands on leotard-wearing athletes practicing gymnastics, ballet, and yoga. All the young athletes are women — or girls. Though precise ages aren’t specified, some look as young as teenagers, at times shown with braces on their teeth. In the AI-generated clips, which are nearly photorealistic, the athletes are often portrayed sweating profusely and breathing suggestively. The coaches run their hands over their lower backs and inner thighs as they stretch or practice poses, caressing and squeezing their bodies, and sometimes patting them on the buttocks or chest. In some videos, the older authority figures are shown touching the girls’ genitals over their outfits. More creepy videos show athletes sitting in a coach’s lap, or coaches kissing them on the mouth. The AI-generated girls in the clips react as though the touching is normal, often smiling and giggling as the harmful behavior occurs. But the touching depicted in the videos isn’t normal. It ranges from unnecessary to inappropriate to obviously abusive, mirroring the kind of real-world sexual abuse and grooming that are rampant in the sports they depict and in women’s athletics writ large. “I do consider this abuse material. These images sexualize and normalize abusive interactions between coaches and young athletes within a sporting environment where significant power imbalances already exist,” Kim Shore, a gymnast and safe sport advocate who consults with Gymnasts for Change International, told Futurism after reviewing numerous accounts publishing the AI content. “What is particularly disturbing is how easily inappropriate or abusive conduct can be disguised as playful, affectionate or helpful coaching behavior. This reinforces some of the very narratives that have allowed abuse to flourish in sport for decades.” But according to Facebook’s moderation system, the disturbing clips don’t violate Meta’s standards. Using an ordinary Facebook account, we alerted the social media platform to ten different accounts publishing this kind of content, reporting them for adult content and sexual exploitation. A full week later, we received notices from Facebook that eight of the accounts were didn’t violate the site’s rules, and would not be removed from the platform. (Two of the accounts vanished, though it’s unclear whether that had anything to do with our reports.) “We use a combination of technology and human reviewers to process reports and identify content that goes against our Community Standards. To keep our review process as fair as possible, we use the same set of Community Standards to review all reports,” read each of the eight identical notifications. “We’ve taken a look and found that the page doesn’t go against our Community Standards.” “We understand that this might be upsetting,” they added, “so we recommend exploring the options available to control what you see.” Some of the clips — which can attract more than 100,000 views apiece — are thinly veiled as educational, insinuating that the predatory coaches are helping the girls’ perfect their technique. “Keep your hips square,” an instructor is shown telling a young-looking ballerina in one video as he moves his hands to her private parts. “I’m trying,” she replies coolly, without flinching. Other accounts make little effort to hide their prurient intent. One has published nearly 20 slightly-different versions of the same AI video, which depicts an elderly coach alone with a leotard-wearing gymnast who looks no older than a teenager. In the comments of each clip, the poster urges viewers to click a link marketing a “members only” channel on WhatsApp — another platform owned by Meta — for the “prettiest video call anytime.” Another account’s header image shows a male coach’s hand directly on the crotch of a young female gymnast doing a split-legged headstand. In its Community Guidelines for “Adult Nudity and Sexual Activity,” Meta claims to forbid content showing “impending sexual activity, as indicated by positions which suggest contact is about to be initiated between a person’s hand… with another person’s genitals or anus.” Under guidelines for “Adult Sexual Exploitation,” its guidelines claim to prohibit material in which a “person in the imagery is (near) nude, engaged in sexual activity or in a sexually suggestive pose (this includes digitally created or AI-generated imagery).” Regarding “Child Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Nudity,” Meta claims to forbid “content (including photos, videos, real-world art, digital content, and verbal depictions) that sexualizes real or non-real children,” as well as “stimulation of genitals or anus, including when activity is imminent or not directly visible.” After receiving the support messages stating that the AI videos didn’t violate Meta’s Community Standards, we reached out to Meta’s media relations team, sharing links to the accounts we’d reported and asked why the content didn’t breach the social media giant’s guidelines. Shortly after we reached out, we noticed that all of the pages had been removed, and several of the support messages we’d previously received had been updated with a new response. “We removed the Page you reported,” read the new message. “We reviewed the Page and found that it goes against our Community Standards.” In a statement, Meta told Futurism that “whether it’s real or AI-generated, content that depicts abuse or exploitation has no place on our platforms. We’ve removed these accounts for violating our policies.” But it wasn’t until it was contacted by a journalist that Meta backtracked on its initial assessment that the pages didn’t violate its policies, a lapse that raises serious concerns about the company’s moderation practices and the effectiveness of the reporting policies available to everyday users. Why was this content held to a different standard when it was raised by a reporter, and not when it was flagged through official Facebook channels by a normal account holder? Asked about the gap between its response to an user on the platform versus a media inquiry, Meta defended its record on removing abusive material from its platform. “While there are times we miss things, we work hard to enforce our rules, which is shown by the fact that just last quarter, we proactively found and removed 98 percent of child endangerment posts before they were ever reported to us,” it said. After we reported the abusive pages, Facebook’s recommendation algorithm started serving up many more similar AI videos of coaches inappropriately touching, kissing, and groping young athletes in the feed of the account we used to flag it. The implication is dark: that Meta has the tech it needs to identify the disturbing clips, but instead of using it to crack down on the content, it’s using it to recommend more of the material to users who have interacted with it. The accounts often publish several near-identical AI videos per day, with some videos accumulating huge numbers of views. One video served to us after Meta shared its statement showed more than 176,000 views just two days after it was posted. “I really enjoyed that session. I feel fresh and positive now,” a girl decked in a pink leotard and matching socks, standing on one leg and holding the other in the air, tells the camera as a gray-haired trainer pats her genitals. Hash tags in the video’s caption include “#ballettok,” “#ballerina,” and “#balletmemes.” It’s hard to imagine that some of the videos aren’t being served by Meta’s algorithms to actual young athletes, raising the risk that they’ll see the grooming and abuse behavior normalized as they navigate the possibility of real-world exploitation. “I’m extremely concerned that children who are exposed to this content will internalize the message that if something similar is happening to them, they have to endure it because it is normalized in similar environments… they will be forced to further suppress their innate wisdom that this type of contact with an adult is not okay,” Shore told us. “Sport already does this to children by normalizing physical discomfort like training on injuries. Young athletes are taught to trust their coaches, follow instructions and accept physical contact as part of training. These images reinforce the dangerous idea that inappropriate touching or sexualized behavior is simply part of the coach-athlete relationship and athletes should accept it, tolerate it or even be grateful for the attention.” Shore added that one of her “greatest concerns” is that real-world predators could model behavior based on the abuse material proliferating on Facebook. “This AI-generated material acts a ‘how to’ manual for inappropriate behavior, particularly when abusive interactions are presented as ordinary coaching practices or expressions of care. It risks giving adults who are already inclined to exploit their authority examples of how to disguise abusive conduct as legitimate coaching, affection or playfulness,” she said. “We need to recognize that this material doesn’t exist in isolation. It circulates within a sporting culture where athletes have historically been conditioned to comply with authority, where boundaries have too often been blurred, and where abuse has frequently been minimized or excused.” “This content is normalizing behaviors,” she added, “that safeguarding efforts have spent decades trying to challenge.” As Futurism previously reported, Meta has also declined to remove AI-generated content depicting extreme physical child abuse. The company has elsewhere failed to catch hundreds of paid advertisements containing real and AI-generated child sexual abuse material, as Wired reported in September. Meta also faced backlash last year after Reuters reported on internal documents stating that Meta chatbots were allowed to “engage a child in conversations that are romantic or sensual.” Women’s and youth sports are notorious hunting grounds for abusers. According to a global 2021 study by the World Players Association, 13 percent of child athletes are subject to some kind of sexual abuse during their time in sports. That figure is likely an undercount, as TIME noted at the time, given that sexual abuse often goes unreported. In that context, the AI-generated Facebook clips of older men abusing their proximity to girls’ and women’s bodies to groom, sexualize, and exploit them is particularly appalling. That the athletes depicted in the videos are shown acting enthusiastic in the face of the predation is even worse, adding an air of normalization — or even romanticization — to the clearly exploitative encounters displayed in the AI-generated clips. But instead of taking its responsibility to police its platform seriously, it seems like Meta is operating by two different standards: dismissing regular users who report disturbing material, while jumping into action when it senses the threat of negative attention. Do you know anything about Meta’s content moderation strategy? Email us: tips@futurism.com. We can keep you anonymous. ChatGPT Is Giving Voters Ludicrous Misinformation Right as the Midterms Hit

Futurism | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
As crucial elections loom in the United States, AI chatbots are dumping political misinformation onto the populace with astonishing confidence. There’s evidence that the problem is widespread. According to recent reporting by the Washington Post, top AI chatbots are giving horribly flawed information to users asking for voting guides on the midterms. For example, when WaPo tasked ChatGPT with creating a voter guide for all the races and candidates relevant to a resident of Missouri, the chatbot added a Senate seat that isn’t even up for re-election. A similar test for a resident in Wyoming claimed that local politicians running in municipal elections were running for federal seats. In all, WaPo took a look at 3,000 AI-generated voting guides, which frequently shared false voting information that usually traced back to other AI-generated websites. In other words, the AI chatbots are eating their own slop and passing it off as authoritative election information. Overall, the paper found chatbots failed to find which congressional district an exact address was located in a whopping 48 percent of the time, while those that did got it wrong 22 percent of the time. Luckily, the share of users actually querying the chatbots seems to be relatively low. According to a late-October survey by the Pew Research Center, just eight percent of US voters say they’ve used an AI chatbot like ChatGPT, Copilot, or Gemini to look up information relevant to the ballot-box. While the ideal number would be zero voters in any nation receiving AI-generated election misinformation, it seems in 2026, we’ll have to take what we can get. Investors Are Getting Palpably Nervous About the AI Industry’s Financial Situation
2026-11-02

Investors Are Getting Palpably Nervous About the AI Industry’s Financial Situation

Futurism | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
For years now, some of the biggest players in the AI space have been investing in smaller startups — which in turn use the same exact money to purchase services from the same large companies. Investors have watched this circular financing with quiet but growing trepidation. After all, as the Wall Street Journal points out, if the AI bubble were to pop, the consequences could be dire. Now, with fears over a sudden collapse are as palpable as ever, such a “tangled web of AI bets,” as the newspaper puts it, could spell disaster. The signs aren’t exactly hard to identify. Just last week, Google’s parent company Alphabet experienced the biggest drop in its market value in its history, wiping out well over half a trillion dollars in value. The accompanying crash likely didn’t just send jitters across tech markets. Since the AI industry is effectively propping up the entire US economy, its failure could have far-reaching consequences to a public that’s increasingly skeptical of its offerings. Since most of these companies’ fates are inextricably linked by circular financing, a major economic downturn or weakening demand could have cascading repercussions, especially considering much of the sector’s financing is based on debt. Beyond the tech industry’s very bad week, data center operators aren’t looking much better. Nvidia customer CoreWave, which analysts have dubbed the “AI trade’s canary in the coal mine,” has also seen its shares decline over months, raising plenty of flags. Despite the widespread unease, enormous data center expansion efforts are in full swing, major bipartisan backlash notwithstanding. It’s an unprecedented all-in that could jeopardize the entire economy if it were to collapse. Even beyond the US, the circular financing is starting to worry investors sweat in Asia. The Bank of Korea recently warned of a “semiconductor version of Dutch disease,” an economic concept describing a booming sector that overtakes all other tradable market segments. Yet the country is doubling down, with president Lee Jae Myung pledging today to “boldly invest in AI and future growth engines,” effectively doubling AI and chip spending for next year. Only time will tell whether that’ll be the right move as more and more investors get cold feet. More on the AI bubble: Stock Market Bloodbath Spikes Fears of AI Bubble Bursting
Futurism
Prisons Using AI to Predict When Incarcerated People Will Commit Future Crimes
2026-11-01

Prisons Using AI to Predict When Incarcerated People Will Commit Future Crimes

Futurism | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Michigan is set to deploy a major new AI system to prisons across the state, meant to classify incarcerated people based on “how dangerous they are.” That’s according to Detroit channel Fox2, which reports that the Michigan Department of Corrections is plowing ahead with an inmate categorization algorithm that will sort people into various housing levels. In Michigan, incarcerated people are separated into five housing levels according to their security classification. In the past, screenings were performed by a committee of staff appointed by the Warden at each facility. Now, it seems the state wants to outsource that decision — or at least have it be heavily informed by — a non-profit tech company called Recidiviz. Recidiviz began as a data organization project at Google, meant to “uncover how technology could standardize criminal justice data and improve the system nationwide.” Its AI-integrated software platform is said to perform features like identifying individuals eligible for state-offered programs, reclassification, or release, and coordinate access to resources like transitional housing and employment. In a press release, MDOC said the Recidiviz software will be used to “optimize placement of incarcerated individuals” starting with a pilot program in a number of men’s facilities beginning in December. The state agency notes that the AI tool is expected to “improve MDOC’s ability to more accurately predict incarcerated individuals’ risk of future violence by approximately 30 percent,” though how the state arrived at that vague figure is unclear. For incarcerated people, housing designations are extremely consequential, determining which programming and resources they’ll be eligible for, and what kind of population they’ll be housed with for the duration of their sentence. Setting the state’s rationale aside, the MDOC-Recividiz partnership leaves some major questions unanswered. What kind of data will be collected, for example, will matter a great deal with regard to the AI system’s accuracy. As a ProPublica investigation into a similar risk-analysis program in Louisiana found, the kind of data being used to classify incarcerated people contained inherent racial and socioeconomic bias. As a result, Louisiana’s carceral system has become even more cruel and harmful to those earnestly working toward rehabilitation. In one case reported by ProPublica, a blind and wheelchair bound 70-year-old was determined to be a “moderate risk” of reoffending despite having a clean disciplinary record — a classification which results in an automatic loss of eligibility for parole. While Michigan’s system is geared toward housing assignments rather than parole decisions, it seems highly unlikely that software alone will solve the state’s ongoing corrections crisis — and if Louisiana’s embrace of AI is any example, it could very well make matters far, far worse. Michigan Department of Corrections was contacted for comment. More on incarceration: What Are the Ethics of Strapping VR Headsets on Inmates in Solitary Confinement?
Futurism
Microsoft Patents an AI That Can Literally Put Words in People’s Mouths in Real Time
2026-11-01

Microsoft Patents an AI That Can Literally Put Words in People’s Mouths in Real Time

Futurism | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Microsoft just filed a patent for an AI product that would be able to transform live video in real time to alter speakers’ words and faces. The patent, as first flagged by patent-tracker Patentlyze, is described by its inventors as a “near real-time artificial intelligence (AI) enhancement of video or augmented reality (AR) presentations of live events.” Using AI agents, the system would identify and alter or remove certain words, while editing a speaker’s face and mouth to match. The agents would rely on set parameters or user profiles to make the changes, and would also edit the video and audio just before and after a removed or replaced word to allow for “smooth transitions.” In an era of media and the internet increasingly defined by AI-fueled deepfakes, disinformation, and information-muddling slop, the system described in this patent may represent a new world of filtering tools that subtly alter reality as it takes place, in this case on a highly-personalized level. Viewers, the patent suggests, could consume whatever live event they want to without hearing what they don’t want to hear. The potential use cases floated in the patent vary. One possible application is real-time translation, with the system allowing for broadcasts and other live footage to be both heard and seen in a viewer’s preferred language. The patent also posits that such a tool could be used to remove filler words like “um” or “ah” from live speech, or to erase stutters or speech impediments. Other possible use cases, though, go much further. According to the patent, the system could be used to cut down on “personal words or phrases” like “coined terms,” as well as “colloquial terms” like the term “y’all” or the phrase “bite the bullet.” The patent also claims that the system could eliminate “mumbled words, mispronounced words, misused words, misspoken words, offensive words, insensitive words, or inappropriate words.” Replacement words, it adds, may come from an “internet search.” In other words, live broadcasts may not be so live anymore after all. Microsoft didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment. Dubbing has existed for ages, and video footage is edited all the time, both for normal and for insidious reasons. Automatically editing live videos in this way with the help of AI agents, however, could change how audiences fundamentally engage with live footage. As it stands, if people don’t like what they hear, they can simply turn something off; the system outlined by Microsoft describes an environment where people can instead adjust someone else’s speech to their personal taste. Language translation aside, does using a tool that eliminates errors, or erases words and phrases associated with a speaker’s local dialect, move us further into personalized bubbles? What’s so bad about hearing someone from the South say “y’all?” To that end, the patent does mention that an event’s original live video and audio may be recorded and stored for the sake of “ground truth,” nodding to potential misuse and confusion that could arise from such a tool. The kinds of live footage that could be transformed by the system include lectures, presentations, a range of television productions — awards ceremonies, game and cooking shows — and live sports, among several others. More on Big Tech patents: Meta Patents Technology to Detect Your Mood by Constantly Analyzing Your Tone of Voice

New Data Centers in the US Set to Wipe Out 15 Years of Greenhouse Gas Reductions
2026-11-01

New Data Centers in the US Set to Wipe Out 15 Years of Greenhouse Gas Reductions

Futurism | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Nearly 15 years worth of hard-won pollution reduction efforts are set to be undone in the United States, as a wave of data centers powered by gas plants enters construction. That’s according to a new analysis by the Sierra Club, first reported by Bloomberg, which notes that the US energy grid is expected to belch out 2.1 billion metric tons of carbon dioxide by 2030, assuming the country’s currently-planned AI developments enter operation as scheduled. That’s a massive rise compared to even 2025 — itself a near-record year for US carbon emissions. If the AI industry and its data center developers get their way, the analysis found, emissions will likely skyrocket by 42 percent over the five-year period, an expected increase of 618 million tons compared to 2025. “The numbers continue to sort of boggle our minds,”  the Sierra Club’s principal advisor for climate and energy Jeremy Fisher told Bloomberg. Due to major bottlenecks in the electrical grid, many data center developers have opted for on- or near-site power plants, which are overwhelmingly fueled by noxious fossil fuels. Projects like Elon Musk’s 1.2 gigawatt data center near Memphis are already choking out residents with fleets of methane generators, which legal experts say flaunt all kinds of local permitting and federal environmental regulations. Still, for anybody banking on having clean air to breathe, there is some hope. The number of data center projects at risk of cancellation is rising precipitously as time goes on — driven by grass roots organizing and increasing financial pressures. So if you’re one of the many activists mounting opposition to data centers in your community, just know that the benefits of a cancelled hyperscale facility extend far beyond your backyard. Custom chip designer Marvell has signed a deal to acquire optical chip and networking equipment maker Lumentum in an all-stock deal with an enterprise value of $115 billion (including debt) and an equity value of about $80 billion. Marvell will provide 4.5 shares of its common stock for each share of Lumentum. This makes the deal one of the biggest acquisitions in tech history. It compares to Microsoft buying Activision Blizzard for $68.7 billion in 2022 and Dell buying EMC for $67 billion in 2016. Interestingly, the two companies have one major shareholder in common: Nvidia. In March, as part of a multi-year agreement to purchase Lumentum’s advanced laser components, the GPU giant invested $2 billion to support Lumentum’s R&D efforts and fab expansion. Later that same month, Marvell announced that Nvidia invested $2 billion in an effort to beef up Marvell’s optical networking and chips portfolio. With Lumentum, Marvell says it will become a leader in AI connectivity, with products that range from chips to data centers. The transaction is expected to close in the first half of calendar year 2028, should both companies’ shareholders, and regulators, agree. Readwise takes on Amazon’s Kindle and Goodreads with its new app for book lovers

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. Readwise, the maker of a read-it-later app and digital reading companion that keeps track of your highlights across services, is now taking on Amazon’s e-book empire with its latest app, Readwise Books. The app is an e-book store also helps you track what you’ve read, similar to Amazon’s Goodreads. The bootstrapped startup described the new app as “a cross between Kindle and the Goodreads apps,” as it offers a single place to buy, read, and track books. However, Readwise Books aims to offer more detailed reading stats, than the dates you read a book and whether you finished it. It lets readers visualize richer reading data like words read, pace, depth, immersion, and sessions. Meanwhile, its new store offers “millions” of ebooks from the big five publishers and others, initially in the U.S., with more countries to come. Print books (fulfilled by Bookshop.org’s distributor) and some audiobooks are available as well, the company notes. The launch comes at a time when there’s been an explosion of new reading apps and book trackers, all hoping to challenge Goodreads by introducing new features like book clubs, widgets, improved discovery features, goal-setting, better lists and organizational tools, habit-tracking, and more. However, few indie developers have gone so far as to launch an alternative to Amazon’s Kindle e-books store, which remains the industry leader, with an 83% share of the market when Kindle+ (formerly Kindle Unlimited) is included in the stats. One of Kindle’s bigger advantages is that it offers an end-to-end solution for everything related to reading e-books, from the hardware devices to the book-tracking log. Readwise isn’t selling its own e-readers, but it is attempting to create its own community around reading, complete with tools for reading purchased and uploaded ebooks, tracking them (like on Goodreads), sharing your reading progress with friends, and more. It also offers a number of ways to track books outside its ecosystem. This includes a way to track reading sessions by using Kindle’s browser-based Cloud Reader and a feature that lets you snap photos of physical books to mark your progress. Plus, you can upload any DRM-free EPUBs (a common ebook file format without copy protection). The app can read your ebooks aloud via text-to-speech. Readwise believes its more in-depth metrics could be a big selling point, too, likening them to a “Strava for reading” — a reference to the metrics-heavy running app. “The desire to read books, like exercising and eating well, is laden with guilt,” the company explained in a blog post penned by co-founders Daniel Doyon and Tristan Homsi. “Many people feel like they should be reading more, but feeling bad about it tends to change nothing. The session tracking taps into a different psychological mechanism. It makes the reading you’re doing legible and, as the adage goes, that which is measured is improved.” The company adds that the most consistent feedback from users is that the app has led them to read more without willpower tricks like goal-setting, streaks, or scheduling. With publishers’ permission, the app will support AI-powered features as well, like session recaps, definitions and term lookups, word and phrase translations, and a location-aware ability to ask questions about the part you’re reading. (So far, only Harper Collins has opted in, though.) Unlike Goodreads’ locked-down ecosystem, Readwise is embracing open technology. This includes a public API and an MCP server. The latter supports AI agents that could be used to research book recommendations on your behalf, use your Readwise data to create a books page on your personal website, or even wire up your hackable e-reader to log your sessions automatically, depending on how involved your AI use cases may be. The new Readwise Books app will remain free, with some AI features later going behind a paywall. Its prior apps for reading and tracking highlights are either $7.99 per month each, or $9.99 per month for both. The Books app is currently in beta and requires an invite, unless you’re already a Readwise subscriber. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. SpaceX reportedly wants to buy Boost Mobile to compete with AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon

TechCrunch | 2026-11-02

SpaceX is looking to buy Boost Mobile from EchoStar, according to a report from The Washington Post. If the deal goes through, it would help the Elon Musk-owned company build a hybrid cellular-satellite network that could help catapult it into fourth place among the top U.S. wireless providers. The space-launch company has been dabbling in cellular connectivity, rolling out satellite access to T-Mobile’s subscribers in 2024. Last year, SpaceX bought $17 billion worth of wireless spectrum from EchoStar for cellular services, which could help the company’s Starlink service reach inside buildings, for example, something it currently struggles with. “This is the last critical piece of the spectrum puzzle needed for SpaceX to provide complete phone coverage in America,” Musk said on X at the time of the spectrum acquisition. Yet, if Musk is still looking to buy access to terrestrial networks, it apparently wasn’t the last piece. Elon Musk, SpaceX’s CEO, reportedly spoke directly with EchoStar chairman Charlie Ergen in October about acquiring Boost Mobile, the mobile virtual network operator. EchoStar has about 7.5 million customers between Boost Mobile and GenMobile, its other mobile service offering. Musk has also approached AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon about buying capacity on their networks. All three rejected Musk, and they may still have a say in a SpaceX acquisition of Boost Mobile. Boost doesn’t operate its own towers, instead leasing capacity from AT&T and T-Mobile, which might need to approve such a deal. Sources speaking to The Post said that network leases are usually nontransferable. The big three providers each have over 100 million subscribers, dwarfing Boost Mobile. But while the acquisition would give SpaceX an immediate bump in subscribers, the true value of the deal would be giving the company access to terrestrial cellular networks. Gaining access to towers leased to Boost Mobile would expand coverage significantly — Boost currently covers most of the 48 contiguous states in the U.S. — allowing Starlink to cover devices when they’re outdoors and relying on cell towers to handle devices when Starlink signals can’t reach them. 42AI comes out of stealth with $62.5M and a world model that can ‘predict human behavior’
2026-11-02

42AI comes out of stealth with $62.5M and a world model that can ‘predict human behavior’

TechCrunch | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. Here’s a question straight out of sci-fi: What if AI could predict what you were going to do before you decided to, even in a completely new situation? Israeli startup 42AI says it is developing a “behavioral world model” that can do just that, and it has just come out of stealth with $62.5 million in seed funding. The company is initially running its predictive system at a handful of financial brokerages, where it says the model can help predict how individual traders will respond to volatile conditions. “Behavior is not deterministic; it’s constantly probabilistic,” 42AI’s CEO and co-founder Amit Gal-Or told TechCrunch when I asked him to justify his company’s tall claim. He argues that people have underlying psychological tendencies that influence their decisions even when their circumstances change, and that’s what the startup is trying to measure and convert into data that the model can understand. “A week from now, you might be a different person, but your tendencies will always play out in certain areas to a probability or distribution,” his co-founder and CTO Ehud Ezra added. And that, the company argues, is what the model does: show the probability of a person making a certain decision. 42AI says the model tries to figure out people’s internal motivations instead of extrapolating patterns based on their previous actions. To do that, 42AI examines how people react to seemingly unrelated events. For an investor or trader, for example, such events might include completing KYC processes, onboarding, support calls, money transfers, or trading stocks. “When the person is talking to support or sales in the initial process […] we’re looking at the sentiment, we’re looking at the voice, we’re looking at a lot of interactions that they’re doing, how many times do they call, and so forth,” added CTO and co-founder Ehud Ezra. “And all of those data points are going into the model. Later on, you have the interactions: how you buy and sell, and when, and we’re looking at the market at that stage.” “So we’re trying to look at how they’re looking at the world. In a world model, every individual is the world,” he added. The company then converts those observations into mathematical representations of traits, which are then fed into a transformer-based world model that lays out probabilities for possible decisions under different circumstances. (The company uses a separate language model to generate explanations for its predictions). Ezra said the model is trained to preserve a person’s underlying tendencies during simulations. So if someone is usually risk-averse, but the model suddenly treats them as a risk-taker, “the target is off,” he said, explaining how the model’s training rewards are structured. 42AI spent three years developing this approach, combining psychological research with neuroscience and genetics, as well as advice from experts in psychology and PTSD, and Dan Shechtman, who was awarded the 2011 Nobel Prize in Chemistry. Gal-Or said the team first studied thousands of people via psych assessments and behavioral scenarios, before adding government and biobank datasets covering more than 2 million people. Its customer datasets have since expanded to cover almost 30 million people, he said. In live deployments, the model analyzes customers’ existing records, including transactions and support calls. Gal-Or pointed out the system doesn’t need personally identifiable information, arguing that “the model never knows who the person is.” However, it seems the company still creates individual behavioral profiles from linked customer activity. In predicting market trades, 42AI claims its model achieves 98% accuracy, but Gal-Or clarified that this is more a measure of whether trading flows fall within a predicted range, not if each trade was predicted correctly. He also said the model consistently achieves an F1 score of 0.8 to 0.85 for predicting trading direction and volume. (F1 score is a metric in machine learning that measures how well a classification model can balance correct predictions against false alarms and missed events). And it’s not like the model can work everywhere, given it’s still a machine in a box. “There’s a natural ceiling because you really don’t have access to everything that a person experiences,” Gal-Or said. Ezra explained that the system’s accuracy also suffers when it misses important situational context, like if someone chooses to change their trading choices based on advice from a friend or a colleague. Gal-Or said 42AI is working with 10 large brokerages, who are using it to manage liquidity, margin requirements, and hedging exposure. The company is onboarding more customers, he said, and is generating more than “seven figures” in revenue, though he wouldn’t specify a number. Customers pay the startup a flat fee, or a percentage of the value the platform helps them gain. He claimed one brokerage generated approximately $30 million in additional value through dynamic hedging. The $62.5 million seed funding came from the founders and Infinity Group over three years, and wasn’t entirely raised from external investors, Gal-Or said. Infinity was founded by his father, Amir Gal-Or, who is also 42AI’s chairman and a co-founder. 42AI now plans to raise more money. Beyond finance, 42AI is now exploring applications for its model in gaming, fraud prevention and cybersecurity, and one day wants to use it to help robots and AI agents understand people. But that may be a ways off yet: Ezra acknowledged that the model needs to be trained on rules and context for each new domain. And like with all AI tools, there’s a potentially troubling side to 42AI’s technology: if a brokerage can anticipate what its customers may do, what’s to stop someone from using that knowledge to their own benefit? Ezra admitted that this was “a valid concern” when I raised it, and the founders pointed to regulations, monitoring and an internal ethics committee, though they didn’t explain what, if anything, would stop their customers from using the technology to gain an advantage over the very people it’s profiling. Gal-Or, however, was confident that the technology could help address such risks. “We are very proud that actually we are part of the solution; we are the solution to this problem,” he said. Omen AI adds liquid know-how in $16 million Aquatic Labs acquisition

Omen AI adds liquid know-how in $16 million Aquatic Labs acquisition

TechCrunch | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. U.S. data centers use 17 to 19 billion gallons of water to cool their hot-running chips each year — and that number is expected to triple in the next five years, according to The Water Foundation. That’s an expensive and politically tricky proposition for investors throwing billions of dollars at the challenge of scaling up AI. One way to use less water is to do a better job monitoring the cooling fluids, a task that currently involves sending samples away to a laboratory for tests in a multi-day process. Omen AI was founded in 2024 to solve that problem. The company is building a real-time sensor to determine whether valves were failing, or if bacteria growth in the system threatened to choke off water flow. After raising a $31 million Series A in June, Omen is now acquiring competitor Aquatic Labs in a $16 million cash-and-stock deal. The deal came together after Omen founder Zach Laberge was introduced to Aquatic Labs’ CEO Allan Adams by an investor in the filtration space. Both startups were building real-time water sensors with complementary technology: Omen’s sensors looked for elements in the water, while the Aquatic Labs sensor tests its alkalinity. Putting those two technologies in the same sensor creates a “much richer picture of the water and the fluid,” Adams told TechCrunch, while uniting their vision of automating what had previously been a slow, manual process. The combination unites two classic species of founder: Laberge dropped out of high school to pursue his first company, which did similar fluid analysis for large construction machines that depend on hydraulics. Adams was an academic, trained as a physicist, who spent much of his career developing advanced ocean sensing technology as an MIT professor and Woods Hole Oceanographic Institute researcher before starting his company. Now, with Adams as the company’s chief science officer, the combined teams are working to integrate both technologies into one sensor, which they expect to have on the market by early next year. Omen’s initial sensors will be monitoring chips pulling in 250 to 300 MW of power by the end of the year, worth about $5 to $7 billion, Laberge told TechCrunch, with the hope of being installed on the equivalent of 1 GW of chips by the end of 2027. The added value is in uptime: If the fluid system gets out of whack, data center operators typically choose to dump the water rather than risk damage to their expensive equipment. That means shutting down operations until the fluid system is restored, which costs a lot of money. Monitoring lets data center operators use the same fluid for longer, and minimize the costs of a clean up. Adams hopes to see their technologies have an impact outside of data centers as well, telling TechCrunch that “water goes into data centers, and then it comes out and gets dumped into municipal water, and you want that whole process to happen in a way that’s as safe and responsible as possible — and that means you got to measure stuff.” AI companies once estimated as much as $4 trillion would be spent on AI infrastructure by 2030, but some investors have grown skeptical of the buildout, sending hyperscaler stocks plummeting in recent days. Omen’s team isn’t worried about shifting sentiment though, since their work comes after a data center has been built. “We’re working customers on stuff they’re building today or have already built, and so it doesn’t really affect us because all of those projects were financed 12 months ago,” Laberge said. “Our view is, if there’s tightness, this is still going to be the biggest market in human history, even if you sliced it in half.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. The CEO Moment: Land O’Lakes’ Beth Ford

The CEO Moment: Land O’Lakes’ Beth Ford

TIME | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings There may be no CEO in America with a resume quite like Beth Ford’s. The leader of Land O’Lakes has worked in publishing, consumer goods, and oil, and now runs one of the country’s leading farming collectives. She is on a mission to explain to Americans the joy and challenges of the farmers who make our food, and to tell the world about what agriculture means for American security and prosperity. As harvest season across the country got underway, Ford joined THE CEO MOMENT, our interview series for the world’s most influential business leaders, to talk about her career path, the state of the American farmer, and whether or not we’re eating more butter. What were your early jobs like?I taught tennis. I “walked beans,” meaning getting the weeds out of a bean field. I was a house painter, and then I was a janitor. I would have different buildings or toilets or things like that I needed to clean. My jobs were very manual, and I was grateful for the work and I certainly understood the importance of it. You ran a barge dock in Boston for Mobil. What did you learn?I was a night warehouse foreman. I was the dock foreman in Boston. You learn about people–you know, some very funny, funny moments. What is a funny moment on a barge dock in Boston?I'd say, “Tommy, you need to be on time and we need to put in your teeth.” I don't mean to be dismissive about it because it's the joy and the mess of the human condition. How are farmers doing in America today?This is the most expensive crop that anybody's ever put in. Fertilizer prices [are] up, diesel is up…steel, everything. They're working under tremendous pressure and a lot of volatility. What do people get wrong about farmers?I fear that there's just a lack of knowledge, because we're a land of plenty….Don't we all agree food is important? Don't we agree it is a strength of America that we have food self-sufficiency? If we agree [about] that, please don't see this as a political discussion. Instead, this is a business-investment discussion. It is a national-security discussion. I also want to say to folks in urban areas, people aren't trying to flee to the coast. Folks are happy in their towns. They deserve investment. They need investment. It is to all of our advantage to have this. I constantly quote that 19% of the population in rural areas, they comprise 44% of the military. They're investing in this country in so many ways. Two-thirds of farm workers are non-citizen immigrants. Can we build a sensible, clear immigration policy in this country?Immigration is a tremendous lever for economic growth through the United States for businesses. We did an analysis that showed that the GDP could grow 3.3% over a trillion dollars in the next 10 years if we get some immigration reform. Are we eating more butter today? Oh, yes. We continue to. We’re eating more cheese as well, although we still trail the Europeans, so everybody should get busy with that. We take credit for that since we once put a big beautiful swirl of butter on the cover with the line, “Eat butter.”I’m so grateful.
