Could a Democrat Win the Race for the Late Lindsey Graham's Seat in Deep-Red South Carolina?
2026-11-02

Could a Democrat Win the Race for the Late Lindsey Graham's Seat in Deep-Red South Carolina?

TIME | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings South Carolina Democratic Senate candidate Dr. Annie Andrews speaks at a Get Out The Vote rally at the Old Town Amphitheater in Rock Hill, South Carolina, on Oct. 28, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings South Carolinians have elected Republican senators in every election cycle in the 21st century. But recent polls indicate that GOP Sen. Darline Graham is facing a tough fight against her Democratic challenger in the race for the seat long held her late brother, Lindsey Graham. Polling by the New York Times/Siena, released last week, found that likely voters in South Carolina are essentially evenly split over whether they would vote for the Republican nominee, who was appointed by the state governor to finish the remainder of her late brother’s term after he died in July and later announced that she would run for the seat in the midterms, and Dr. Annie Andrews, a pediatrician who won the Democratic primary in June. In August, the Cook Political Report rated the South Carolina race as “Solid Republican,” indicating that the GOP was widely expected to retain control of the Senate seat. But the election forecaster later shifted the race slightly in Democrats’ favor to “Likely Republican;” then, on Oct. 20, it changed its rating again to “Lean Republican.” The race is just one example of how Republicans are apparently struggling to maintain voter support in this election cycle. Most generic ballot polls show that voters are more likely to back Democrats than Republicans in this midterm election. Other polling suggests that GOP candidates are even having trouble keeping leads in states that are typically considered strongholds for the party, such as in Texas, where most surveys have found that the Democratic candidate James Talarico is ahead—by a narrow margin—of Republican Ken Paxton in a heated and expensive Senate race heading into Election Day on Tuesday. This trend is also visible in gubernatorial races, such as the one in Ohio, where Republican candidate Vivek Ramaswamy has launched an aggressive and controversial campaign against his Democratic opponent Dr. Amy Acton. The last time Ohioans elected a Democrat to be their governor was in 2006, but the Cook Political Report recently shifted its rating of the race from “Toss Up” to “Lean Democrat.” Political experts say the battle for South Carolina’s Senate seat is in line with this national trend. They also say, though, that there are local circumstances that have contributed to how tight the race has become. According to political experts, part of the reason the race in South Carolina has become so close is because the current national political environment is challenging for Republicans. Historically speaking, the sitting President’s party tends to lose seats in Congress during midterm elections. And right now, the pressure on Republicans is being compounded by the fact that President Donald Trump’s approval rating has dropped to a new low; the last time a President’s approval rating fell to a comparable level heading into the midterms was in 1946, when Harry Truman was in the Oval Office. Read More: Trump’s Approval Is at a New Low. Here’s Where Other Presidents’ Stood Before the Midterms—and How Their Parties Fared Polling shows that the economy and the war in Iran are significant contributors to the public’s dissatisfaction with the President and his Administration. “That’s hurting Republicans everywhere across the country,” says Joseph Ura, a professor of political science at Clemson University. “You see tight races in places that aren’t usually tight for Republicans, like Texas and in Ohio … so that’s everywhere, including South Carolina.” On top of that, there are local circumstances at play that were set in motion by Lindsey Graham’s death in July. Before his death, Lindsey Graham, who had held his seat in the upper chamber since 2003 and served in the House before that, was running for re-election and won the Republican primary in June by a nearly 28-point margin. He was widely expected to win the general election in November. But his death “fundamentally changed the race,” says Shannon Bridgmon, a political scientist at the University of South Carolina. Although Darline Graham is technically running as an incumbent, she has been in the upper chamber for only a few months, so “the race is functioning as an open race within the state,” Bridgmon says. “That—I don’t want to say never happens here, but it’s certainly rare.” Over the summer, a crowded field of Republican candidates sought the party’s nomination to vie for Lindsey Graham’s seat. But no candidate was able to secure a majority of the vote in the August primary, so the race advanced to a runoff election later that month between Darline Graham and Rep. Ralph Norman. Darline Graham ultimately emerged victorious by a less than 5-point margin. The primary, Bridgmon says, “created this significant fracture” within the Republican party, as voters were split between Darline Graham and Norman. The divisions within the GOP have become even more apparent as Norman has said that he has no plans to join the Senator’s campaign. He has said, however, that he intends to vote for her. “There seems to be some sort of dissatisfaction within the party that [Darline Graham] is the nominee,” Bridgmon says. “There were questions about ‘Should she just gracefully finish out the late Sen. Graham’s term and step aside?’ There were some resentments there in some of those camps, and there have even been whispers and, well, rumblings, I would say, about a write-in campaign for Norman among his most ardent supporters.” Scott Huffmon, a professor of political science at Winthrop University and director of the Center for Public Opinion & Policy Research, says that some Republicans seem to feel that Darline Graham was “ordained” as the party’s candidate. In July, Trump publicly praised her, urging her to run in November even before she had announced her intention to vie for a full term. Huffmon adds that some of the more conservative members of the Republican Party had not been big supporters of Lindsey Graham because they thought he was “too liberal.” “Now you have a situation where the most conservative people within the party feel that their voice has been ignored, they’ve had a candidate foisted on them, and that candidate happens to be the sister of the person they had a problem with before,” Huffmon says. “Their internal fight has sort of made this a race that Darline Graham is kind of fighting to win instead of coasting.” Before she was appointed to finish out the remainder of her brother’s term, Darline Graham had never held an elected office. And her minimal political experience has generated criticism in recent months. She received blowback, including from members of her own party, after a debate against Norman before the runoff in August, in which she said “I’m not that informed on national security” upon being asked a question about Taiwan and the South China Sea. “She does not have the same public standing in the state as her brother did and does not have the same kind of relationship with the voters in the state that [the late] Sen. Graham had,” Ura says. Darline Graham is also up against a strong Democratic candidate, according to Huffmon. Andrews is “a great fundraiser” who has been able to spread strong campaign messaging in the state and “gins up excitement wherever she goes,” Huffmon says. Bridgmon points out that Andrews has even been able to secure bipartisan endorsements; two former South Carolina governors—Republican David Beasley and Democrat Jim Hodges—appeared in an Andrews campaign video in which they both endorsed Andrews in the race. “Democrats are motivated to go against Trump anyway, but they are excited by Annie Andrews, so there’s a whole lot of excitement on that side,” Huffmon says. “Darline Graham is not an experienced campaigner. She’s not generating the excitement among her base that she needs to, and … the Republicans need it doubly so because of the lack of popularity of Trump, so her inability to generate enthusiasm among the people who should be her base is helping this go down to the wire.” Bridgmon says that Andrews “still has an uphill climb” to win the Senate seat. A critical question, Bridgmon says, is who will turn out to vote on Election Day: Will Norman’s supporters, for instance, show up to the polls, and if so, will they cast their ballots for Darline Graham, or for someone else? Ura says that South Carolina is a state that “still leans Republican,” so he would be a little “surprised” if Andrews did end up on top after Election Day. But he notes that this race has taken an unexpected turn, and “will have effects for how Republicans and Democrats both campaign going forward.” “This race is almost certain to be much, much closer and a much better performance for the Democratic nominee than anybody could have reasonably expected in 2024,” Ura says. “I still think the Republican is more likely than not to win this election, but it’s going to be probably the best performance by a Democrat in a statewide race in South Carolina in a very long time.”

2 of the Most Effective Democrats of the Midterms Aren't on the Ballot, Campaign Operatives Say

2 of the Most Effective Democrats of the Midterms Aren't on the Ballot, Campaign Operatives Say

TIME | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Left: Pete Buttigieg departs after speaking during a "Get Out The Vote" rally on Oct. 19, 2026, in Highland Springs, Virginia. Right: Sen. Raphael Warnock of Georgia campaigns with Rep. Chris Pappas, a Senate candidate in New Hampshire on Oct. 6, 2026, in Nashua, N.H. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings One is a former mayor, White House hopeful, and Transportation Secretary. The other preaches from the pulpit once claimed by the Rev. Dr. Martin Luther King Jr. when he isn’t casting votes in the Senate. Neither Pete Buttigieg nor Sen. Rafael Warnock of Georgia are on the ballot this year, but both have been all over the campaign trail, stumping for candidates across the country ahead of Tuesday’s midterm elections. Democratic operatives say they are two of the most in-demand boosters this cycle. While the likes of Sen. Bernie Sanders of Vermont and Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez of New York are surefire base pleasers and former Presidents Barack Obama and Bill Clinton are draws for legacy Democrats, Warnock and Buttigieg offer an efficient and plain-spoken manner that admirers say cuts across ideologies and generations. It’s the kind of positive feedback that is sure to gain notice as handicapping for the 2028 presidential race begins in earnest after Tuesday. And, coincidentally, both men’s stump schedules have sent them to places that may prove crucial to anyone with White House ambitions. Just last month, Warnock made stops in South Carolina, which is due to kick off the Democratic nominating calendar, as well as Nevada, New Hampshire, North Carolina, Michigan, and Wisconsin. He also dipped into Mississippi as Democrats are hopeful that a Blue Wave might just be big enough to flip a Senate seat there. Read more: The Key Congressional Races to Watch on Election Night Meanwhile, Buttigieg has campaigned in 38 states this year—18 of them more than once. His final week of campaign travel alone had him in Michigan and Wisconsin—both recent swing states—as well as Colorado and Minnesota. All told, Buttigieg has campaigned for more than 70 candidates in person this cycle—including 24 House hopefuls in Republican districts marked by national Democrats as top contenders for flipping. Both itineraries suggest the hustle of someone already running for President. Democratic strategists say each figure brings a unique constituency to the table. But they share an overlapping ability to inspire activists’ imaginations while also appealing to middle-of-the-road voters who just want to skip past the partisan screaming that seems to dominate the discourse this late in the campaign. “No one confuses these guys for socialists,” says one Democratic strategist who has pushed for the party to move back to the center. Adds a second: “These guys aren’t hacks.” Buttigieg, a Harvard-educated veteran who is a technocrat at his core, has become, along with his husband Chasten, favored advocates for the Democratic brand. Buttigieg has proven an able sparring partner with Fox News personalities, showing how Democrats can go into unfriendly territory and hold their own. It’s why the former Mayor of South Bend, Ind., who ran the Department of Transportation in the Biden Administration, is a frequent mention when Democrats mull their bench for 2028. In August, Buttigieg said he’s “more inclined than not” to launch another White House run. “One of the things the voters have seen is the loud-mouth guy at the end of the bar is a source of noise but not a source of strength,” Buttigieg said during a swing through New Hampshire for the Senate campaign of Rep. Chris Pappas. “Right now, voters are looking for the strength that comes with discipline and not chaos. People have had enough of the chaos. They want people to get things done.” Warnock, meanwhile, has made inroads with a different slice of the Democratic Party. Representing a swing state that was not too long ago viewed as deep-red, he is a clear-eyed pragmatist in the Senate, where he has managed to work with Republicans like Ted Cruz on infrastructure programs and has privately counseled colleagues on showing restraint during heated moments. Warnock has maintained his role as the senior pastor at the Atlanta church once led by King, and is known among colleagues as the Reverend Senator. His stump speeches are often regarded by attendees more as sermons than straight politicking. “We lose elections by the margins of our disengagement,” Warnock said during a visit to another deep-red state last month. “Mississippi is a blue state. It just doesn’t know it yet.” When asked in September if he planned to launch a presidential bid, Warnock responded, “We’ll see what happens.” The two men are among a long list of aspiring Presidents showing up for fellow Democrats on the trail. Sens. Chris Murphy of Connecticut, Cory Booker of New Jersey, and Mark Kelly of Arizona have all been popular draws from the savvy and curious members of their party eager to start building for a post-Donald Trump era. Former White House Chief of Staff Rahm Emanuel, Gov. Andy Beshear of Kentucky, Gov. J.B. Pritzker of Illinois, Gov. Josh Shapiro of Pennsylvania, and Gov. Wes Moore of Maryland are certainly in the mix. And a pair of Michiganders, Sen. Elissa Slotkin and Gov. Gretchen Whitmer, are also discussed. But it’s Warnock and Buttigieg who are proving to be the most dynamic surrogates for swing-state candidates given their communications skills. Warnock’s connection with Black voters in particular has drawn the attention of grateful candidates looking to shore up that crucial voting bloc. Last month, he dashed to Detroit to lend his credibility to Abdul El-Sayed, who is locked in a tense and pivotal Senate race in Michigan against Rep. Mike Rogers. “This is not just for our U.S. Senate seat,” El-Sayed said on Oct. 20. “It is for control of the entire United States Senate. There's a reason that the Reverend is here. It's because he really wants to make sure that he's not in the minority for the next two years.”

Why a Top House Democrat Now Supports Impeaching Trump and Hegseth
2026-11-02

Why a Top House Democrat Now Supports Impeaching Trump and Hegseth

TIME | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Rep. Adam Smith, a Washington Democrat who has served in the House since 1996, at the U.S. Capitol on Sept. 4, 2025. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings When the top Democrat on the House Armed Services Committee was asked by TIME in May about the prospect of impeaching President Donald Trump, he declined to commit, warning that every impeachment he had witnessed during his decades in Congress had left its target politically stronger. Five months later, Rep. Adam Smith of Washington is prepared to take that risk. With Democrats hoping to reclaim control of the House in Tuesday's midterm elections, Smith says that he now supports impeaching Trump and is inclined to also back proceedings against Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, making him one of the most senior Democrats to endorse such a course. "Trump has become beyond dangerous. He is just completely out of control in what he says and how he is governing," Smith told TIME in an interview a week before the election. "I think we have to try, even if it doesn't succeed." Smith's endorsement is particularly significant because he would be in line to chair the Armed Services Committee if Democrats win the House, putting him at the center of investigations into the Pentagon's handling of the war with Iran, its approach to Ukraine, and military operations in Latin America. His position comes as Democrats prepare for the possibility of reclaiming congressional power with little agreement over how aggressively to confront the President. Interviews with more than two dozen Democrats reveal a party broadly convinced that Trump has engaged in conduct warranting impeachment, but deeply divided over whether another attempt to remove him would be an effective use of its authority. Trump's two previous impeachments ended in Senate acquittals, and even a Democratic victory this week is unlikely to produce anything approaching the two-thirds majority required for conviction. Read more: Calls to Impeach Trump Collide With Reluctant Democratic Leadership Smith, who has served in the House since 1996, noted previously that he was one of the few House members to have sat through three impeachments. "Every single time the person impeached got stronger after they were impeached than before," Smith told TIME in May, arguing that Democrats needed to consider what another impeachment could realistically achieve rather than pursue it simply to demonstrate their willingness to confront Trump. "I want to make sure that if we're going down that road it's going to help defeat Trump and his poisonous ideology," he said at the time. Since then, he says, Trump's increasingly conciliatory posture toward Russian President Vladimir Putin, his decisions involving American support for Ukraine, and his public rhetoric have convinced him that the President's continued tenure presents an extraordinary danger. "The Ukraine stuff definitely had a profound impact," Smith says. "When I saw how close he was getting to Putin and shutting off support for Ukraine, being more pro-Putin. I mean, that's a big part of it." He also cites Trump's increasingly "unhinged speeches," including remarks about rising gasoline prices amid the war in the Middle East. Smith’s willingness to embrace impeachment puts him at odds with more cautious Democrats who want the party to prioritize addressing the economic concerns that have dominated the campaign, while establishing an investigative record that might ultimately lead to impeachment. He acknowledges the formidable obstacles to removing Trump but argues that the political environment has changed since the President's first term. "I don't think it will be viewed as negatively or as partisan as in the previous two efforts, just because the public is waking up to the con game that Trump put on," he says now. "The impeachable offenses have been obvious for a long time.” Other senior House Democrats are less certain that pursuing impeachment would accomplish much, even if they share Smith's assessment of Trump's conduct. Rep. Jamie Raskin of Maryland, who led the House managers during Trump's second impeachment trial in 2021, says he has "little doubt that the president has committed multiple impeachable offenses," citing what he describes as unconstitutional military action and abuses of congressional spending authority. But he draws a distinction between establishing grounds for impeachment and deciding whether another trial would be the best use of Congress's time and political capital. "The question of whether he committed impeachable offenses and whether we are going to pursue impeachment are two different issues," Raskin tells TIME. Trump was impeached in 2019 over his dealings with Ukraine and again in 2021 over his role in the Jan. 6 attack on the Capitol. Both proceedings ended in acquittal, although seven Republican senators voted to convict him in the second trial. While some Democrats regarded those efforts as necessary to establish a historical and constitutional record, another impeachment could consume months of congressional attention, galvanize Trump's supporters, and leave voters questioning what the party had accomplished with its new majority. Sen. Adam Schiff of California, who led the prosecution during Trump's first impeachment trial, says Democrats should avoid reaching conclusions before knowing the composition of the next Congress and what investigations might uncover. "There's no question he's been engaging in conduct that is very likely impeachable," Schiff told TIME. "The question is, is it viable?" Schiff disputes the notion that the earlier impeachment efforts strengthened Trump politically, but argues that Democrats should concentrate initially on investigating corruption that has affected Americans' finances. "Our primary focus ought to be on corruption that has raised the cost of living for people," he says. House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries has similarly declined to commit to impeachment, saying Democrats "haven't ruled anything in, and we haven't ruled anything out." He has indicated that investigations should help determine whether the House pursues that course. Some Democrats are looking toward members of Trump's Cabinet, particularly Hegseth, as potentially more viable targets, reasoning that Republicans who would never vote to remove the President might be more willing to turn against a defense secretary whose leadership has drawn criticism from within their own party. "There are Republicans who would support impeaching Hegseth who might not support doing it to Trump," Smith says. The Defense Secretary has faced mounting scrutiny over his handling of the Iran war, the removal of senior military officers, and the Pentagon's relationship with Congress. Some Republicans have publicly expressed frustration with his leadership and the department's failure to provide adequate information about the war's objectives, costs, and duration. A push to impeach him has already taken shape in the Republican-controlled House, although Trump has defended Hegseth, calling him a "great warrior" and dismissing Democratic efforts to remove him. Raskin says that impeaching a Cabinet secretary would be less likely to consume Congress's agenda or raise the same concerns about overturning the result of a presidential election. "The question of cabinet officers is a little bit easier because it doesn't necessarily consume all of the policy oxygen in Congress," he says. However, impeaching Hegseth would require a majority in the House, and removing him would still require a two-thirds vote in the Senate. Republicans who have criticized his performance have not necessarily indicated any willingness to support impeachment. But the growing unease over the administration's military decisions, particularly the conduct and costs of the war with Iran, could offer Democrats an opening that does not exist with Trump. Rep. Jason Crow of Colorado, a member of the Armed Services Committee who served as a House manager during Trump's first impeachment trial, says lawmakers have been preparing for extensive investigations into the Pentagon regardless of the election's outcome. He cites the removal of senior military officers, alleged misuse of defense funds, threats against NATO, possible troop withdrawals from Europe, and the administration's handling of the Iran war as areas demanding scrutiny. But Crow stops short of endorsing impeachment as the preferred course. "Impeachment is only one tool that Congress has," he told TIME. "If the goal is to get Pete Hegseth out of the Department of Defense to stop corruption, to erect some guardrails against this runaway, reckless administration, and to protect the country against the increased erratic and dangerous behavior of Donald Trump, then we have to look post-November what the Congress looks like," Crow says. "Maybe impeachment is part of that. I wouldn't hesitate to use that if that's the right one."

Update your preferences in Account Settings The Nov. 3 midterm elections are already groundbreaking. It’s the most expensive election cycle in U.S. history in terms of political ad spending, with more than $11 billion expected to be spent, according to analysis firm AdImpact. States have redrawn congressional maps between censuses at a rate the National Conference of State Legislatures says has not been seen since the 1800s, as parties try to secure victories. And Donald Trump has an historically low approval rating for a President heading into the midterms. This election is also set to see massive turnover on Capitol Hill, with dozens of members of Congress resigning, retiring, or seeking other office. Read More: Republican Panic Sets In Over Prospects of a Massive Blue Wave Depending on the results, it could see a number of identity barriers broken, too. A record 26 women are vying for Governor posts across the country, according to Rutgers University's Center for American Women and Politics. A handful of candidates for Congress could also become national historic firsts if they win. Rep. Joseph Rainey of South Carolina is believed to be the first Black American elected to the U.S. House in 1870. He also was the first Black American to preside over the House of Representatives—albeit temporarily—during a debate on an appropriations bill providing for the management of Native American reservations in 1874. But no Black person has served as Speaker of the House. That could change if an anticipated blue wave in November gives Democrats the majority. House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries has broad support in the New York congressional district he represents and is on track for re-election in a race against Republican opponent Lewis Mizrahi that is widely considered uncompetitive. Jeffries, who served in Congress for nearly 14 years, was elected unopposed in 2022 as House Democratic Leader, succeeding Speaker Emerita Nancy Pelosi and making him the first Black leader of a major political party in Congress. But the path to the speakership isn’t guaranteed, as the center-left Jeffries may have to unite an ideologically fractious Democratic caucus. House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries (D, N.Y.) speaks to media in Washington, D.C., on Sept. 30, 2026. Finn Gomez—Getty Images Michigan Democrat Abdul El-Sayed, a former local public health director and podcaster who has never held elected public office, could become the first Muslim U.S. Senator if he beats Republican candidate Mike Rogers. El-Sayed leads, according to public opinion polling, though Michigan electoral results can be unpredictable. El-Sayed has run on a progressive platform, including advocating for Medicare for All and ending U.S. military funding to Israel, which has ruffled some moderate and Jewish Democrats. His identity has also become a campaign target for Republicans: Rogers and Donald Trump have tried to link El-Sayed and his family to the transnational extremist group Muslim Brotherhood, and El-Sayed has been pressured to reaffirm his commitment to the Constitution over his faith, which defenders have said reflects the Islamophobia he faces. “I'm not running to be the first Muslim anything,” El-Sayed told CNN in August. “I just want to be a really, really great senator for my state.” Read More: 'We're Proving It Works': Inside the DSA’s Push to Remake the Democratic Party The first openly LGBT person elected to the U.S. Senate was Sen. Tammy Baldwin (D, Wis.), who was elected in 2012, but Chris Pappas, a Democratic House member who has represented New Hampshire's 1st congressional district since 2019, could become the first out gay man serving in the U.S. Senate. Pappas, 46, married his partner, lawyer Vann Bentley, in 2023. He is also a vocal LGBT rights advocate in Congress. “I’m not thinking much about history right now,” Pappas told the New York Times in an interview. “I’m really thinking every day about the struggles of New Hampshire.” Pappas is running against Republican nominee John E. Sununu, a former U.S. Senator seeking to reclaim his seat from retiring Sen. Jeanne Shaheen. Pappas leads in public opinion polling. While Republicans have largely not made Pappas’ sexuality a focus of their political campaigns, the GOP has alleged that Pappas failed to disclose a relationship with a corporate lobbyist. Oklahoma Democratic gubernatorial nominee Cyndi Munson speaks at a campaign rally on Oct. 26, 2026. Nick Oxford—Reuters Cyndi Munson, a Democrat, was the first Asian-American woman elected to Oklahoma’s state legislature and currently serves as the lower chamber’s minority leader. She now aims to replace the term-limited Republican Gov. Kevin Stitt and become the first Korean-American governor in the U.S., though a recent poll shows her Trump-backed opponent, Republican former state senator Mike Mazzei, leading. Read More: The 7 Key Governor Races to Watch in the Midterm Elections Keisha Lance Bottoms is the Democratic nominee running against Republican billionaire Rick Jackson to replace the term-limited Georgia Republican Gov. Brian Kemp. Polls have been mixed and most forecasters have rated the race a toss-up. If Bottoms wins, she would not only become the state’s first Black and first female governor; she could also become one of the U.S.’s first Black female governors, an honor she might share with Minnesota gubernatorial candidate Lisa Demuth. Bottoms told ABC News in October that the potential milestone is “much more personal” for her. “My family goes back at least five generations in Georgia,” she said. “I’m a descendant of slaves on both sides.” Stacey Abrams, the former minority leader of Georgia’s state House, previously tried to become the first Black female governor, but fell short in the 2018 and 2022 elections. Bottoms was the mayor of Atlanta from 2018 to 2022, presiding during the COVID-19 pandemic, and shocked the state’s political establishment when she said she would not seek re-election. She has faced criticism from Jackson and others for walking away from the job. She since served in the Biden Administration in various roles, including as a senior adviser and leading the White House Office of Public Engagement. Minnesota GOP gubernatorial nominee Rep. Lisa Demuth speaks to supporters during a campaign stop in Hastings on Oct. 31, 2026. Aaron Lavinsky—The Minnesota Star Tribune/Getty Images In Minnesota, whoever wins the race for governor will break the state’s glass ceiling, as no woman has ever been elected to lead the state. Senator Amy Klobuchar is the Democratic nominee, while Republicans nominated Demuth, a state representative since 2018 and speaker of the state House since 2025. “I look forward to serving—if given the opportunity, your trust in me—as the first female governor here in the state of Minnesota and the first Black woman to serve as governor of any state in the United States,” Demuth said in her mid-October debate with Klobuchar. Polls, however, show voters leaning toward Klobuchar, as Demuth faces stiff political headwinds from being associated with Trump, who has frequently attacked Minnesota. New Mexico Democratic gubernatorial nominee Deb Haaland waves to attendees during a primary election night watch party in Albuquerque on June 2, 2026. Jon Austria—AP In 2018, Deb Haaland made history as one of the first Native American women, alongside Sharice Davids, to be elected to Congress. Haaland is a member of the Laguna Pueblo, a federally recognized tribe in New Mexico that has existed longer than the U.S. has. As a congresswoman, she focused on environmental justice, climate change, and missing and murdered indigenous women. In 2021, Haaland became the Biden Administration’s Interior Secretary. Haaland is now running to become governor of New Mexico, and she could potentially make history again, this time as one of the first Native American women to lead any state. But to do so, Haaland, a Democrat, must defeat her Republican opponent Gregg Hull, who was formerly the mayor of Rio Rancho. So far, polls show Haaland leading. In Alaska, Bernadette Wilson, who is of Aleut heritage, is a Republican candidate for governor. Wilson, a conservative activist and former radio host, has the backing of President Trump, but veteran Sen. Lisa Murkowski, a moderate Alaska Republican, and her father, former Gov. Frank Murkowski, both endorsed Wilson’s Democratic opponent, Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins, whom Wilson trails in the polls. Forecasters, however, rate the race as a toss-up. At least four Native American men have served in the U.S. Senate, but no Native American woman ever has—yet. Left: Alaska Democratic U.S. Senate candidate Mary Peltola. Right: Minnesota Democratic U.S. Senate candidate Peggy Flanagan. Mark Thiessen—AP Photo; Stephen Maturen/Getty Images When Yup'ik Alaska Native Mary Peltola was first elected to the U.S. House in an August 2022 special election to replace the late Representative Don Young, she became the first Alaska Native member of Congress and the first woman to represent Alaska in the chamber. If she beats incumbent Republican Sen. Dan S. Sullivan—and polls show her slightly leading—she could become the first Alaska Native Senator as well as one of the first Native American women in the Senate. Read More: Alaska Governor Orders Probe Into Peltola Campaign Over Alleged Spoiler Scheme In Minnesota, Democratic Lt. Gov. Peggy Flanagan is running for the Senate and could also become one of the first Native American women in the chamber. That is, if Flanagan—a member of the White Earth Band of Ojibwe, the largest federally recognized tribe in Minnesota—beats GOP nominee and political newcomer Michele Tafoya. Polls show Flanagan leading. “I’m not running to make history, but it’s about damn time there was a Native American woman serving in the United States Senate,” Flanagan said on the campaign trail in October. “And she might as well be from White Earth.”

Trump Invites Haley Stevens to Join GOP as Democrats Condemn Her ‘Betrayal’
2026-11-02

Trump Invites Haley Stevens to Join GOP as Democrats Condemn Her ‘Betrayal’

TIME | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Mike Rogers, the Republican nominee for U.S. Senate from Michigan, and Democratic Rep. Haley Stevens speak to reporters outside Perfecting Church in Detroit on Nov. 1, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump invited outgoing congresswoman Haley Stevens, a longtime Michigan Democrat, to join the Republican Party after she surprisingly endorsed her state’s GOP nominee for the U.S. Senate, Mike Rogers, in a last-minute move her partymates have called a “betrayal.” “I don’t know Haley, I’ve never met Haley,” Trump said at a rally in Iowa on Sunday, the final weekend before the Nov. 3 midterm elections. “But Haley said what few people are willing to say. I think I’d probably—I’d love her to join the Republican Party actually.” Stevens ran for the U.S. Senate seat herself but lost the Democratic primary to progressive Abdul El-Sayed in August. While she initially congratulated and offered her support to El-Sayed for the general election, the two have been at odds, primarily over concerns about antisemitism, which Stevens has accused El-Sayed, who is Muslim, of not denouncing strongly enough. On Friday, Stevens demanded that El-Sayed denounce a controversial ad that blamed Michigan’s cost-of-living issues on U.S. aid to Israel. El-Sayed, whose campaign was not behind the ad though it supported his candidacy, responded with a statement that the focus of his campaign is on the “challenges Michiganders are facing” and defeating Rogers, while Stevens “seems intent on relitigating a primary that ended three months ago.” He told reporters that he did not agree with the ad’s contents but did not want to “play the condemn game” about an unaffiliated ad. Stevens was supposed to appear with El-Sayed at a planned Democratic campaign event on Sunday, but Rep. Debbie Dingell (D, Mich.) said in an interview that before the event, she disinvited Stevens on account of her strained relationship with El-Sayed. “There’s nothing to be gained by the two of them being in the same place,” Dingell said Saturday. On Sunday, Stevens appeared instead at an event at Perfecting Church in Detroit with Rogers. “Our Democratic nominee for Senate, Abdul, said I was no longer welcome in his campaign and that I should stay away,” Stevens said in a video posted on social media later that morning. “And he’s right. And that’s why I am endorsing Mike Rogers for the United States Senate today.” On CNN on Sunday night, Stevens attempted to explain her move, saying it was because of El-Sayed’s “anger and lack of kindness.” She cited his past associations with controversial political influencer Hasan Piker and Piker’s uncle Cenk Uygur, whose Rebellion PAC was behind the recent anti-Israel ad. She insisted that she is “trying to help” the Democratic Party by turning on El-Sayed, whom she compared to Graham Platner, the former scandal-plagued Democratic nominee for U.S. Senate from Maine who withdrew from that race in July. Stevens said she remains a “really proud Democrat” who “absolutely” wants Democrats to win control of the Senate, but her shock announcement of an endorsement for the GOP candidate just days before Election Day could upend a tight race that may decide the fate of the upper chamber. Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer slammed what he called Stevens’ “despicable betrayal,” posting on social media that Senate Democrats were “united” in their support for El-Sayed. Other Democrats have also shared their dismay and disappointment. House Speaker Emerita and veteran California Democrat Rep. Nancy Pelosi said on social media that Stevens “has chosen to dangerously betray Michigan families’ healthcare, freedoms and future with her horrific endorsement of Mike Rogers.” Elissa Slotkin, Michigan’s Democratic Senator elected in 2024 and a former House colleague of Stevens from the same 2018 blue wave that saw a record number of women elected to Congress, posted on social media: “Anyone who makes an election this important about themselves, and not Michiganders and our democracy, should think hard about whether they are serving the greater good.” Ex-Rep. Susan Wild (D, Pa.), also elected in 2018 and formerly a roommate with Stevens in Washington, said in an emotional post about the Rogers endorsement that “the fallout for Haley won’t just be in Dem politics; she will be a pariah among her friends in the Democratic caucus in Congress.” Ex-Rep. Andy Levin, a Michigan Democrat who was elected alongside Stevens in 2018 but after redistricting lost to her in a 2022 primary, posted that Haley’s endorsement of Rogers was “unconscionable, beyond the pale.” Stevens’ chief of staff and multiple other staffers resigned on Sunday. “I speak on behalf of our team in wholeheartedly supporting Abdul El-Sayed for Senate,” Justin German, Stevens’ former chief of staff of eight years wrote in a statement. “This election is too important to sow division or stand on the sidelines.” Stevens’ communications director Logan Basch, appears to have also left his post: his email sent back an auto-reply to TIME that read, “Hello, as of November 1st I am no longer with the Office of Congresswoman Haley Stevens.” The Michigan Democratic Party also appeared to have distanced from Stevens, with state party chair Curtis Hertel posting on social media Sunday: “Congresswoman Stevens is not a current member of the Michigan Democratic Party, nor will she be in the future.” Republicans immediately jumped on the opportunity, which could offer a late boost amid an election year where retaining the majority in either chamber of Congress has looked unlikely. Rogers touted Stevens’ endorsement on social media, adding that she “showed courage and bravery in putting Michigan first” and that he’s “grateful for her support.” House Speaker Mike Johnson told reporters Sunday as he flanked Trump upon his arrival in Iowa that he sent Stevens a note commending “how courageous she was, how much we appreciate that she’s standing for truth.” For his part, El-Sayed, who leads Rogers in public opinion polling, has appeared unfazed by Stevens’ actions, telling reporters: “At the end of the day, I actually don’t think about Haley Stevens.”

The first and more irrelevant argument is that LLMs aren't actually thinking, their process is not like ours just because they use neural networks, which bear only a passing resemblance to how our brain might work. But the real argument is that copyright exists to protect the work of humans to induce them to make more work, so what the software is d The issue with this semantic argument is that the model weights already more or less "contain" several partial OS kernels, in the sense that you can squeeze them out as repeatable, token-identical structures with the right inference setup. When humans compete in reciting longer and longer parts of the infinitely long number pi, we don't say they "derived pi from scratch up to N places" (that's also a thing humans can do, we just typically don't compete in it, because it smells like shit that should run on a computer); we say they "memorized and recited pi up to N places", and what the LLM did with the kernel here is closer to remembering and reciting, rather than "making from scratch". Now, don't get me wrong, reciting significant parts of even vaguely functional OS kernel from memory would still be a super impressive and potentially useful skill for a human, but it's a bigger question of how useful of a skill is it for a computer. "Legacy" software can "remember" vmlinuz at cost of ~8MB of storage and mW needed for seconds to decompress it, and I'd argue that spending 11 days on building a kernel of unknown quality, unknown novelty and very limited utility is not all that useful or impressive of a function when compared with the existing ability to maintain a bit-perfect copy of the OS you already know works. The feat is being presented as impressive, except, the impressive part of it doesn't really hold up to the specific implications of "from scratch" in the context of the question. Is the new OS likely to have existing development paradigms grandfathered in? Absolutely. Is it likely to inherit some of the bugs and vulnerabilities of existing OSes? Indubitably. Is its apparent "novelty" going to make it a compelling alternative to existing, available OSes? Unlikely in the least. Was it at least less expensive or avoided usage of some limited resource that'd a compressed image of linux core would need to work? Absolutely not. Did the developer learn enough to now repeat the feat without the help of the LLM? Probably not super likely. If it has none of the practical advantages or even traits you'd expect of something made "from scratch", then perhaps it shouldn't be called that? model weights already more or less "contain" several partial OS kernels, No, they don't. The phrase "from scratch" was originally a sports analogy meaning from the starting line, like a footrace, or a facing line like in boxing. A line in chalk was scratched into the ground. They Were a Key Voting Bloc Trump Made Progress With in 2024. Republicans Have Done Everything to Reverse That.
2026-11-02

They Were a Key Voting Bloc Trump Made Progress With in 2024. Republicans Have Done Everything to Reverse That.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. In today’s GOP, it would be next to impossible to find a Republican ready to stand behind the statement “Islam is peace,” words uttered by none other than Republican former President George W. Bush just weeks after 9/11. That’s because in the subsequent 25 years, the Republican Party has decided that scapegoating the Muslim community for the country’s problems is a way to galvanize white Christian voters to elect Republicans. Nowhere is that strategy more evident than in the state of Texas. Politicians there this election cycle have been even more vicious than usual in proudly peddling Islamophobia. What these actions reveal is a deepening desperation within the Texas GOP as they face down once-in-a-lifetime political headwinds that suggest Democrats are on the verge of flipping Texas. But it’s no longer 2001, and American Muslims have been asserting more and more political clout. There’s actually a decent chance that Texas Republicans’ extreme Islamophobia backfires by alienating the one group that was shaping into a growing GOP demographic: Muslims. “It’s especially bizarre for the Republican Party to do it because it’s supposed to stand for conservative values, religious freedom, all of that. That’s not what they’re doing,” Mohamed Hussein, a self-described conservative Texan and a practicing Muslim, recently told me. While attending a GOP convention in Houston over the summer, he says, he was told to convert to Christianity or leave the country. “It was shocking, because I wasn’t at a religious convention, to be told to convert. It kind of came out of left field,” Hussein said. He attended a Judeo-Christian seminar where attendees were told that non-Christian immigrants will create problems for America and that Muslims were lying to win political power. This was a version of the Republican Party that Hussein did not recognize; “I don’t think that had this convention occurred a year ago, it would have been this nasty toward Islam and Muslims.” Clearly, a lot has changed since 2025. Americans went from having a Democratic president to a Republican one, suddenly finding themselves at war with Iran, facing record high gas and diesel prices, skyrocketing healthcare costs, and immigration agents knocking down their doors. It has made the 2026 midterms an especially consequential election cycle, and in Texas, Republicans have decided Muslims could be their ticket out of the backlash to the mess created by President Donald Trump. As I am an American Muslim myself, and have plenty of family and friends in Texas, it is a phenomenon I was curious to explore. So I took a trip to Houston, home to one of the country’s largest Muslim populations, to see what it’s like for Muslim Texans, especially those who may have voted GOP in years past but were now looking at a party they didn’t recognize. Though Muslims make up only about 1 percent of Texas’ total population—roughly 300,000 people—the state is home to the fourth-largest population of Muslims within the U.S. and the largest in the South. And Islam has long been considered one of the fastest-growing religions in the country, with Pew Research estimating that by 2040, Muslims will replace Jews as the nation’s second-largest religious group after Christians. By 2050, Muslims are projected to account for 2 percent of the total U.S. population, compared to the 1 percent they currently represent. Muslims’ growth is especially pronounced in Texas, where the Muslim voter population grew 35 percent from 2016 to 2020, which translated to an additional 80,000 registered voters, according to the Muslim civics group Emgage. Yet, state Republicans, this cycle at least, seem not to want anything to do with these voters. Since at least last year, they have been aggressively inciting anti-Muslim rhetoric and policies, starting at the top with Gov. Greg Abbott, who is currently locked in a surprisingly tight race for reelection. He went to war with EPIC City, an Islamic development project that sought to build a K–12 school, a mosque, retail space, and housing units, and it took over local headlines. Abbott went so far as to sign a law banning EPIC City from moving forward, characterizing the developers as “bad actors” who would subject occupants to “Sharia law,” whatever his definition of that might be. His fellow Republicans were taking notes. Soon enough, Attorney General Ken Paxton, the Republican nominee for a Texas Senate seat who’s also locked in a tight race—the contest, against Democrat James Talarico, could determine control of the U.S. Senate—followed up with a lawsuit against EPIC City, while Texas Rep. Keith Self also called on the Justice Department and IRS to investigate the developers. Then Abbott struck again, this time sabotaging plans for a celebration of Eid, one of the holiest Islamic holidays celebrating the end of 30 days of fasting, that was supposed to take place at a local water park—a venue that the community had used for the past two years with no issue. Suddenly, Abbott was threatening to pull more than half a million dollars in state funds from the city of Grand Prairie if they allowed the Eid swim party to proceed. It worked, and the city canceled the Eid celebration. Soon, the slogan “Don’t Sharia My Texas” was taking over the state, inspired by an anti-Muslim propaganda film that features conspiracy theorist Alex Jones and right-wing former national security adviser Michael Flynn. Sharia is a set of religious and worldly values for Muslims to follow and is entirely separate from Islamic law, though Republicans have rarely—if ever— attempted to explain what it is they are fearmongering against. “Like racism, Islamophobia can be adapted to align with the political and economic interests of those in power,” Khaled Beydoun, a professor of law at Arizona State University who studies Islamophobia, told the Dallas Morning News. Islamophobia may have hit its peak in America immediately following 9/11. President Bush declared a “war on terror,” and, again, failed to clearly define terrorism, which made the concept borderless and timeless, as explained by the Bridge Initiative at Georgetown University. This oversimplification created two sides: terrorists vs. everyone else, with Muslim Americans becoming targets of both the state and the general population. This period gave way to controversial policies like the National Security Entry-Exit Registration System, the Terrorism Screening Database, and even the establishment of Guantánamo Bay. It would take decades for the public to learn that Bush’s wars were utter failures, and slowly but surely it became normalized to publicly criticize American foreign policy. Eventually, Muslim Americans were empowered to be a part of America’s shifting governance, while also changing the narrative around Islam. Voters began electing Muslim candidates to Washington, and today Michigan Rep. Rashida Tlaib, Minnesota Rep. Ilhan Omar, and New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani are some of the most recognizable political figures in the country, and are also practicing Muslims. In Michigan, Abdul El-Sayed is hoping to make history as the first Muslim American to serve in the U.S. Senate (while also facing perhaps the most bigoted campaign in recent memory). Notably, each of these figures are Democrats, as their party has largely embraced a diverse coalition of voters. Meanwhile, Republicans have decidedly gone the opposite direction, reinvigorating an especially pernicious element of the culture wars of the early aughts. In fact, it was Texas Rep. Chip Roy who cofounded Congress’ “Sharia-Free America Caucus.” He’s also running in a competitive race for Texas AG, and in the process “has posted from both his campaign and official accounts about Muslims, Islam, or ‘sharia law’ more than 244 times since January—the most of any member of Congress,” according to a Washington Post analysis. In the run-up to Texas’ March primary, blatantly anti-Muslim ads were running rampant across the state on behalf of Republican campaigns that baselessly tied Islam with terrorism and crime. All of it was considered “solid gold” by one Republican strategist, who told CNN: “It is playing as well as anything I have ever seen with Texas Republican voters.” Why would overt bigotry that pits fellow Texans against one another be resonating with voters? Perhaps because Republicans themselves have spent decades perpetuating the very Islamophobia the rest of America has been trying to heal. Equality Labs, a South Asian–focused civil rights group, found that Texas led the country in promoting Islamophobia-related disinformation in 2025, largely driven by online posts suggesting that Muslims were invading the U.S. and attempting to impose Sharia law. Consider Bo French, a longtime far-right activist who’s currently running for Texas Railroad Commissioner. In September, he characterized a photo of a group of South Asian University of Texas students celebrating the school’s football team’s win: “Like body-cams exposed criminals, social media is exposing how many Americans have been displaced by foreigners. It’s everywhere and far worse than any of us could imagine.” French’s comment quickly went viral and ignited a national conversation about blatant bigotry against minorities. Equality Labs also found that nationwide, Republicans are largely responsible for spreading Islamophobic content, as opposed to Democrats and independents. This phenomenon is particularly insidious in states, like Texas, where Republicans hold a supermajority, because “the primary electorate becomes the decisive contest, and candidates have to appeal only to a very small sliver of their own electorate,” Brandon Rottinghaus, a political scientist at the University of Houston, told the New York Times. All of this is having a discernible impact nationwide, as Pew Research found that U.S. adults’ attitudes toward Islam have worsened since 9/11. In 2002, six months after the al-Qaida attacks, 25 percent of U.S. adults said Islam is more likely than other religions to encourage violence, but when asked the same question in 2026, that figure had more than doubled to 51 percent. Republicans and independents who lean toward the GOP were far more likely than Democrats and Democratic leaners to say that Islam encourages violence—76 percent vs. 29 percent. Particularly in Texas, this puts hundreds of thousands of Muslims in an untenable position. People like Hussein are actively being disenfranchised by a political party whose conservative values they typically align with. Khalid Hamideh, a spokesman for the Islamic Association of North Texas, told me: “We’re more aligned with the values of the Republican Party when it comes to abortion, same-sex marriage—on all of the social issues, we’re very conservative. We’re very aligned with the people that hate us most.” As they’re left with no other option, Hamideh believes that the recent GOP attacks on Texas Muslims have pushed many toward the Democratic Party unwillingly. Jida Nabulsi also holds conservative values but doesn’t identify herself as either a Democrat or a Republican. “You could say that I would have aligned more with the Republican Party, but given the current state of things, there’s just no sympathy from the Muslim community,” she told me. Nabulsi is the Texas state director for Emgage, a group that has been supporting Talarico’s candidacy. She believes there are plenty of Muslim conservatives in Texas who share her perspective. “The conversations I’m having with my community and other community members, they are telling me that even though they identify with Republicans, nobody should be voting Republican. Nobody should be aligning with that.” Because they are obsessed with perpetuating anti-Muslim hate, Nabulsi believes, Texas state lawmakers are forgoing talking about the actual issues voters care about. “Their top priority as a group is not affordability, bringing down prices, or making life easier for us, but to target one community,” she said. “It does not make sense.” Democrats aren’t necessarily viewed more positively than Republicans right now, but Nabulsi believes they’re at least signaling an open line of communication with a message she interprets as “We are not here with hate.” Just a few weeks ago, Talarico’s campaign met with local Muslim officials and community members in Dallas, where he emphasized a bill he filed that would include Muslim imams as legally recognized marriage officiants. One October poll found Talarico leading Paxton by nearly 17 points among Asian American and Pacific Islander voters, though it’s challenging to carve out Muslim voters here, as they can fall under the listed “Asian,” “South Asian,” “Middle Eastern,” and “African” ethnic groups, all of which also include other religions. Nonetheless, getting Texas Muslims on board could be the windfall Talarico needs to get him over the line against Paxton. Given the serious risk Texas Republicans face right now of losing their ruby-red grip over their state, it might make strategic sense for them to widen their tent, particularly to voters who are already inclined to support them. Muslims are especially primed for the taking, as they’ve increased their voting power over the past 10 years. The Institute for Social Policy and Understanding found that in 2016, 60 percent of eligible American Muslims reported being registered to vote, and by 2025 that figure had jumped up to 85 percent. And since 2024, Muslims have been shifting away from Democrats over their handling of Israel’s war in Gaza, which culminated in tangible action with the “uncommitted” movement, which galvanized voters who were unhappy with the Biden administration’s Israel policy to refrain from voting for either Joe Biden or Kamala Harris. Plenty of ink has been spilled over whether the Biden administration’s Israel policy cost it the 2024 election, but the data suggests that there has been a clear shift. Exit polling conducted by the Council on American-Islamic Relations, the largest Muslim civil rights group, found in 2020 that Biden earned 69 percent of the Muslim vote, compared to Trump’s 17 percent. Four years later in 2024, Harris earned only 20 percent, while Trump received 21 percent and third-party candidate Jill Stein notched 53 percent. That is a lot of voters ripe for the taking. The shift in Muslim voters’ politics is comparable to that of America’s Hispanic electorate, which was considered solidly Democratic until Trump arrived. In 2024, he captured 42 percent of the Latino vote as discourse around immigration doomed the Biden and Harris campaigns. Ironically, Latinos now may swing back to Democrats in 2026, thanks to Trump’s deeply unpopular mass-deportation agenda. The current vitriolic rhetoric being spewed in Texas by Republicans could similarly push Muslims right into Democratic hands. This doesn’t surprise Hussein, who senses that Texas Republicans’ true loyalty always goes back to white Christian nationalist voters. “I think it probably appeals to a portion of the base. I think it’s also a distraction from other things that matter to Texans,” he told me. “It’s a reliable boogeyman to blame Muslims.” This time around, they may just be shooting themselves in the foot. Haley Stevens Just Did Something Unforgivable. But She’s Not the Only One Responsible for Democrats’ Michigan Mess.
2026-11-02

Haley Stevens Just Did Something Unforgivable. But She’s Not the Only One Responsible for Democrats’ Michigan Mess.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The first thing to know about the Democrat-on-Democrat catastrophe engulfing the Michigan Senate election is that it didn’t have to happen. The second thing to know is that there’s a lot of blame to go around. On Sunday, Rep. Haley Stevens pulled a heel turn for the ages. Stevens, a pro-Israel centrist who narrowly lost the Democratic primary to progressive Abdul El-Sayed, was slated to appear at a “unity rally” on the last weekend before Election Day. Instead, she pulled out—and went to church with El-Sayed’s Republican opponent, former Rep. Mike Rogers. A formal Rogers endorsement followed, completing the betrayal. Now, in a tight race that’s crucial to Democrats’ hopes of retaking the Senate and grinding Donald Trump’s agenda to a halt, a candidate who got almost half of the primary vote is supporting the Republican. There are a few ways in which this disaster could’ve been avoided. If Democratic Sen. Gary Peters—who in congressional terms is but a 67-year-old whippersnapper—had sought reelection, the messy primary that set up Stevens’ about-face never would’ve happened. The same thing is probably true had either Michigan Gov. Gretchen Whitmer or former Transportation Secretary Pete Buttigieg, who lives in Traverse City, Michigan, decided to run for Peters’ seat. Once they passed, the state’s Democratic primary voters could have picked a compromise candidate in Mallory McMorrow, a (now-proven) team player who is more centrist than El-Sayed and more progressive than Stevens. After McMorrow dropped out, Stevens and El-Sayed could have pulled their punches instead of sparring nastily over the Israeli government’s actions in Gaza and Stevens’ support from AIPAC, the pro-Israel lobbying group. And once the primary ended, the pair could have buried the hatchet permanently rather than letting the tensions simmer into the general election. In short, everything in the race has broken toward conflict rather than comity. Michigan wasn’t the only bare-knuckled race in a primary season that served as an extended proxy fight between the Democratic Party’s center-left establishment and its progressive insurgency. The Senate contest in Florida, several House races in New York, and the gubernatorial primary in Wisconsin all got heated. A few other primaries even yielded sore losers; in Texas, Rep. Jasmine Crockett has refused to campaign with fellow Democrat James Talarico after he beat her. Yet even Crockett says she “WOULDN’T, EVER” endorse Talarico’s Republican opponent, so something has clearly gone uniquely sideways in Michigan. What went wrong? Who’s to blame? And is anyone entirely innocent? Stevens is obviously the headline villain, the architect of a hard-to-find-precedent-for defection that is indefensible on both substance and timing. But the difficult reality is that the blame doesn’t rest with her alone. At the heart of this disaster is a spectacularly poor match of political egos, an issue that promotes intransigence over compromise, and, in El-Sayed, a nominee who failed a critical test. Let’s start with the person who’s most at fault for Sunday’s explosion: the one who actually pulled the pin out of the grenade. Despite continuing to describe herself as “a very proud Democrat,” Stevens has arguably done more damage to her party’s nominee than Republicans have. It’s certainly possible that El-Sayed will win, and that Democrats could retake the Senate even if he doesn’t. But winning Michigan in what’s shaping up to be a blue-wave election shouldn’t be this hard, and retaking the Senate without it would require Democrats to vastly overperform in much more Republican-friendly states. But Stevens has betrayed more than just her party: She’d previously argued that a Rogers win would imperil a suite of issues she professed to care about. After losing the primary, she full-throatedly endorsed El-Sayed and argued that Rogers would “keep rubber-stamping Donald Trump’s harmful agenda of higher prices, manufacturing jobs lost nationwide, corruption and abuses of power” while helping “ban abortion, gut health care, and roll back workers’ rights and voting rights.” The only thing that’s changed since then is the candidate Stevens supports. Then there’s the matter of her rationale. Stevens’ defection followed a war of words over a pro-El-Sayed ad, which argued that groceries have become unaffordable because the U.S. sends weapons to Israel. Stevens criticized the ad as antisemitic and demanded El-Sayed condemn it. (More on that in a moment.) But if bigotry were really her concern, she wouldn’t be throwing in with Rogers, a candidate who built his campaign around Islamophobia. Rogers has accused El-Sayed of adhering to a “radical Islamic agenda” and of backing the Muslim Brotherhood, an extremist group the Democrat has repeatedly denounced. Stevens is also, by her own prior admission, helping a president who mocks El-Sayed’s name (“To me, he’s Mohammed, and he’s a radical jihadist,” Trump said last month) and fearmongers that his victory would “invite hordes of jihadis to our shores, creating a nightmare of violence and terror until you cannot even have a Christmas.” On Sunday, Stevens characterized her flip-flop as “a personal and private decision” in the middle of a press conference talking about it, which made the whole thing look more petty than principled. His culpability is more complicated, but it really does take two to tango. Stevens chose to pull her switcheroo, yet El-Sayed has done a bunch of tactically questionable things that seem to have made that outcome more likely—and could make it more politically damaging, too. One of those questionable choices has been to associate himself with controversial figures. During the primary, El-Sayed campaigned with Hasan Piker, a left-wing streamer who has said that the U.S. “deserved 9/11”. El-Sayed spent months refusing to distance himself from Piker, deriding questions about their joint appearances as “gotcha politics.” And although El-Sayed ultimately did condemn those remarks and others (including Piker’s claim that Jewish Americans, or Jewish American institutions, being too pro-Israel could lead people to “take action” against them), some Democrats already had concerns about El-Sayed’s approach to antisemitism. In March, El-Sayed responded to an attack against a Detroit-area synagogue by saying that “hurt people hurt people,” which sounded to some like a justification for the violence. (El-Sayed later apologized.) “I’m just sick of the people he stands with,” Stevens told Anderson Cooper on CNN on Sunday night. Self-defeating prickliness also seems to have shaped El-Sayed’s response to the ad Stevens criticized before endorsing Rogers. It’s true that the ad didn’t come from El-Sayed’s campaign; a left-wing commentator named Cenk Uygur, who happens to be Piker’s uncle, posted it online. But even as he distanced himself, El-Sayed also released a statement accusing Stevens of “relitigating” their primary that pointedly noted her millions in AIPAC support and disinvited Stevens from Sunday’s unity appearance, which national Democrats helped broker in a bid to squash their beef. El-Sayed’s defensiveness, as he runs to be the nation’s first Muslim senator, is understandable. His opponent has put El-Sayed’s religion and ethnicity at the center of his attacks, yet El-Sayed is the one who’s continually asked to disavow bigotry. It must be exhausting. And it’s a challenge for a candidate whose rise was fueled by a combative approach to entrenched power, an approach many of his supporters appreciate. But politics is often unfair and rarely easy, and the job of the candidate is to win. That’s particularly true in an election where Democrats have repeatedly (and accurately) told supporters that their would-be Senate takeover is an essential bulwark against Trump’s authoritarian impulses. In that context, it’s not unfair for Democrats to ask El-Sayed to make sacrifices in the name of winning—and it’s not always clear that he has been willing to. Before the August primary, El-Sayed predicted a double-digit victory against Stevens, only to end up winning by less than a point. A nominee laser-focused on the general election might have been chastened, hastening to court the 47.6 percent of Democrats who’d backed his opponent. Yet even now, polls suggest El-Sayed is leaving votes on the table. He currently leads Rogers by an average of 5 points, while the Democratic nominee for Michigan governor leads her Republican opponent by almost 9. As my colleague Aymann Ismail has reported, El-Sayed has struggled to convince some core Democratic constituencies, including Black Michiganders, that he will improve their lives. Of course, it’s possible that nothing could have kept Stevens inside the tent. Some of her criticisms—like going on CNN to accuse El-Sayed of getting “angrier and angrier”—aren’t things politicians say if they’re open to appeasement. Stevens has also refused to explain her apparently spur-of-the-moment decision to switch sides; as of Saturday, she was still telling fellow Democrats that she supported El-Sayed. Maybe getting engaged to a Democratic donor who also recently pledged to vote for Rogers caused a (literal) change of heart. Maybe Stevens snapped after spending another week butting heads with the rival who foiled her Senate dreams. Or maybe she’s been promised a job after she leaves Congress in January in exchange for changing jerseys. (On Sunday, Trump praised Stevens’ “bravery,” and House Speaker Mike Johnson said he’d encouraged her to “keep speaking the truth.”) The losers of competitive primaries—and their supporters—always face pressure to back their party’s nominee, and lately that pressure has fallen hardest on the left. During the previous three presidential elections, more moderate Democrats urged progressives to shelve their objections and get on board with Hillary Clinton, Joe Biden, and Kamala Harris in the interest of beating Trump. Stevens’ desertion isn’t a great sign that the party’s more centrist losers are willing to return the favor. Still, many moderate Democrats—including erstwhile Stevens supporters, congressional staffers, and House colleagues—have condemned the Rogers endorsement, meaning Stevens’ exit may not bring many voters along. So far, El-Sayed has chosen to downplay it. “This race was never about me. It was never even about Mike Rogers. It’s certainly not about Haley Stevens,” he said on MS NOW on Sunday night. “This race is about the people in the state of Michigan, the people that I’ve gotten to get to know in their churches and synagogues, in their basements, in their living rooms, talking about what they need and deserve.” Yet if there’s any race in which the “vote blue no matter who” calculus could flop, it’s this one. The tension at the heart of the primary—and the feud between El-Sayed and Stevens—is less a policy disagreement than a moral one. Debates over healthcare have a moral valence, but there’s room to compromise between, say, expanding Obamacare and passing “Medicare for All.” By contrast, there isn’t much of a middle ground between thinking Israel is committing genocide in Gaza (as El-Sayed does) and believing that it isn’t (per Stevens). It’s certainly Stevens’ right to decide that her support for Israel outweighs her desire to see a Democrat win her state’s Senate race, just as it’s El-Sayed’s right to say good riddance to a Democrat who feels that way. But part of the job of being a party’s nominee is uniting as many of that party’s supporters behind you as possible. The reasons for El-Sayed’s failure to do that may be a matter of debate, but he will bear the consequences all the same. On Tuesday, it could cost his entire party.
