News and Politics - Slate Magazine
It’s a Race Between a Pop Star and a MAGA Die-Hard. It’s Also a Measure of the Most-Important Swing Voters in America.
2026-11-02

It’s a Race Between a Pop Star and a MAGA Die-Hard. It’s Also a Measure of the Most-Important Swing Voters in America.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The Rio Grande Valley is not like anywhere else in the United States of America. This isn’t to say that there aren’t similarities. The clean subdivisions springing up around McAllen could be in any prosperous suburb in Florida or Arizona. The abject poverty in a rural colonia could be something out of the most dire hollow in Appalachia. But taken as a whole, this stretch of South Texas, starting at the mouth of the Rio Grande and stretching over a hundred miles along its north bank is just different. It has its own culture, its own music, and its own fast-food chains. Spanish is as commonly heard as English in a region where over 90 percent of residents are Mexican American. But it also is still fundamentally rooted in American culture as well. Like any other Texans, residents still gather for high school football on Friday night and scarf chicken wings while watching the Dallas Cowboys on Sunday. (In Donna, Texas, the logo of the Donna Redskins is proudly emblazoned on the town water tower even if it’s considered problematic on the Potomac). The politics of the Rio Grande Valley are also different. Even as much of the rest of Texas shifted toward the GOP in the past half-century, the Rio Grande Valley remained loyal to the Democratic Party. From local offices up to the top of the ticket, it remained a Democratic bastion. At least until Donald Trump appeared on the scene. It wasn’t immediate. Unlike the voters at Rust Belt diners who consumed the national imagination (or at least those of editors on national desks) after the 2016 election, the Rio Grande Valley didn’t really shift to the right in the race between Donald Trump and Hillary Clinton. However, first in 2020 and then in 2024, the valley’s voters veered hard toward the GOP. Perhaps the most stunning political transformation happened in Starr County, a deeply impoverished county that is the most heavily Hispanic jurisdiction in the country. Hillary Clinton won nearly 80 percent of the vote there in 2016. Eight years later, Kamala Harris won the support of just under 42 percent of voters. Republicans acted like the shift was permanent. They promptly passed a mid-decade redistricting that was designed to shift five seats in Texas to their column. Their hope was that the lone Republican representing a Rio Grande Valley district, Monica De La Cruz, would soon be joined by two others members of her party, who would unseat longtime Democratic incumbents Henry Cuellar and Vicente Gonzalez. It turned out that they were overambitious. It’s not just continued economic woes that have plagued Republicans across the country this cycle. It’s a fundamental miscalculation by the Trump administration on its signature immigration policy. Just because voters were fed up with Joe Biden’s lax border policy, it didn’t mean they were willing to embrace the brutal and indiscriminate tactics of Border Patrol commander Greg Bovino either. For many of these voters, 2024 was a rejection of a Democratic Party that was more interested in catering to white Brooklyn progressives than Hispanics on the border in Progreso, Texas. But it turns out they are not particularly happy with the Republican Party of 2026 either. The resulting backlash could not just cost the GOP a plethora of races in Texas; it could arrest, or reverse, a nascent political realignment in the Rio Grande Valley. Three weeks before Election Day, Speaker Mike Johnson came to McAllen, Texas, to rally Republican faithful in a hotel ballroom only a few miles from the Mexican border. There was an open bar with beer and wine, chicken wings, and burgers for the crowd of over 100. They’d come to see Johnson appear with De La Cruz and two Republican challengers: Tano Tijerina, a much vaunted recruit running against Cuellar, and Eric Flores, the GOP hope for unseating Gonzalez. On the way in, it was hard to avoid free swag from the De La Cruz campaign, which gave out baseball hats, T-shirts, and pens. The crowd appeared to be roughly evenly divided between Anglos and Hispanics in an area where Hispanics make up an overwhelming majority of population. And the message they heard was overwhelmingly clear. The polls were all wrong. Johnson took the stage telling the crowd, “The polls say that these guys aren’t going to win. That’s not true, is it?” to loud noes from the loyal Republicans in the room. He added, “Do not believe the polls right now. I’m telling you, they’re wrong. They’re wrong.” This was weak tea compared with De La Cruz who told the hollering crowd, “I don’t care what the polls say because my God is bigger.” The next day, congressional Republicans pulled their funding for Tijerina, effectively giving up on the race against Cuellar. Republicans are struggling in most races this year across the country, but what is happening in the Rio Grande Valley is, like the region itself, unique. It’s also exemplified by the Democratic standard bearer against De La Cruz, Bobby Pulido. A 53-year-old Tejano singer who had a number of hits in the genre in the 1990s, Pulido was one of the most prized recruits for Democrats this year. He’s running in a district that Donald Trump won by 18 points in 2024 and has since been further gerrymandered to stretch from the border over 250 miles north into the rural areas between Houston and San Antonio. However, the bulk of its population is in the Rio Grande Valley, and both candidates live there. Pulido’s celebrity gives him instant universal name ID in the district. It also has been the basis for much of his campaign. After De La Cruz gibed that the race “isn’t about who you want performing at your niece’s quinceañera,” the Democrat said he was open to playing anyone’s quinceañera in the district. Thousands of invitations rolled in for a singer whose hit song “Desvelado” can still be sung by generations of Tejanos in the Rio Grande Valley. However, there is a dark side to the celebrity. De La Cruz has repeatedly attacked Pulido for problematic social media posts across his career, as well as his association with Frankie Caballero, the singer’s former accordionist who is a registered sex offender for indecent contact with a minor. This has been one of the centerpieces of her campaign. After all, who could ever imagine anyone in the Rio Grande Valley supporting a problematic celebrity? De La Cruz’s campaign office is a former restaurant space on a state highway in Weslaco, a sprawling town just east of McAllen. The entrance of the dingy space includes a display of “Faces of South Texas,” which includes Selena, Nolan Ryan, Tom Landry, and some war heroes. She’s having an event for voters to pick up campaign signs, and about 50 people have crowded in to see their congresswoman and pick up her swag at 5:30 on a Thursday afternoon. Burgers are available, with accompanying plastic bags that include lettuce, tomato, and pickle, to allow attendees to individually dress their sandwiches. There are coolers of generic cola and name-brand beer. The older crowd mostly avails themselves of the first and not the second.Next to the congresswoman, the star attendee is a student at Sam Houston Elementary School who dressed up for school as her role model, who happened to be Monica De La Cruz. Dressed in a white blazer and red skirt, she recites De La Cruz’s biography as the real congressman looks on with a glowing smile. When the student is done, De La Cruz hugs her and presents her with an American flag pin and a personalized Monica De La Cruz challenge coin. Before the attendees grabbed their signs, which included standard yard signs as well as pink-shaded Spanish-language signs saying, “Mujeres Para Monica,” they could stand in a photo line for a picture with the congresswoman. Bruce Springsteen’s “Atlantic City” blared in the background. The room appeared to be entirely Hispanic. It included an older woman who spoke broken English but made clear her disdain for immigrants who lack permanent legal status. Also present were Luis Rosa and Luisa Collin, a couple who praised De La Cruz for her constituent services. Rosa had been a Republican and had brought over Collin, who had previously described herself as “a Democrat by tradition” and now was a fervent conservative listening to right-wing podcasts. They both were loyal Trump supporters. “We love him,” said Collin. De La Cruz, though, needs to appeal to more than Trump die-hards to win reelection. Speaking to Slate in her office the day before, she often deflected criticism of administration policies on key issues—noting that she raised concern with key stakeholders. “I had those tough conversations with the administration,” she said about Trump’s immigration policies. “ICE enforcement should focus on the criminals and on the cartels, not hardworking immigrant families trying to navigate this extremely complex immigration process.” However, she acknowledged, “the reality is the White House is the one that dictates enforcement.” She also touted various bills for immigration reform that she had introduced and backed in the House, even though none have, or will, receive a vote in a Republican-controlled Congress. The incumbent waived off criticism that she had not backed a discharge petition on an immigration reform bill that was one signature away from forcing a floor vote on Capitol Hill. “Never in the history of America has true immigration reform been done through a discharge petition,” she said. (A discharge position famously resulted in a vote on the release of the Epstein files earlier this year.) It was the same on Trump’s trade policy, which is particularly resonant in a district on the border. “It’s a discussion that I’m a part of with the trade ambassador, Ambassador Greer, and the White House. I’m going to keep having these conversations as we continue the trade policies,” she said, as she insisted businesses in the district were benefiting from Trump’s tax cuts regardless of any issues with tariffs. She was even reluctant to notably distance herself from the president on easy issues. When asked after the Johnson event about Trump’s decision to buy diesel fuel from Russia, which was denounced by a number of elected Republicans, she dodged. In her view, “I’m going to tell you that the president is thinking outside the box, and sometimes we’ve got to think outside the box to get something done.” But as to whether doing business with Vladimir Putin was not just outside the box but beyond the pale wasn’t a question she wasn’t willing to answer when pressed. De La Cruz’s contempt for her opponent, though, was not disguised. “He’s a silver-spooned celebrity that has never held a job in his life. He had a couple of nice hits back in the ’90s, but he’s a show horse.” She viewed him as a product of the “compadre system,” the political machines that once dominated South Texas politics. She tied Pulido’s past employment of the pedophile accordionist, Frankie Caballero to his political pull as “the product of one of the most corrupt political families in this area. … His uncle was the DA, which let this child sex offender out of jail. Basically, gave him a get out of jail free card, put him back on the streets to molest children.” Both Pulido and his uncle have denied that Caballero received any special treatment, and the Democratic candidate has insisted that he never knew that his accordionist was a registered sex offender when they performed together at a local middle school. De La Cruz isn’t distancing herself from other Texas Republican candidates, including some of the least popular. A number of prominent Republicans in Texas have made clear that they will not vote for the party’s Senate nominee, Ken Paxton, who has been plagued with scandals, or Bo French, the GOP’s candidate for railroad commissioner, who has a long history of racist posts online. “It’s important that people go all the way down ballot,” she said. “I would pray that they go all the way down ballot and that they vote Republican.” Pulido wasn’t just playing quinceañeras on the campaign trail. Less than three weeks before the election, he held a concert at a local arena with around 8,000 people. It wasn’t a campaign event; it was his farewell show. “La Última Desvelada” as he billed it, a reference to his biggest hit song. Attendees came from all over Texas and were motivated far more by Pulido’s celebrity than his candidacy. Amy Gayosso drove eight hours from Nacogdoches, near the Louisiana border, for the concert. She and her brother grew up loving Pulido’s music. He took his own life, and for her, going to the concert was a way to honor her late brother’s memory. There were long lines both for Pulido T-shirts that cost up to $55 and for beer and cocktails at concession stands throughout the arena. Politics was almost entirely absent from the concert until right before the last song, when Pulido spoke, reading off a teleprompter buried by the stage lights. He not only thanked his family and his friends for their support throughout his music career but gave a stump speech pitched for a nonpolitical audience. “We’ve forgotten that we’re neighbors, and that we’re family, and that we’re supposed to care and have each other’s backs. I don’t care whether you’re a Democrat or Republican. We’re fed up with both parties. I’m not team red. I’m not team blue. I’m team you.” At a rally the night before, Pulido struck a different tone. It was in a vacant industrial building out in the country where cars parked the grass, kids played in a bounce castle, and their parents watched while taking advantage of a well-stocked open bar. It felt almost as if Chuck E. Cheese had replaced its animatronic band with several real-life Tejano ones. Pulido said of De La Cruz, “She’s not a good representative. She’s my representative in Edinburg and she’s terrible. And I’m going to tell you why: Because she’s a puppet for Donald Trump.” Pulido went on to further deride his opponent as a partisan shill. “Monica has not made a case for herself. Her case is to try to say, ‘Vote for me because I’m a Republican.’ And I’m telling you, ‘Don’t vote for me because I’m a Democrat. I want to earn your vote. Vote for me because I’ll do what’s right in your interest, and I don’t care about the party.’ ” Speaking to Slate at a juice bar near his house, Pulido kept up his combative tone. He was wearing a campaign button-down shirt with his name emblazoned on it while sipping a cup of matcha (to protect his vocal chords, the two-time Latin Grammy Award–winning singer doesn’t drink coffee). Pulido scoffed at the narrative that South Texas had gone Republican. “No, it didn’t,” he argued. “There’s no elected Republicans here countywide—zero, not one, and there never has been.” Democrats consistently win local races with well over 60 percent of the vote. Instead, he said that the Democratic Party at the federal level had not simply been running the right kind of campaign in the Rio Grande Valley. “Recently when they’ve run congressional campaigns here, that number goes down to 55 percent. So it’s like 7 or 8 points difference when you don’t run a retail campaign and you just put up signs and put up commercials. It’s not how you run it here,” he said. “The saying ‘all politics is local’ really rings true.” However, he made clear that running as a leftist wouldn’t cut it. To succeed as a Democrat, in Pulido’s view, “you’ve got to run on your values and conservative values.” “Believe it or not, we’re all pretty much Blue Dog Democrats here,” he said. “There’s tons of conservative people that are still Democrats. They absolutely aren’t socialists.” Pulido noted that Republicans “always paint every single Democrat as the absolute leftist person, Starbucks barista, with pink hair, and a nose ring. That’s what they want everybody to think that the Democrats are. … They paint this caricature of what a Democrat is, and I’m here to tell them and show them that that’s not the case. I wear boots and a cowboy hat, right? It’s not a fucking costume for me.” In his view, national Democrats abandoned the area in every possible way. “They stopped investing. They took this area for granted. They thought it’d always be Democrat. They got really heavily focused on social justice and other issues, and not so much on economics. They failed on the border, and the Biden administration was terrible. And more importantly, like, it goes back to the economic message, I think that’s the biggest one. You can’t run on a platform down here attacking the rich when everybody here wants to be rich and aspires to be rich.” When asked if Kamala Harris should run again, Pulido said, “It’s a free country “ He added, “I think it’d be a tough sell here, but that’s just my own personal opinion.” He had particular scorn for De La Cruz’s attacks on him and her claims that he was the product of the compadre system.  “You know who says that? People with no friends say that.” Pulido’s voice dripped with disdain as he simply asked rhetorically, “What are you talking about? You have that fucking picture with Trump enriching himself, and now you’re the fucking—you’re the like the anti-corruption person. Like, whatever. The guy made $2 billion on fucking cryptocurrency and trading, and he’s traded 10 billion times more than Nancy Pelosi has. Yeah, and all of a sudden you’re like this anti-corruption?” The stakes in this race though aren’t just about one out of 435 seats. It becomes a big indicator of how real the political realignment is among Latino voters. After all, East Texas was once filled with socially conservative voters who stayed loyal to Democrats out of habit, tradition, and economic populism. It’s now blood red. If the Rio Grande Valley follows the same trends, it would doom Democrats’ hopes of winning the Lone Star State for another generation. This has meant the region has become one of the crucial battlefields not just for control of Congress but control of the Senate. Both the campaigns of Democratic state Rep. James Talarico and Republican state Attorney General Ken Paxton have invested in the region. Democrats need not just win here but win decisively to have a path statewide in Texas. It means that national groups are playing in the Rio Grande Valley as well. Somos PAC, a progressive group that seeks to boost Latino turnout held an event in the parking lot of Pulido’s final concert. There were musical performances, free food, and a chance to win tickets to the sold-out show. Melissa Morales, the head of Somos, said the event was part of a wide-ranging effort to convince Latino voters to support Democrats this year after they broke so heavily for Trump in the previous election. “I think anybody who tells me that the shifts in Latino voters weren’t real or didn’t exist in 2024 is kidding themselves,” Morales said. “I like to call Latinos the last real swing voters. They really could have gone either way, which is why we’re talking to soft Republicans.” These are the voters like the parents of Fred Del Barrio, the Republican mayor of Palmhurst and a prominent businessman in the Rio Grande Valley. “They’ve become more conservative, and the older people, a lot of them have changed. They see the values of the Republican Party. They see the conservative values, which I think a lot of us have and the influence of [Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez], those very left-leaning, extreme left Democrats. I don’t think they fit in here.” Certainly Pulido couldn’t be mistaken for a socialist, nor could Cuellar or Gonzalez. (Though Republicans were trying, and billboards saying Gonzalez supported taxpayer-funded sex changes for minors were ubiquitous throughout South Texas.) But what matters in 2026 isn’t so much the flaws of the Democratic Party, it’s frustration with Trump’s GOP. Tariffs have walloped a region where cross-border trade is a regular institution in daily life, and the abuses of ICE have caused a serious backlash among a Latino community that was aghast at Biden’s lax border policies but are just as upset, if not more so, at Trump’s overreaction that has led to separated families and racial profiling in South Texas. Trump has squandered many of the gains that Republicans made here only two years ago, but then again, even Democrats are ceding generational support in the Rio Grande Valley. Pulido was reflective on what his race might mean.”I don’t know if I’m going to win, but I think it’s real simple,” he said. “If I win, and if I win with a good margin, probably if that were to be 5 points or better, I think a big message has been sent that people here did not like what was going on with Trump, and our message resonated. “If I don’t win, then obviously we just didn’t do a good enough job. It’s just that simple. And then I guess maybe if that were to happen, then maybe the negative campaigning was the way to do it.” No matter what happens on Tuesday—whether Pulido gets his big win or goes home with regrets—the Rio Grande Valley will continue to be contested territory for both parties for the foreseeable future. But if Democrats don’t make big gains there this year, it’s hard to see their future in the region looking particularly bright. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The Backlash to Woke 1.0 Seems to Have Created a Very Male Candidate Field. But There’s Something Else Happening Too.
2026-11-02

The Backlash to Woke 1.0 Seems to Have Created a Very Male Candidate Field. But There’s Something Else Happening Too.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. In the world of Trump 2.0, perhaps the most notable thing about the left’s collective reckoning with the re-rise of Trump is that it’s been less about Donald Trump himself than a grappling with—and feverish denouncing of—the excesses of Woke 1.0. An overemphasis on race and gender, the now contemporary wisdom goes, combined with a pitched political moment and a large helping of pandemic mania, led many progressives—earnest activists, sanctimonious social media stars, even grandstanding elected officials—to take up positions that struck the average voter as weird, performative, censorious, or even threatening. Now, it seems, one of the only things uniting the broader left is that Woke 1.0 was crazy. A lot of Woke 1.0 was crazy. But the complaints at its core were not. The quick retreat from talking about race and gender is particularly unfortunate because noting pervasive inequalities could have been an advantage for Democrats if done right, especially given the attacks on immigrants and on abortion rights during Trump’s first term. Those issues certainly helped galvanize voters for Joe Biden in 2020, and after the Supreme Court overturned Roe v. Wade, young female voters in particular were livid. But progressives did not exactly stay focused. The GOP did, campaigning on marginal but highly inflammatory issues like trans women in sports and leaning into, rather than away from, the support of rank misogynists. While the left was debating whether we could say that women have abortions rather than the non-gender-specific people, the right was embracing the kind of men who say that women shouldn’t vote, are male property, and are responsible if they’re raped. We know what happened next: Trump won, and the misogynists crowed. (“Your Body, My Choice” was the viral postelection slogan of the online far right.) Ever since, Democrats have quieted down on racism and sexism. Even though roughly a third of American women (and more than half of Black women) live in a state that criminalizes abortion, reproductive rights have hardly been at the forefront of the midterms. The Women’s March is defunct. (No Kings rallygoers are not crying “Yas, queen!” out there.) Landmark civil rights legislation has been systematically dismantled, and any efforts to improve diversity, equity, and inclusion are not just mocked but, in many places—including in government contracts and U.S. foreign aid—totally verboten, the kind of behavior that could get your organization defunded and you personally fired. So perhaps it’s not surprising that the big races to watch in 2026 have seemed conspicuously male. Think of the Democrats who may grab Senate seats or maintain battleground ones: Abdul El-Sayed. Troy Jackson. Jon Ossoff. Sherrod Brown. James Talarico. Adam Hamilton. Among Polymarket’s five biggest races, all of the candidates are men, with the single exception of Susan Collins, in Maine. Close races in Nebraska, Ohio, Georgia, and New Hampshire all feature the same man-vs.-man dynamic. There are exceptions, of course. Three Republican women are running in the country’s most contested races: the aforementioned Collins, Ashley Hinson in Iowa, and Ashley Moody in Florida. Mary Peltola’s campaign in Alaska has been such a success that the Trump administration has already asked the courts to refuse to certify the election if she wins. Angie Nixon, the Democrat running in Florida, has been one of this year’s most exciting surprises—she’s a DSA candidate who initially got little establishment backing but now is running neck and neck with her opponent. But of 22 candidates vying for the most hotly contested Senate seats this year, just five are female. Only one (Nixon) is Black. Whom Democrats select to run their most important races matters, and the underrepresentation of women in these major races certainly says something about the political moment we’re in. But it’s also not the whole story of gender in the midterms. Woke-wary Democrats may not be talking about it, but there are a record number of women running for House seats this year, with the growth entirely on the Democratic side of the aisle. There are a record number of women running for governorships as well—with, again, the growth thanks to Democratic women. The Senate is a less heartening story—numbers of women running are down on both sides from 2020 and 2022 highs—but there are at least many more female candidates in Senate races this year than there were in 2024. And in most of the highest-profile House races, the Democratic candidates are female. This is still not, obviously, a vision of equality. Women account for only about a third of Democratic candidates, and only about a fifth of Republican ones. Feminists have rightly emphasized the dearth of women in American political office and critiqued all of the ways in which the deck is stacked against women’s electoral success: less funding, more scrutiny on their personal lives and appearances, the way power has long been coded as male. In the Goldilocks game of American politics, female candidates are always a little too something, or not quite enough—never the just-right fit. “I’d vote for a woman, just not that woman” is a dark cliché for a reason. But something interesting is happening here too: A whole lot of women are running for office, and few are remarking on it. This suggests that female candidates are increasingly seen as, well, normal. Such is the unsteady path of progress and the reality of social change: It often seems to be accelerating very quickly, but in the aftermath of that acceleration, it moves slowly enough as to sometimes be invisible—until, one day, you look up, look back, and see just how much ground has been gained. In 2016, when Trump took the presidency for the first time, women made up just 17.8 percent of U.S. House candidates; this year, they’re 27 percent. In 2016, 40 women ran for Senate seats; of those, 15 won their primaries and competed on national Election Day. This year, 65 women ran, and 19 moved forward to be on the November ballot. This is not a rapid rate of change. But it is change. There remains far to go. Sixteen states have still never sent a woman to the U.S. Senate. Seventeen have never elected a female governor. Mississippi has never sent a woman to the House at all. And of course, Americans have never put a woman in the White House. Still, it is something of a relief to see women running for office being treated as unremarkable. Feminists want women to be equally represented in the halls of power. But we also want to move away from a cultural view that treats men as the default and women as aberrations, or at least variations on a male norm. One sign that men are no longer widely viewed as the default face of political power is that women are competing for that power in larger numbers, and with less pushback or even notice. It may seem counterintuitive, but in some ways, the sound of progress is the sound of silence. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Democrats Are in the Middle of a Remarkable Comeback—Mostly Because of One Man
2026-11-02

Democrats Are in the Middle of a Remarkable Comeback—Mostly Because of One Man

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Shortly after their 2024 disaster, Democrats couldn’t even console themselves with thoughts of 2026. The House might have been in play, but they were facing a brutal Senate map—one that made it hard to see them picking up many seats even if voters had soured on the party in power. The only Senate Republican up for reelection in a state won by Kamala Harris was Maine Sen. Susan Collins, and Collins is famous for her inability to lose. The next best opportunity was North Carolina Sen. Thom Tillis’ seat, but Democrats hadn’t won a presidential or Senate race there since 2008. After that? Bupkis. Once-swing states like Iowa, Florida, and Ohio had become red states. Texas hadn’t been blue once this century, and retreated into safe GOP status in 2024. Plus, Democrats would have to defend Democratic seats in Georgia and Michigan, two states Trump won in 2024. Now, one day before the 2026 midterms, Democrats don’t just have an opportunity to net the four seats they need to retake the Senate majority. They’re actually narrow favorites to do so, according to election forecasters. Democrats hold delicate polling leads for Republican-held seats in Maine, Texas, Ohio, Alaska; are neck and neck in Iowa and Kansas; and are throwing a few bucks South Carolina and Mississippi’s way in the event of a generational night. On the defensive side, Democrats’ biggest threat of losing a Democrat-held seat is in the tight Michigan contest. And, by all accounts, Jon Ossoff has put the Georgia Senate race to rest, as has Gov. Roy Cooper in North Carolina’s Senate race. That Democrats have this good of a shot at the Senate majority isn’t something they would have believed two years ago—or a year ago, or six months ago, or even three months ago. It is, however, just a shot. A 2- or 3-point polling lead in any of these improbable states is within the margin of standard polling error. There’s a world in which Democrats outperform their own incredible polling, and the evening resembles the biggest landslide since the 2008 election. But there’s another in which they underperform it, even modestly, as red states revert to their partisan lean in the end. It could either be a historic night for Senate Democrats or a night with a historic number of impressive but close losses. Still, if you would have offered Democrats these odds in 2024, they would have scrambled for them. So how, then, did Democrats get in this unlikely position? For the most part, Donald Trump put them there. We’ve never seen a president do less to help his own party heading into an election, with so little to show for it, than Donald Trump has. There are 6 or 7 million instances we could recite in which Trump has done the opposite of attempting to help his party win, but the two defining policy moves of his second term (so far) are directly responsible for the creation of this map. Trump weaponized (and at times exceeded) powers ceded by Congress to both blanket the globe with tariffs and threaten countries whose foreign leaders were mean to him with additional levies. This has raised prices under a president who won the 2024 election on his promise to bring prices down. The pain has been especially acute in farm country, which can’t export its products due to retaliatory tariffs; the U.S. auto industry, whose supply chain is closely integrated with that of Canada; and both northern and southern border states. We refer you, again, to the map of Senate battlegrounds. Whoops. And then, of course, there’s the war in Iran, a major conflict the president launched after running on a pledge of no more forever wars. Aside from increasing gasoline prices for everyone, it spiked diesel and fertilizer prizes, placing yet another burden on rural America. It has resulted in the death and injury of Americans abroad, and caused a rift in the Republican coalition between more traditional hawks and MAGA isolationists. It’s also a war that has not, at the present time, been won by the United States. These issues make the 2026 midterms such a different beast than the midterms of Trump’s first presidency, in 2018. In 2018, the economy was pretty good, and Trump’s approval rating on his handling of the economy wasn’t half bad, either. Those midterms centered around some material factors—the attempted repeal of Obamacare, for example. But so much more of it was a reaction to Trump’s very presence in the White House, and the shock of his behavior, and his tweeting. That revulsion allowed Democrats to pick up 40 House seats largely concentrated among suburban, college-educated voters drifting away from the MAGA-fied GOP. But it didn’t spread into red America—especially with the Brett Kavanaugh confirmation fight that fall firing up Republican base voters—and the GOP that year flipped Democratic Senate seats in North Dakota, Missouri, Indiana, and Florida. This year, Trump’s approval over his handling of the economy is in the low 30s, while his overall approval rating is only 5 or so points better. It’s much more fertile ground for Democratic gains across blue, purple, and red America than would be a campaign against Trump’s poor manners. “The president is making it harder to pay your bills” is also a more effective line of attack than—valid as they may be—highfalutin arguments about Trump’s threats to democracy and the Constitution. But Trump’s behavior has not been entirely excised from Democrats’ message. Instead of emphasizing his foulmouthed tweets, Democrats are marrying a populist corruption argument with their broader criticism of the economy: that while you are suffering, Trump and his allies are enriching themselves. Corruption, according to some recent polls, has joined the economy as voters’ most pressing issue. Democrats were able to meet this opportunity in red states with quality recruits. The North Carolina Senate race has effectively been over since Democratic Gov. Roy Cooper entered it. The only Democrat who’s won an Ohio Senate race this century, Sherrod Brown, opted to run again. The only Democrat to win a federal election in Alaska since 2008, Mary Peltola, was also convinced to run for Senate instead of governor. (We’ll put a slight asterisk on the quality of the overall effort to elect Peltola in light of recent news developments.) Democrats landed friendly and compelling preacher types in Kansas and Texas, and a moderate Paralympian in Iowa. Party leaders failed to get their preferred candidates in Michigan and Maine—a purple and a blue state, respectively—but the anti-GOP atmosphere may well carry Democratic nominees Abdul El-Sayed and Troy Jackson to victory. (Of course, things got more complicated Sunday for El-Sayed when his primary appointment retracted her support and endorsed his Republican opponent.) So Democrats got the environment, gift-basketed to them by Donald Trump. They got the message. They got the candidates. How gettable, really, is the Senate majority? We need to reemphasize how tall an order this is, and the amount of ground Democrats need to make up relative to 2024. That year, Trump won Iowa by 13 points, Ohio by 11, Kansas by 16, Alaska by 13, and Texas by 14. All of those numbers are just on the cusp of what Democrats’ advantages in the national environment, candidate quality, and messaging will allow them to reach. If polls are underestimating GOP turnout in these states even a little bit—or not able to capture a last-minute GOP awakening—Democrats could be in for an agonizing night of close misses. Trump has brought Democrats back from how far they’d fallen in 2024. Now it’s just a question of whether they can breach the walls that two years ago seemed impossibly high. Thieves pilfer environment variables from Trigger AI workflow tool
2026-11-02

Thieves pilfer environment variables from Trigger AI workflow tool

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
So, who's ready to clear the dust out of that server closet and move things back on-prem? If you're a customer of cloud-hosted AI workflow platform Trigger.dev, take heed, because your environment variables may have been exposed in a security incident disclosed on Sunday. Trigger offered little explanation in its Sunday security incident report, noting that its investigation had identified “exposure across a subset of organizations” and that an attacker had gained unauthorized access to environment variables belonging to those organizations. Whatever exploit was used to gain access has been blocked, the company added, and affected customers have been notified of steps they need to take to prevent further security problems. We likely don't have to tell The Reg readers how important environment variables are in SaaS and cloud services like Trigger. If compromised environment variables contain sensitive credentials, an attacker could basically end up with the keys to the kingdom, potentially gaining access to connected services and digital infrastructure. For those unfamiliar with Trigger.dev, it’s a platform for building AI agents and workflows in TypeScript, but here’s the kicker: Trigger advertises itself as a durable execution platform that handles pesky things like retries, scaling, and keeping long-running tasks going through crashes and redeployments, while providing tools to monitor what those tasks are doing. Trigger, the company’s site notes, can be used to “build durable AI agents that survive refreshes, redeploys, and crashes,” and is ideal for “long-running tasks with retries, queues, AI observability and elastic scaling.” In short, companies running their AI on Trigger probably want it to keep running, which makes stolen environment variables all the more dangerous. Elastic scaling, automated retries, and, as Trigger notes, the ability to run lots of concurrent operations without timeouts could compound the consequences of compromised API keys, depending on what those credentials allow an attacker to access. An attacker with stolen credentials could potentially interfere with a customer's connected services or workflows. Additionally, Trigger recently added webhooks to its offering, supporting services like Stripe, Supabase, and Slack, potentially expanding the range of integrations that could be affected if their credentials were exposed. This could be pretty bad for anyone affected. Trigger has some fairly high-profile customers, with companies like fashion brand Emporio Armani, Pentagon-supporting AI acquisitions platform GovSignals, SaaS calendar platform Cal.com, and others all listed on the company’s site. Trigger has not publicly identified which organizations were affected by the intrusion. We reached out to Trigger, which declined to provide any additional information on the incident. A company spokesperson instead told us to wait for a full incident report to learn more. Luckily for Trigger customers and those concerned about SaaS security now that AI is regularly coming up with new vulnerabilities in commonly used platforms, Trigger is open source and can be self-hosted, which might be a better alternative in this day and age than relying on a hosted service.  ® IP house believes optics demand is going to explode as Nvidia and others eye bigger systems In the middle of a datacenter capex binge, vendor promises no more borrowing until the end of 2030 PARTNER CONTENT: 300+ AI applications delivering surges in core metrics and annual cost reduction of over CNY100m ISAC technology adds detection ability to existing cell networks, enabling them to track low-flying small drones So, who's ready to clear the dust out of that server closet and move things back on-prem? Token thieves costing companies $100K+ per day in illicit LLM charges
2026-11-02

Token thieves costing companies $100K+ per day in illicit LLM charges

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Victim organizations are losing millions of dollars in token charges as criminals hijack their corporate LLM access to steal expensive computing resources and AI services. These fraudulent charges from resource consumption can exceed $100,000 a day, according to a Monday report by Google's Mandiant incident responders, who saw cumulative charges at some organizations hit $1 million before the victim companies revoked intruders' access. "Whenever possible, threat actors want to use this technology to gain a scale advantage over defenders. They achieve that with agentic tools and by stealing tokens from unwitting third parties,” John Hultquist, chief analyst at Google Threat Intelligence Group, told The Register. “Locking down these resources will not only protect organizations from runaway costs, but also protect the broader community from a deluge of cheap, autonomous attacks.” This tactic of hijacking access to LLMs is called LLMJacking, and it’s been increasing steadily over the past two years. In one extreme case, documented in an August report from CrowdStrike, a token thief sent about 200,000 API requests in just two minutes. In one case detailed in the report, a group Google tracks as UNC6993 illicitly gained access to LLM API keys, then generated additional keys and enabled services within the victim’s cloud projects, racking up $100,000 in charges. The threat intel biz also documented a different token thief subscribing victim accounts to Pay-As-You-Go agreements to access advanced models on the victim company’s dime. The crook selected premium foundation models, launched high-volume inference streams, and used a “burn and rotate strategy” to bypass rate limits by creating and deleting short-lived service accounts while auditing the billing interface to monitor financial impact. In yet another incident, an intruder executed more than 500,000 LLM API calls in less than eight hours using stolen third-party API keys to power a token routing service called Jet Night, according to a Monday report from Google. “Given Jet Night’s per-token costs to users are so far below typical market rates GTIG believes the service relies on stolen keys to operate profitably,” the report says. “For example, Jet Night offers an unlimited token plan for approximately $60 USD per month with alleged access to frontier models, a consumer service level that would typically run between $100 and $250 per month.” Jet Night has also admitted to multiple outages on its public Telegram channel, which the Google threat hunters note is “likely due to stolen keys being revoked as victims discover their respective compromises.” As more criminals join the LLMJacking ecosystem, Google says it has documented a division of labor between the criminals that establish initial access to compromised cloud and AI services, and those that manage downstream monetization infrastructure, called “LLM Mixers.” In one case that Mandiant responded to in mid-2026, UNC6993 compromised a cloud instance through a misconfigured Service Account key and used this access to grant further access to a model API. The crooks also spun up a session hijacking page purporting to be an application portal to join a group of LLM users. “This page tricks users into navigating to the legitimate LLM service website, and pasting their raw JSON payload containing an active access token, which was then posted to the backend API to bypass password and multifactor authentication (MFA) controls,” the report says. Google predicts miscreants will “increasingly prioritize LLMJacking” compared to other types of resource theft like cryptojacking. And to prevent cloud LLM service abuse, the security firm says defenders should prioritize identity and access management plus supply chain security, and use a centralized secrets management platform to rotate keys and secrets. Also apply least-access privileges to users and service accounts, ensuring that they only have permissions necessary to complete their roles and tasks. And automate secrets scanning within codebases to identify and then remove any API keys and secrets added to source code. ® IP house believes optics demand is going to explode as Nvidia and others eye bigger systems In the middle of a datacenter capex binge, vendor promises no more borrowing until the end of 2030 PARTNER CONTENT: 300+ AI applications delivering surges in core metrics and annual cost reduction of over CNY100m ISAC technology adds detection ability to existing cell networks, enabling them to track low-flying small drones So, who's ready to clear the dust out of that server closet and move things back on-prem? SUSE to cut one in ten employees because of... AI
2026-11-02

SUSE to cut one in ten employees because of... AI

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
'Modern tooling is already changing our daily workflows' says CEO at open source biz One in ten employees at SUSE are facing the chop due to "automation and AI," according to a memo dispatched by management at the privately owned open source software biz. In an email to the 1,900 strong workforce on Monday - seen by The Register - CEO Dirk-Peter van Leeuwen, the former Red Hat exec who joined SUSE in March 2023, confirmed the "difficult news." "We are announcing a reduction of SUSE’s global workforce of approximately 10 percent," he said. "These decisions are painful for our people, our culture, and our open source community, but they are necessary for us to adapt and build a sustainable, resilient SUSE for the long term." Van Leeuwen says the business, bought by Swedish private equity provider EQT, decided to cut jobs after scrutinizing its "future strategy, required capabilities, and team structures." The rest of the organization will get a look at the fiscal year 2027 strategy on November 16. "What is clear is that automation and AI are fundamentally shifting how software companies operate. AI capabilities are advancing exponentially, forcing us to radically reimagine our work. We have seen this trend already both inside our sector and in the wider economy. This means that the organizational design and cost base that SUSE is built on today will not carry us forward successfully. "We are adapting. Across SUSE, modern tooling is already changing our daily workflows, enabling teams to move faster with less operational overhead and flatter reporting lines. The requirement for some existing roles has greatly diminished. Other roles will evolve over the next few years. This means we are accelerating investments in high priority areas, and deprioritizing others. These are hard, painful decisions to make, but necessary for our future." As The Reg reported in 2025, SUSE doubled down on AI. The specific roles at risk of redundancy were not outlined but SUSE claims it has considered where the people in those positions are "able to evolve, what they can stop doing, what they need to do more of - and decisions have been made on that basis." Staff caught up in the redundancies will be informed by November 4, employee representatives nominated the following day, and one to one meetings will begin at the start of next week. Van Leeuwen says in the internal memo that the job cuts are a "reflection of shifting organizational needs, not your talent or commitment. You have tackled hard infrastructure and open source challenges that power critical enterprise systems around the world. "Any organization will be fortunate to have your expertise." Back in September, we reported that the company was asking old hands if they would take "voluntary separation." It seems not enough people chose to walk through the door, so instead, they're getting thrown through it instead. SUSE "geckos" in the Czech Republic may be glad they joined a trade union. Back in 2018, EQT bought SUSE from Microfocus for $2.5 billion. EQT floated SUSE on the Frankfurt Stock Exchange in 2021 with slightly disappointing results. A couple of years later, new CEO Dirk-Peter van Leeuwen took over, and later in 2023, EQT chose to take the company private again. The commercial context is that in March SUSE's corporate owner EQT was reportedly considering selling it off again. ® IP house believes optics demand is going to explode as Nvidia and others eye bigger systems In the middle of a datacenter capex binge, vendor promises no more borrowing until the end of 2030 PARTNER CONTENT: 300+ AI applications delivering surges in core metrics and annual cost reduction of over CNY100m ISAC technology adds detection ability to existing cell networks, enabling them to track low-flying small drones So, who's ready to clear the dust out of that server closet and move things back on-prem? Nuclear threats and street brawls: Russia, Ukraine report attacks on their emergency alert apps
2026-11-02

Nuclear threats and street brawls: Russia, Ukraine report attacks on their emergency alert apps

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Within minutes of a ceasefire lifting in Kherson, Ukraine’s citizens were pummelled with threatening notifications Cyberattacks on both Russia’s and Ukraine’s emergency alert apps resulted in fake messages about missile and nuclear strikes broadcast to the public. Officials from both countries confirmed the attacks, which occurred at similar times, on Sunday evening. According to public statements and messages sent via social media, Ukraine’s alert app was hit first, with the country’s Air Force quick to dispel nuclear fears. "The Air Alert app has likely been compromised," it said via Telegram at around 2100 UTC. "There is no radiation threat! Stay calm, follow our messages on our channel!" At around 0030 UTC on Monday, Russia's Ministry of Emergency Situations confirmed the country’s app had also been abused, with fake messages sent to citizens' devices. "Attention! The Russian Ministry of Emergency Situations' mobile app has been subjected to a massive cyberattack, allowing attackers to send fake information to the public," it said, also via Telegram. "Work is currently underway to patch the vulnerability and restore normal operation." The ministry issued an update around 90 minutes later, saying the app was once again functioning normally, and pointing out that an attack on the app was not the same as a compromise of the state’s national alert system. "Citizens are alerted through primary channels: regional and municipal alert systems (sirens, loudspeakers), communication networks (SMS messaging), and television and radio broadcasts. The mobile app is merely an additional, supporting information service," it stated. Ukraine's Ministry of Digital Affairs also issued a public announcement reminding citizens about how to receive emergency warnings while its “Alarm!” app was down. These include enabling iOS and Android emergency alerts, state-controlled sirens, and other official sources, such as the official social media accounts of Ukraine's government. According to the messages sent to devices after the compromise of Ukraine's alert app, the fake warnings pertained to various threats, such as street fights in Kyiv, chemical attacks, and radiation hazards. Alerts about the threat of nuclear fallout from official government warning systems would normally lead to panic, however, one of the earliest messages sent to citizens was written in Russian and referenced the "Oreshnik" missile, which was an immediate signal that the message may not be genuine. Equivalent alerts sent to Russian citizens also warned of impending nuclear strikes "at all regions of Russia," referencing "P1-Sun," the name of a Ukrainian attack drone. Oleksander Merezhko, who chairs the Ukrainian parliament's foreign affairs committee and noted the suspicious Russian-language alert, told the BBC the attack was almost certainly part of Russia's cyber war. "It's a very unpleasant fact but it is part of cyber war and people in Ukraine understand that," he said. "It is possible and we are quite prepared for such possibilities." Russia has not explicitly blamed Ukraine for the attack on its app, and official sources say the app is running as normal. Ukraine's announcement of the alert app cyberattack came almost simultaneously with the official conclusion of a six-day ceasefire in Kherson. Russia agreed to the rare temporary suspension of conflict to allow hundreds of Ukrainians to evacuate the Russian-occupied city of Olehsky. Russia did not agree to a proposed extension of the ceasefire, which officially ended at around 2100 UTC – around the same time Ukrainians began receiving fake warning messages. ® IP house believes optics demand is going to explode as Nvidia and others eye bigger systems In the middle of a datacenter capex binge, vendor promises no more borrowing until the end of 2030 PARTNER CONTENT: 300+ AI applications delivering surges in core metrics and annual cost reduction of over CNY100m ISAC technology adds detection ability to existing cell networks, enabling them to track low-flying small drones So, who's ready to clear the dust out of that server closet and move things back on-prem? Microsoft's Canada timezone troubles leave admins fixing calendars, pretending they're in Bogota
2026-11-02

Microsoft's Canada timezone troubles leave admins fixing calendars, pretending they're in Bogota

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Canadian regions stop turning back the clocks, Microsoft wishes it could Microsoft has discovered once again that few things strike fear into the heart of a software engineer quite like a government deciding to fiddle with the clocks. Parts of Canada stopped observing seasonal clock changes on November 1, leaving affected areas of British Columbia, Alberta, and Manitoba permanently on UTC-07:00, UTC-06:00 and UTC-05:00 respectively. Unfortunately, Microsoft's sprawling software empire hasn't entirely caught up. The company has confirmed problems across a chunk of its software estate, leaving some customers facing incorrect times, broken schedules and a collection of workarounds while Redmond catches up. Outlook users have been left with the most homework. Switching to the new Canadian time zones won't automatically update existing calendar entries, meaning that meetings scheduled after November 1 are potentially an hour out of whack. Microsoft advises users to change their Outlook calendar time zone, then update upcoming events and meetings to use it. For meetings involving other people, only the organizer can make the change. Recurring meetings come with an additional treat: changing the time zone resets any previously moved occurrences, which then have to be recreated. Anyone with a calendar full of recurring meetings may wish to put the kettle on first. Microsoft is working on an Exchange Online PowerShell cmdlet to remediate non-recurring meetings, but according to its guidance, that's not due until later this week. A fix for recurring meetings remains "in development," with an ETA yet to be confirmed. Things get stranger with Power Automate and Fabric, where Microsoft's temporary fix involves pretending you're somewhere else entirely. Depending on the product and province, that could mean Saskatchewan, Arizona or, for Manitobans, Bogota. Because apparently time zones weren't confusing enough already. SharePoint On-Prem customers, meanwhile, have to wait until November 10 for the correct time zones to arrive, while Power Pages users could see server-formatted dates appear an hour out until a service fix lands. Microsoft's current milestone for that is December 12. Admins dealing with the fallout aren't exactly delighted. One sysadmin on Reddit said Microsoft's guidance had been "terrible" and claimed to have written roughly 900 lines of PowerShell to change users' Outlook settings and manually adjust calendar events. Others described switching systems to Saskatchewan time while waiting for Microsoft's new zones to settle in. Microsoft published its guidance on October 26, less than a week before the change took effect, and was still adding affected products and workarounds four days later. Meanwhile, admins were urged to test calendars, recurring meetings, and schedules before the November 1 switch, leaving little time to find out what might break. If any of this sounds familiar, it's because time has caused trouble for Microsoft before. In 2022, Chile delayed its switch to daylight saving time by a week. Microsoft responded by warning that Windows clocks could be wrong, scheduled tasks could run at unexpected times, and timestamps on files, transactions, and logs could end up an hour out. Four years later, governments are still messing with time and software is still apparently objecting. ® IP house believes optics demand is going to explode as Nvidia and others eye bigger systems In the middle of a datacenter capex binge, vendor promises no more borrowing until the end of 2030 PARTNER CONTENT: 300+ AI applications delivering surges in core metrics and annual cost reduction of over CNY100m ISAC technology adds detection ability to existing cell networks, enabling them to track low-flying small drones So, who's ready to clear the dust out of that server closet and move things back on-prem? Frozen part II: Hackers remotely defrost produce in Euro supermarkets ... again
2026-11-02

Frozen part II: Hackers remotely defrost produce in Euro supermarkets ... again

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Let it go (bad): Dozens of stores report mass food wastage as managers start their week in puddles The cyberattacks on commercial refrigerators and freezers in Europe continue, as a slew of grocery stores in Belgium, Ireland, Luxembourg, and Sweden report widespread food wastage as a result of miscreants raising the temperatures to highs of 50ºC (122°F). In Belgium, around 40 Delhaize stores were affected after the attackers reportedly switched fridges and freezers into defrost mode overnight, between Sunday and Monday, resulting in stock issues. Diego Louwers, a Proxy Delhaize store manager in Overijse, near Brussels, told RTL Info that the consequences of the cyberattacks were "catastrophic," forcing the store to throw out the entirety of its fresh and frozen products. "We can't have anything worse in our line of work," he said. "We have to throw away all of our merchandise, and it's both heartbreaking and very complicated operationally." A Delhaize spokesperson said it took steps to ensure stores remain stocked, although fresh and frozen products are "temporarily somewhat limited." "On Monday, 2 November, we identified an IT incident affecting the refrigeration systems in several dozen of our stores," they said. "We immediately took the necessary measures to keep our stores operational and ensure that our customers can continue to do their shopping as usual. "In the affected stores, the range of fresh and frozen products is temporarily somewhat limited. Our distribution centres are operating normally, and there has been no disruption to deliveries to our stores. "We are, of course, continuing to ensure that our products are stored and presented under optimal conditions, so that we can provide our customers with the quality and freshness they have come to expect from us." Around 15 Carrefour stores are also reportedly affected, as are sites operated by Intermarché, Spar, and Shop & Go stores located at Q8 service stations. Belgium’s De Laet & Van Haver chain of butcher shops reported temperature rises in its appliances too, but followed up with a statement on Monday saying there was no impact on its produce, and all stores remain open. Simultaneously, similar issues have affected Swedish grocery chain ICA, which confirmed attacks on a "small number of stores." "We can confirm that we have been informed of an incident overnight affecting a small number of ICA stores operating outside ICA’s central network," a company spokesperson said. "The incident impacted the stores’ refrigeration and freezer systems. ICA is currently supporting the affected stores in managing the situation." ICA did not respond to questions about the scale of food wastage nor when normal operations are expected to continue. One store, ICA Nära in Böda on Öland, said via social media that it is open today but has no fresh or frozen produce to sell. "Unfortunately, we are one of the stores whose refrigerators and freezers have been hacked," it said. "We are open as usual, but unfortunately we have no refrigerators and freezers to sell. "We have no status on when the refrigerators and freezers are expected to be up and running again." The store manager, Mats Olsson, told SVT that he arrived to work on Monday morning and was greeted by a puddle of water and freezers set to 55 degrees C – a tale echoed by other managers across the country. Coop stores are affected by similar attacks too, with six locations in central Sweden experiencing fridge-freezer issues, according to a company spokesperson. Likewise, at least 13 SuperValue stores in Ireland are also affected, with fresh and frozen food being thrown out, according to parent company Musgrave speaking to RTE. The news comes less than a month after various grocery chains in France resorted to discarding bucketfuls of produce after their appliances were remotely defrosted by cybercrooks. Carrefour, Coopérative U, Intermarché, Monoprix, and others were hit with cyberattacks in mid-October, and hundreds of stores across the chains were reportedly impacted. At least some of the affected stores were running outdated firmware on their Danfoss refrigeration controllers, and shortly after the attacks were confirmed, CERT-FR published an alert pertaining to numerous Danfoss AK-SM and AK-SC controller compromises. Other retailers, such as Coopérative U, confirmed they were not Danfoss customers. It remains unclear if the latest attacks across Europe used the same methods as those used for the disruption in France last month. Anthropic clamps down on Claude’s AI video game clones

The Verge | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Anthropic is steering Claude away from recreating specific titles, but users can still use it to make their own games. Anthropic is steering Claude away from recreating specific titles, but users can still use it to make their own games. Anthropic has updated Claude Code’s system prompt to prevent the AI tool from being used to recreate existing video games, as reported by Tom’s Hardware. Retro gaming is currently being overrun with AI-made decompilations, recompilations, and ports, but while Anthropic will still let people use Claude Code to make games, it doesn’t want people to use it for direct clones. Last week, a user on X posted a screenshot allegedly showing two new sentences added to Claude Code’s system prompt: “Do build original games, games in a genre, and games from the user’s own designs and assets. Don’t build a recreation of a specific existing game, even if it is named or called original.” Anthropic’s Addy Osmani, in a reply, didn’t confirm that specific language but did say that the company had updated the prompt in Claude Code on Opus 5.5: We added two sentences to Claude Code’s prompt on Opus 5.5 to steer it away from recreating someone else’s specific game because using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy. Original games, genre games, and games built from your own designs and assets are meant to keep working. We’ll continue to iterate to ensure we’re striking the right balance. In the meantime, if Claude turns down something that’s yours, send it my way. We’ve asked Anthropic if it has or will be updating other Claude system prompts as well. Xbox’s former chief operating officer, Helen Chiang, suddenly left Microsoft less than two weeks ago after being promoted into the role in July. I wrote in my Notepad newsletter last week that it seemed very weird for Chiang to suddenly depart Microsoft after 21 years with a surprisingly short memo from Xbox CEO Asha Sharma. Now, Chiang says she has “serious concerns” about how she was treated. In a lengthy LinkedIn post — entitled “clarity is kindness,” a reference to Sharma’s WSJ profile — Chiang doesn’t dispute that she was fired, but she doesn’t say why she was let go. Eurogamer reported last month that Chiang had been “terminated for policy violations,” but she says this is “not accurate.” Chiang denies unfounded rumors that speculated she was leaking confidential information, or that she left to join a competitor. It’s clear the decision to leave Xbox was out of her hands. “You don’t spend two decades somewhere without developing a deep loyalty to the people, teams, and businesses you’ve helped build,” says Chiang. “Xbox was never just another stop in my career. My end game was always to keep building here.” She also denies engaging in “discrimination, bullying, harassment, insider trading, embezzlement, or other fraudulent activity.” Like I wrote in Notepad last week, Chiang has been described to me by multiple Xbox employees as highly respected, with high integrity. “I stand by my record: both the results I delivered and the way I have treated people throughout my career,” says Chiang. The most significant part of Chiang’s post is a paragraph on fairness and accountability, hinting that she wasn’t treated fairly: I believe in fairness, accountability, and holding ourselves to the standards we expect of others. Those standards only mean something when they’re applied consistently. I have serious concerns about how they were applied in my case, and I believe those concerns deserve to be taken seriously. Chiang also says “This is not how I ever imagined my story here would end. And it is not the whole story.” We’ve reached out to Chiang to ask if she’s pursuing legal action against Microsoft. We’ve also asked Microsoft to comment on Chiang’s post, but the company didn’t respond in time for publication. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. OpenAI pitches data center power plants as a way to protect nearby areas
2026-11-02

OpenAI pitches data center power plants as a way to protect nearby areas

The Verge | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
As public opinion has soured on data centers, it’s selling ‘AI campuses’ as protectors, instead of noisy polluters. As public opinion has soured on data centers, it’s selling ‘AI campuses’ as protectors, instead of noisy polluters. In an announcement on Monday, OpenAI says it will pilot its “Grid Protectors Program” at an upcoming data center in Effingham County, Georgia, where the company promises to free up to 1,000 megawatts of power during extreme weather or emergencies, enough to power air conditioners in around 700,000 homes. The announcement comes as opposition to data centers spreads across the US, in response to massive projects that have begun development and construction, frequently with little or no input from local residents. In Utah, residents protested after Box Elder County commissioners signed off on a data center expected to consume more than double the state’s peak electricity demand in 2025, and spread hot air in the desert valley surrounding the data center. Now OpenAI’s approach is to pitch the construction of power plants that feed the electricity required by the new AI data centers it’s building as “a resource that utilities can call on when communities need it most.” Several states like Tennessee, Florida, Oklahoma, Oregon, and others have enacted laws that say they will prevent AI companies from passing energy costs on to residents, in addition to moratoriums and other restrictions. This is besides the environmental impacts, as a recent study by the Houston Advanced Research Center (HARC) said that powering proposed Texas data center projects could undo decades of air quality gains over just five years. OpenAI, which has switched to calling its AI data centers “compute campuses” in an effort to separate from their poor public reputation, claims it helps reduce costs for customers by operating year-round: “Electricity demand also rises and falls with the seasons, with different regions facing their biggest peaks at different times,” OpenAI’s announcement says. “Our compute campuses need power year-round. With the right agreements, that steady demand can help spread the grid’s fixed costs across more electricity sales, while flexibility lets us reduce demand when the system is busiest.” OpenAI says it’s going to continue working with policymakers and utility services to make “arrangements for our future campus to reduce electricity demand when the grid is under strain.” Along with its data center in Georgia, OpenAI is also partnering with Oracle and SoftBank to build its “Stargate” data centers across the US, including in Shackelford County, Texas; Doña Ana County, New Mexico; and Milam County, Texas. Brave has allegedly provided ‘stolen copyrighted content’ to many AI companies.﻿ Brave has allegedly provided ‘stolen copyrighted content’ to many AI companies.﻿ The New York Times and a group of other publications have filed lawsuits against the company that makes the Brave web browser over alleged unlawful scraping to train AI and also letting users bypass their paywalls, as reported by Reuters. According to a complaint filed by the companies that run New York Daily News, the Chicago Tribune, and the Orlando Sentinel, Brave Software has monetized its index of scraped websites by, in part, providing AI companies with paid access to “real-time online content from the Brave Index.” The AI companies then use that index to train their models, access content to “respond to specific user queries,” or both. Brave has allegedly provided “stolen copyrighted content” to “many” AI companies including Anthropic, Perplexity, Mistral, xAI, and Cohere. Brave also promotes “hundreds of top publishers” as being accessible through its “Paywall blocker” feature in its browser. “Brave has never asked for the Publishers’ permission to access their websites, much less to copy the material and provide it to users or other AI companies,” the complaint says. “On the contrary, as noted above, Brave allows its users to bypass the Publishers’ paywalls without their consent, and has taken other steps that prevent the Publishers from blocking Brave’s access to the Publishers’ websites. Brave has never compensated the Publishers for the unlawful use, copying, and distribution of the Publishers’ copyrighted content.” “While we believe in the ethical and responsible use and development of AI, Brave has flouted established copyright law and ignored repeated requests by The Times to stop its infringing conduct,” Graham James, an NYT spokesperson, says in a statement. “We filed this lawsuit to hold Brave accountable for stealing our content to power its own products and to provide it to some of the most prominent and well resourced generative AI companies in the world.” Brave, Anthropic, Mistral, SpaceXSI, and Cohere, didn’t immediately reply to a request for comment. “The New York Times is overpaying its attorneys and underpaying its journalists. (Who could’ve easily identified the disinformation in this suit.)” Jesse Dwyer, Perplexity’s chief communications officer, says in a statement to The Verge. MIT Technology Review
Raising the quality of insights in agentic analytics
2026-11-02

Raising the quality of insights in agentic analytics

MIT Technology Review | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
In just a few years, generative AI has changed the face of enterprise analytics, notably by making analytics accessible to users across the organization. Agentic AI promises to do even more, by automating entire workflows and taking actions based on agent insights. But there is a problem: The quality of insights that agents currently generate leaves much to be desired. Fewer than half of agent-generated insights at the organizations surveyed for this report lead to business improvement. And while most respondents praise the quality of their agents’ descriptive insights, reviews of their prescriptive insights—those on which actions are based—are decidedly mixed. Failure to produce insights that improve the business deprives organizations of their desired return on investment in agentic analytics. Worse, flawed insights that trigger incorrect actions can cause reputational or financial damage. This report gauges how companies view the quality of the outputs produced by their analytics agents and explores how organizations are seeking to boost insight quality and get their agents working harder for the business. Some key findings from the report include the following: Agentic analytics is only just starting to deliver. On average, 48% of insights generated by analytics agents at the surveyed companies lead to actions that improve the business. That is a marked advance from the 37% average the respondents say generated such actions one year ago. But it means that more than half of the insights generated by analytics agents today are still failing to help businesses. Better quality of insights leads to clear business advantage. At a small cohort of organizations, which we term “insight leaders,” AI agents perform much better than the average. In this group, more than 70% of agent insights lead to actions that improve the business. The insight leaders also give the quality of their agents’ insights higher marks than do the total respondents overall. The quality gap is particularly wide when it comes to prescriptive insights. Insufficient context reduces insight quality. When analytics agents deliver insights of substandard quality, the main reason is a lack of business context, as cited by 55% of surveyed executives. Fragmented or inaccessible data is the other major factor, according to 45%. Fewer insight leaders say their agents struggle with access to context. Insight overload further inhibits agent effectiveness. At a majority (52%) of surveyed organizations, analytics agents generate too many insights to be properly evaluated and therefore acted upon. Less than one-quarter (24%) are able to evaluate all agent insights; most do so on a case-by-case basis, and that process is currently human-intensive. Governance and semantics are areas of focus for improving insights. The surveyed companies are prioritizing initiatives that enable better agent insights by strengthening AI and data governance (cited by 60%). They are also adding a semantic layer to their data architecture (45%). The experts we interviewed agree that the semantic layer is crucial to ensure high-performing agents. This content was produced by Insights, MIT Technology Review’s custom content arm, not its editorial staff. It was researched and written by humans, with any AI tools that may have been used limited to production processes under human oversight. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible.
