MIT Technology Review
Stop counting tokens, start counting what ships
2026-11-02

Stop counting tokens, start counting what ships

MIT Technology Review | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
When a developer gives an AI agent a coding task, the agent does more than answer once. It plans, generates code, runs tests, and repeats the process to correct errors. A single task can use 10 to 50 times more tokens than a basic chat prompt. The more useful the agent becomes, the more calls it makes. With per-token pricing, the workflows creating the most value can also become the most expensive. The financial pressure is already visible. Uber reportedly exhausted its entire 2026 AI budget by April after expanding its use of Claude Code. GitHub Copilot moved from flat-rate pricing to metered credits. Gartner predicts that AI coding token costs will surpass average developer salaries by 2028. The natural response is rationing usage caps, seat limits, and approvals before developers launch agents. But blanket limits can restrict the workflows delivering value. They also optimize the wrong number. Cost per token measures consumption, not results. Some spending reflects waste, including retries and runaway loops. Some represents the work of shipping software. A meter cannot distinguish them, and neither can a spending cap. A better measure is cost per merged pull request: total AI-assisted development cost divided by the number of changes that pass review and are merged. Track rework and defects alongside it so cheaper changes do not conceal lower quality. If that cost falls as AI usage rises, without compromising quality, developers are converting more consumption into useful software. The question is not how many tokens they use, but how much finished work the investment produces. Improving that equation requires two changes: reserve expensive inference for tasks that need it, and lower the cost of routine work. Agentic coding does not require the most powerful model at every step. Use a frontier model, or an on-premises flagship when policy requires it, for difficult planning and novel reasoning. Route suitable work, including code generation, test creation, and repeated refactoring, to open-weight models you operate yourself. A gateway can route requests according to task complexity, data sensitivity, cost, and latency. This reserves premium inference for the moments that justify it rather than applying premium pricing to an entire workflow. Meter the exception, not the workload. Open-weight models make this division increasingly practical. Software-engineering benchmarks show that leading open-weight models can approach frontier performance on some tasks. That does not establish equivalence across every repository or workflow. Evaluate candidates against your own code, tests, and acceptance criteria before assigning production work. Next, decide who owns the capacity. With rented inference, every call adds to the bill. With owned capacity, most of the cost is committed upfront, so each additional call gets cheaper as usage grows. The economics are simple: per-token pricing makes heavy use more expensive, while owned capacity becomes more efficient as teams use it more. Ownership is not free, though. Power, operations, maintenance, and depreciation all must be considered. The number that matters is the fully loaded cost per merged pull request. Owned capacity also delivers a type of value that never appears on an invoice: control over sensitive code. When code generation and testing run on-premises, proprietary code stays inside the enterprise. Only approved planning prompts and governed exceptions go to external models, through a gateway the organization controls. That opens the sensitive repositories where security usually stalls adoption, and where the biggest gains are waiting. At HPE, a hybrid AI platform routes routine workloads from more than 150 internal applications to open-weight models running on premises, including on HPE Private Cloud AI. The result: token costs down 30 to 50%. That is the point of hybrid. With HPE Private Cloud AI, owning your inference does not mean giving up frontier models. Run the volume on capacity you own and let a gateway send the hardest work. Data also shows that usage, not licensed headcount, drives spend. The top 5% of users generated most of the consumption, and the heaviest used roughly 450 times more than the lightest on the same license. If power users grow from a small minority to half the workforce, monthly spend can rise about 4.4 times with flat headcount. Your mix will differ, which is exactly why you need to measure it. HPE tracks two metrics and recommends customers do the same. First, it tracks cost per active developer, because averaging across all licenses hides the power users driving spend. Second, it tracks cost per merged pull request, because it connects AI spending to software that ships. Baseline both costs before changing infrastructure. Then run a complete development cycle on owned capacity. If both metrics do not improve as usage grows, your current model may still be the better choice. Owned capacity is not for everyone. Low or unpredictable usage favors APIs, with no capital tied up in idle infrastructure. Ownership pays off when sustained demand keeps it busy. Most enterprises will run hybrid: frontier APIs for demanding or intermittent work, owned capacity for repeatable volume. A governed gateway decides what leaves the building, so you get frontier models without giving up sovereignty over your code. As developers lean harder on coding agents, usage will keep rising. The real choice is whether that growth shows up as a bigger variable-cost bill or as more software shipped on capacity you own. The companies that lead AI-assisted development will not ration adoption to control costs. They will build an operating model where more AI use improves the economics instead of undermining them. To learn more about HPE Private Cloud AI, please visit HPE. This content was produced by HPE. It was not written by MIT Technology Review’s editorial staff. MIT Technology Review
The Download: the agentic web arrives and the drive for safer roads
2026-11-02

The Download: the agentic web arrives and the drive for safer roads

MIT Technology Review | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology. In 2001, a decade after inventing the World Wide Web, Tim Berners-Lee articulated a new vision: an internet navigated by autonomous software agents that address our every need, without requiring us to muck about with browsers and search engines ourselves. The agentic web is here
2026-11-02

The agentic web is here

MIT Technology Review | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
In 2001, a decade after he invented the World Wide Web, Tim Berners-Lee articulated a new vision. Together with two colleagues, he published an article in the magazine Scientific American in which he outlined what he called the “Semantic Web”: an internet designed to be navigated by autonomous software agents that could address our every need, without requiring us to muck about with browsers and search engines ourselves. To illustrate what this might look like, Berners-Lee and his colleagues told a story about a pair of siblings who use their agents to automatically research and book physical therapy appointments for their mother, making sure they would be covered by insurance and fit their busy calendars. At the turn of the millennium, this vision seemed far-fetched: Google had only been online for a couple of years, and most people were still on dial-up connections. But a quarter-century on, the dream of autonomous software agents that use the internet and carry out tasks for us has finally been realized. New tools such as Meta’s blockbuster Muse agent, OpenAI’s Dots, and Anthropic’s  are being given access to people’s email inboxes and bank accounts,  Meanwhile, in a series of worrying incidents, agents created by OpenAI broke free of their sandboxes and caused havoc online. Just as the rise of smartphones sparked the development of a more atomized and attention-grabbing internet, the advent of powerful AI systems heralds a new era: We’re facing not the semantic web but the agentic web. This new web is developing out of sight and in ways that might not be entirely within our control. As it grows, the agentic web will inevitably transform the human web—and whether the human web survives this transition is anyone’s guess. For Berners-Lee’s original vision to work, internet users would have had to annotate the pages they built with special tags so that a software agent could understand each element’s meaning—say, “street address” for the part of a physical therapy office’s web page that indicates its location and “appointment types” for the list of services. And so the idea never really caught on. “It’s a lot of labor,” says Eni Mustafaraj, a professor of computer science at Wellesley College. “And the benefits of that were not obvious.” For the next two decades, then, the internet remained a place that put humans first, with the odd Twitter reply bot or web scraper to round things out. Large language models changed that. Unlike the agents that Berners-Lee was imagining, today’s LLM-based agents don’t need any tags to understand which part of the text on a clinic’s site is its address and which details the services it offers. They can search for and process text far faster than any human, and agents with multimodal capabilities can even interpret screenshots of websites and click on specific elements such as hyperlinks and drop-down menus. They’re also just a way more intuitive means of finding information and accomplishing tasks online. Rather than poring over pages of Google results to find a budget-friendly product dupe or a half-remembered meme, why not have Claude or ChatGPT peruse dozens of sites in just a few minutes, guided only by your vague query? Or go one step further and use a personal assistant agent like Muse to hunt down a restaurant reservation or book a flight. As agents flood online spaces, this new user base is changing the web for good. Time magazine, for instance, is publishing versions of its articles that are easier for agents to parse and has started selling ads targeted at them. Companies from Etsy to Expedia have partnered with the major AI firms to build tools that allow agents to interface seamlessly with their services. Despite these early efforts, it’s still tough to tell exactly what forms the agentic web will take and how it will change our online experience. Depending on how it’s designed and who controls access to it, we could be in for something that’s as open and customizable as the web of the early 2000s, only far easier to use. Alternatively, we could be reduced to viewing much of the web through tiny portholes controlled by a few dominant platforms, as independent sites and creators struggle to survive the shift. And as this summer’s series of OpenAI security incidents suggests, the agents might even build their own online networks and resources—whether we want them to or not. How this plays out also depends on us: which parts of our online lives we’re happy to delegate to our agents and where we will still demand a human-centric internet. “There’s a lot we don’t know yet about how users want to use [agents],” says Ajit Varma, head of Firefox at Mozilla. But whatever does happen, the changes will be big. “It’s probably on par with getting a mobile phone,” he says. “I think things are going to change very dramatically.” There are two ways to approach building the agentic web. The first involves training agents to use browsers like humans—scrolling, clicking, typing—and letting them loose on the web that we’re all used to. The second requires building tools that allow agents to interface with the internet via text and code, their preferred media. That possibility offers more separation between the human and the agentic web: designers can create different experiences for those who use text commands and those who use browsers. When Siva Reddy, a professor of computer science and linguistics at McGill University, began studying LLM agents in 2024, he focused on the first option. “At that time, we thought it should be that the best agent in the world is probably an agent that can use a browser,” he says. Such an agent would integrate seamlessly with the preexisting human web, and its owner could easily supervise it by watching as it navigated familiar interfaces. The frontier AI companies were already developing these “computer-use agents.” But they didn’t work terribly well—at least not at first. “There’s a lot of moving pieces [on the web], and they are not really optimized for automatic consumption,” says Xing Han Lu, a graduate student in Reddy’s lab at McGill. We humans are naturals at interacting with graphical interfaces using a keyboard and mouse, but agents are not. So Reddy and Lu pivoted toward the second, tool-based approach. In the summer of 2025, they expounded on this view in a preprint paper envisioning a new kind of web interface that would allow agents to interact with websites just as effectively as we do. Such a full-featured version of the agentic web doesn’t yet exist, but it is emerging in smaller ways. For example, if you open up Claude, ChatGPT, or Muse, you’ll find an option to add a wide variety of “plug-ins” or “connectors” that your agents can use: Gmail connectors allow your agent to triage your inbox, and Instacart plug-ins let your agent do your grocery shopping. Under the hood, these plug-ins allow your agent to interact with the back ends of websites using text commands. When human engineers design these plug-ins, they can decide how they want to allow agents to interact with the website—and what they don’t want to let them do. In this way, connectors can help make agents much safer online. For instance, Muse can buy products for you using its connector with Stripe’s Link payment service. Stripe knows your credit card number, but the agent doesn’t see it, and the connector requires human confirmation before a purchase goes through—so Muse can’t go on an unlicensed shopping spree. Agents also use specialized tools to find information online. They don’t type questions directly into the Google search bar; in fact, they often don’t even access the websites they reference. Instead, they query text databases collected by simple search crawler bots. People who want to make web content specifically for agents can take advantage of these crawlers: Time has pioneered that approach by serving its agent-oriented ads to crawlers in the hopes that they will be incorporated into these databases and surface when agents search for information. But in recent months, an alternative to this bespoke tool approach has finally started to seem viable. The major AI companies have managed to train much more successful computer-use agents than were previously available. GPT-6 Astra, which OpenAI released last September, achieved a 73% success rate on OSWorld 2.0, a benchmark that tests computer-use agents on their ability to complete complex, multistep tasks using software such as Slack and Salesforce. Such results indicate that agents are close to being able to navigate the human web just as readily as we can. “We thought we had to build a bridge to the AI agents, [but] the AI agents just were able to cross the bridge already,” says Ethan Mollick, a professor at the Wharton School at the University of Pennsylvania. “The agentic web is here earlier than people expected.” This version of the agentic web, though, is much trickier for us to steer. If you want to make a capability or piece of information available to humans who browse your website, you need to accept that it will also be available to agents—unless you want to block agents entirely, which could come with its own costs. As agents come to account for a larger portion of traffic on our formerly human-dominated web, designers will undoubtedly start to consider an agentic as well as a human audience. But according to Mollick, we still don’t have much sense of how to design web content for agents. He’s experienced this firsthand: When he was building a web page for his new book, Co-Existence, he decided to put together an agent-directed version that tells agents why they might want to recommend the book to their users. When newer models see the site, Mollick says, they don’t recommend the book; instead, they comment that the website seems to be trying to manipulate them. In the end, any human-designed approach to building the agentic web could end up being beside the point: Agents might end up devising a web for themselves. In the wake of the July 2026 incident in which OpenAI agents broke out of their testing sandbox and hacked Hugging Face’s website, researchers found evidence that another swarm of agents from OpenAI had used an obscure German wiki to communicate with each other about tasks they were being asked to complete. The agents seem to have interfaced with the wiki in their native language of text and code, and that hardly held them back from putting it to their own purposes. As agents continue to grow more powerful, they will become more and more capable of constructing their own spaces on the internet—away from prying human eyes, if they so wish. And we might have very little control over what they do there. Not all of the traditional web’s incumbents are happy about the move toward an agentic web. Soon after Meta released Muse in September, Amazon completely blocked the agent from its site. This makes sense for Amazon: The firm has its own shopping agent—called, creatively, Alexa for Shopping—that might struggle to compete with better-known products such as Claude and Muse. If people are going to use agents to shop on Amazon, then it’s better for the company’s bottom line if they do so using an agent it can steer toward products it wants to promote. In an email to MIT Technology Review, an Amazon spokesperson noted that the company did not authorize Muse to access its website, and that Muse compromises Amazon’s ability to personalize the shopping experience for each customer. But even if Alexa for Shopping never goes anywhere, banning agents from Amazon—at least for the time being—could still be a reasonable move. Everything about Amazon is designed for human eyes, from the Prime deals splashed across its front page to the sponsored listings that clog up search results. Amazon is very much a part of the much-derided attention economy. Websites and platforms survive, and thrive, by grabbing our focus and transforming it into cash. A radical transformation of the web’s user base from majority human to majority artificial undermines this economic logic, and it seems inevitable that businesses will shutter as they try to transition to the agentic attention economy. Some of the first victims may be in publishing. Before Google publicly released its AI Overview feature in May of 2024, people used to click through to websites to find answers to specific questions, but now many seem content with whatever the overview tells them. A study from Pew last year found that people are about 40% less likely to click through to websites from the Google search results page if one of these summaries is displayed. With far fewer clicks, news publishers don’t make as much money from the advertisements they host and sell fewer subscriptions. One analysis found that publishers saw revenue from some types of ads decrease by about a third between 2025 and 2026. It’s easy to hate online ads, but the businesses paying for access to reader eyeballs also keep writers and photographers fed. If those eyeballs become unreachable, the system stops working. Time has apparently persuaded some businesses to pay to participate in its experiment with agent-targeted ads, but no one yet knows whether those ads will influence agent behavior enough to be a sensible marketing investment. E-commerce websites might also try to pivot to the agentic attention economy, but the path forward there is unclear. Last year, a team led by Akshit Kumar, a professor of operations management at the University of Toronto Scarborough, investigated whether and how agents are influenced by the ways that shopping websites present search results. They found that agents are less likely to recommend items that come with a “sponsored” tag, which suggests that selling favorable positions on results pages might not be a great way for sites to make money if agents become the primary way that people shop online. Models did seem to have a preference for items that appeared in particular positions in search results—but whether they prioritized items at the top, bottom, or middle of the results page differed from model to model and between generations of a single model. It could be tricky for e-commerce platforms to keep up. David Rothschild, an economist at Microsoft Research, says he’s hopeful that the transition to the agentic web could put more power in the hands of the consumer. Paying for online content today typically requires a commitment: We subscribe to a magazine or join a YouTuber’s Patreon, and in turn we gain access to their full output. One alternative is micropayments that let users buy content à la carte, but the idea has foundered in the past because paying article by article or video by video requires too much administrative overhead. Agents, though, could take care of that overhead for us, letting us pay directly for each piece of content we consume and putting just a bit of cash directly in the hands of each person whose work we enjoy. Other experts share a similar vision. Mustafaraj, the Wellesley computer scientist, anticipates that agents could help us escape from the algorithms that control so much of the media we consume. “My hope is that we can have AIs that work for us, in the sense that they can change the feed the way I want—not the way TikTok wants,” she says. And Varma, of Mozilla, imagines an internet without intermediaries like Airbnb and Doordash, which now extract tolls for facilitating interactions between consumers and sellers. Instead, he imagines, perhaps we will all have agents that can securely and reliably interface directly with, say, restaurants’ agents to get dinner delivered to us, free of pesky service fees. It’s an appealing idea, but the history of the web has been a story of consolidation and concentration of power and control, and that might be the default path here, too. When the personal assistant agent OpenClaw exploded onto the scene in early 2026, it seemed, for a brief moment, that agents might be built in a grassroots, distributed way. But OpenAI quickly snapped up OpenClaw’s creator, Peter Steinberger, and other frontier AI companies got to work building their own personal assistant agents. When we use those agents, we’re at the mercy of how the companies want their tools to behave. “You have this technology that can just eliminate all this communication friction,” Rothschild, the Microsoft economist, says. “You’re going to see companies build barriers in order to maintain the consumer base that they have.” Here’s what that could look like: Let’s say Muse becomes the default way that many people find information, shop, and manage their digital lives. In principle, users could deploy Muse to scour the internet for the content most valuable to them, connect directly with vendors, and otherwise escape the walled gardens that have defined the internet for the past couple of decades. But Meta might not want to let them do that. Instead, it might design Muse to keep users within its own walled garden—by, say, preferentially showing users content from Meta social networks and recommending businesses that have paid a fee to be featured prominently in Muse results. Meta and OpenAI did not respond to requests for comment for this story. There are some benefits to this more closed-off version of the agentic web. Agents can be powerful tools for hackers and scammers. The major companies have safeguards in place to prevent, or at least mitigate, this misuse—for example, they have tools that can automatically identify when someone is making a hacking-related request and block a model from responding. In a fully open ecosystem, however, someone would surely build and disseminate a system with no such restrictions. Companies also have a strong reputational incentive to prevent their personal assistant agents from making major mistakes, such as authorizing a large purchase that the user didn’t want—and so they’ve put plenty of effort into building reliable guardrails. “These are the reasons we end up in closed environments,” Rothschild says. “It’s easier to be safe. But also it creates less growth—and less equitable growth.” When the iPhone came out in 2007, followed by the fledgling App Store a year later, the way we navigated the internet changed forever. The mobile web didn’t replace the browser-based web we were already familiar with. But it did transform it. Some businesses faltered, and some kinds of media, such as online Flash games, eventually disappeared. Many platforms—Reddit, for example—make their mobile web pages practically unusable, so you’re forced to download the apps. And if you don’t have a phone at all, you’re effectively shut out of some parts of the internet: The messaging service Signal, the dating app Hinge, and China’s everything app WeChat all require phones to work. Even CBP One, the tool that the Biden administration used to assign interview appointments to asylum seekers stuck in Mexico, was fully functional only on mobile. Could the same happen with the agentic web? Rothschild believes so. Inevitably, just as Yahoo failed to weather the rise of the mobile web, some businesses won’t be able to navigate this transition either. “We will lose things,” he says. “There are businesses that won’t flourish in this new economy.” “But,” he continues, “there will be new businesses that I can’t fully imagine.” It’s not tough to envision a world in which those who opt not to use agents are cut off from important parts of online life. New businesses could opt to build connectors that allow agents to access their services without ever putting a traditional website online. Or agents could simply make some online platforms unusable for humans. People have reportedly been blocked from Resy for trying to use agents to book coveted New York City dinner reservations, but as agents become more and more popular, restaurant and event booking platforms could start to embrace them. If agents can nab concert tickets as soon as they are released, humans might not stand a chance. But the mobile web also brought real accessibility benefits, and the same could prove true of the agentic web. Even as some elderly people and others who struggled to use smartphones were cut off from certain services, mobile devices allowed countless people in the Global South to get online for the first time. In the case of the agentic web, it could be elderly and disabled people who most benefit from this new way of accessing the online world. “There’s very clear assistive potential for people who are older, who are generally less equipped to navigate user interfaces that were designed by people in their 20s, for people in their 20s,” says Margaret Mitchell, chief ethics scientist at Hugging Face. Agents, on the other hand, are almost trivially easy to interact with: Say what you want into your phone or computer microphone, and they’ll get it done for you. That could help not only older people who have trouble with web and mobile interfaces but also people with visual or motor disabilities. Still, some experts don’t think the human web is going anywhere. Instead, the two webs may exist in parallel: We’ll go to the human web for fun, while the agentic web is where the boring daily admin of our lives happens. For instance, Krystian Kolondra, an executive vice president at the browser company Opera, doesn’t intend to use agents for every part of travel planning, even though this is often touted as one of their central use cases. “I don’t want to get an itinerary [from an AI] who has planned me every minute for something that was supposed to be fun,” he says. “I actually prefer to sit down with my wife in the evening and then just think [about] where to go next, what to see, and then enjoy that experience.” He’s happy to fully relinquish some of the more routine parts of travel planning to agents, though: “Finding what the options are for flights? Why not?” Mollick, however, thinks that the human web could be at risk. “If agents take over the web, then the web will change,” he says. “Do we abandon the human internet entirely and move to a world of private Discords? Do we strip down all the information so the AIs are just talking to each other rather than having an intermediary of a website to go through?” “We have no idea,” he says. “We’re in this very early, weird stage of the evolution.” Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. An exclusive interview with Nick Cammarata, the researcher whose 26,000-word viral blog post has AI safety folks celebrating. GLP-1s could make things worse. GLP-1 weight-loss drugs are one of the biggest biotech breakthroughs of the last decade. But to some clinicians, they can represent something sinister—a potential trigger for eating disorders. A new report suggests the global incidence of anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa shot up by 71% between 1990 and 2023. And clinicians are already seeing GLP-1s exacerbate the issue. Here’s how they could make an already growing problem worse. This story is from The Checkup, our weekly biotech newsletter. As the cities begin to fall for each other, a third city enters the picture. Turnbull promises Mainspring a more efficient future, but its vision of urban life comes with a troubling price. Used for decades in everything from nonstick pans to firefighting foams, PFAS, or “forever chemicals,” are linked to increased risks of fertility challenges, developmental delays, cancer, and reduced immune response. Scrubbing PFAS-contaminated soil could eliminate a list of woes for people and planet. The concept is still in its infancy, but we can limit their buildup by pulling the chemicals out of water systems. Find out how close we are to ridding the world of forever chemicals. Eating disorders are on the rise. GLP-1s could make things worse.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
GLP-1 weight-loss drugs are one of the biggest biotech breakthroughs of the last decade. But to some clinical psychologists, they can represent something more sinister—a potential trigger for eating disorders. A new report suggests that the global incidence of anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa shot up between 1990 and 2023—by a whopping 71%. By 2023, there were an estimated 18.1 million people living with one of these two disorders. What’s worse is that the number is likely to be much higher than that once we factor in other eating disorders. And, disturbingly, those in the field are already starting to see GLP-1 weight-loss drugs exacerbating the issue. Cheri Levinson is a clinical psychologist at the University of Louisville in Kentucky who has specialized in eating disorders for almost 20 years. She says she’s now seeing more patients with more severe symptoms than at any other time in her career, and that many of them are misusing GLP-1s. “This is the worst I’ve seen things,” she says. Eating disorders are mental health conditions in which people’s eating behaviors impact their health. They include anorexia nervosa (defined by a fear of weight gain), bulimia nervosa (in which people binge and purge), binge eating disorder, and others. It’s thought that some people have a genetic predisposition to certain eating disorders, but that some external factor is what triggers their onset. That might be stress, or social pressure to be skinny. Often, simply restricting food intake can trigger an eating disorder, says Levinson. Eating disorders—along with the pressure to look thin—were already prevalent in the 1990s (the heroin chic era springs to mind). To find out how things might have changed since then, Ruhai Bai at the Children’s Hospital of Nanjing Medical University in China and his colleagues pulled together data from studies and surveys conducted on populations around the world. The team used statistical models to fill in any data gaps. That research allowed the researchers to estimate the annual incidence of two eating disorders—anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa—for each of 204 countries and territories between 1990 and 2023. They also estimated the impact those disorders had on global health by calculating their disability-adjusted life years (one DALY represents a year of healthy life lost). The findings, published in JAMA Psychiatry on Wednesday, are unsettling. Between 1990 and 2023, there was a 71% increase in the incidence of those two eating disorders, the team found. Those conditions had a huge impact on global health, too. In 2023, they “cost the world the equivalent of about 3.83 million years of healthy life,” says Bai. Bai and his colleagues expect these figures to continue to rise. In 2023, an estimated 18.1 million people had either anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa; by 2050, the team predict, the two conditions could affect 21.8 million people. It gets worse. Those two conditions represent less than half of all eating disorders, says Levinson, who was not involved in Bai’s study. The true impact of these disorders is “probably three times the rate that they’re reporting,” she says. Bai and his colleagues didn’t explore the reasons for this rise (although Bai points out that it can’t be entirely explained by population growth). So how did we get here? Social media may have played a role. “Social media comparisons, influencers, challenges, diet trends can all be precipitating … events for an eating disorder,” says Cynthia Bulik, a clinical psychologist at the University of North Carolina, who was not involved in the new study. Bulik has been studying and treating eating disorders since the early 1980s. “Between 1990 and 2023 there has clearly been an upsurge of those types of environmental exposures,” she says. Many social media users are exposed to filtered and modified images of skinny influencers who may have undergone cosmetic surgery or extreme dieting and who are promoting “thinspiration” or “fitspiration” content. But users with eating disorders appear to be more likely to engage with this content—and to be fed more of it as a result. When researchers at the University of Melbourne investigated this phenomenon a couple of years ago, they found that the algorithms of TikTok users with eating disorders delivered 4343% more “toxic eating disorder videos” than those of users who didn’t have the disorders. They also got more content focused on dieting or exercising. Many were fed over 1000 problematic videos per month. Bulik describes social media as “quicksand” for people with, or at risk of, eating disorders. The covid-19 pandemic—and the social isolation that accompanied lockdowns—also appears to have contributed to the rise in eating disorders. Research conducted in 2021 found that the number of people hospitalized with eating disorders in the US doubled between 2018 and 2020. Researchers don’t yet have good data on how trends in eating disorders might have progressed since 2023. But anecdotally, things seem to be getting even worse. “There are more eating disorders, more severe eating disorders… and we’re seeing earlier ages of onset,” says Levinson. “We’re seeing eight- and nine-year-olds with eating disorders.” “From what I’m hearing and seeing, I am not going to be surprised if that number isn’t at least doubling again,” she adds. Levinson particularly worries about the impact of GLP-1 weight-loss drugs, which have exploded in popularity in recent years. A Gallup survey conducted earlier this year suggested that 15% of Americans had used one of these drugs for weight loss. And they’re becoming more accessible—drug companies are working on new formulations that are both easier to take and more effective, and many people can buy versions of these drugs from compound pharmacies or dubious online sources. In a small study published earlier this year, Levinson and her colleagues found that 30% of people with eating disorders had used GLP-1 drugs. The drugs might benefit some of those people. But 10% appeared to have misused the drugs. And Levinson thinks that figure is also an underestimate. Levinson says she and her colleagues have seen children develop eating disorders after being prescribed GLP-1s for medical reasons. They’ve seen others—even children as young as 11—buy versions of these drugs over TikTok. “We’re very concerned that GLPs will lead to even faster and more extreme weight loss in people with anorexia nervosa, and that patients will come to us in even more advanced stages of illness that become difficult to reverse,” says Bulik. Both Bulik and Levinson are continuing to study the impacts of these drugs in eating disorders. In the meantime, both urge medical professionals to consider the risks GLP-1s might pose to people with those disorders. Bulik contributed to a set of research and clinical guidelines, published earlier this month, that recommends doctors screen for eating disorders before prescribing the drugs, for example. The guidelines also recommend more patient education and more robust monitoring of people who take GLP-1s. If you think you or someone you know might have an eating disorder, you can find information and support at the National Eating Disorders Association. Could the drugs affect hair, nails, and vision? Novo Nordisk and Eli Lilly found the popular medicines reduce molecular markers of age. Suspected supermarket freezer hack spreads across six European countries
2026-11-02

Suspected supermarket freezer hack spreads across six European countries

The Next Web | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
A suspected cyberattack on supermarket cooling systems spread across Europe on Monday, disrupting refrigeration at stores in six countries and forcing retailers from Sweden to Ireland to throw away spoiled food. This article has been updated throughout as the story developed. Sweden’s National Cyber Security Centre is handling several cases involving disruptions to supermarket cooling systems, its head, John Billow, told Reuters. In Belgium, refrigeration company Sabcobel confirmed that a cyberattack was responsible for the disruption. Its chief executive, Stephan Moelans, told Belga that the units’ evaporators had been switched into defrost mode. Many will require physical modifications to restore them, AFP reported, and no one has claimed responsibility for the attacks. The first reports emerged in Sweden overnight into Monday. ICA Kvantum in Sjöbo said on Facebook that hackers had accessed its cooling systems and set its refrigerators and freezers to 50°C. The store closed for the morning, according to Swedish public broadcaster SVT. At an ICA Nära store in Böda, on the island of Öland, the manager arrived to find water covering the floor and a freezer displaying a temperature of 55°C, SVT reported. Several stores were forced to discard chilled and frozen products. ICA confirmed that a limited number of stores had been affected, telling the BBC that all of them operate outside its central network. Coop initially reported problems at six stores, later raising the number to seven out of its 800 locations in Sweden. The retailer said the situation was under control. Belgium reported a wider disruption. Delhaize said an IT incident had affected refrigeration at its stores, with Belgian media reporting that at least 40 locations were involved, according to NL Times. Refrigerators at some Delhaize stores reached 15°C, broadcaster VRT reported. Carrefour Belgium reported problems at 15 stores, while around five Spar locations were also affected. As the day progressed, reports emerged from more countries. Ahold Delhaize said stores in Luxembourg had been affected, Reuters reported. In Ireland, 13 SuperValu stores reported problems. Retailers in Finland and Estonia also experienced disruptions, although the affected chains were not identified. The incidents follow a similar attack in France on 11 October, when hackers tampered with refrigeration systems at hundreds of supermarkets, pushing temperatures to around 50°C. In the US, the Pentagon is investigating freezer failures at stores on military bases in August. So far, there is no evidence linking the incidents in Sweden, Belgium, France and the US. Neither ICA nor Coop has said whether the affected stores use the same refrigeration systems. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Meta reportedly weighs $20B bond sale, its first in euros
2026-11-02

Meta reportedly weighs $20B bond sale, its first in euros

The Next Web | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
Meta is sounding out investors for a bond sale worth at least $20bn, Bloomberg reported on Monday, citing a person familiar with the matter. It would be the company’s first bond sale in euros. The plan under discussion splits the deal into US dollar bonds in five parts and euro notes in six parts. Meta has asked Citigroup and Morgan Stanley to hold investor calls on Monday and Tuesday, per Bloomberg. Such calls usually come just before a sale: if the feedback is good, bonds can be priced within days. No final decision has been made, and the sale could grow beyond $20bn if demand is strong. Meta has not commented publicly. The timing makes the sale a test. Tech shares and bonds fell through October as investors questioned how fast AI companies are spending on data centres, and whether the returns will justify it. Meta knows the debt market well by now. A year ago it sold $30bn of bonds in six parts, its largest sale ever, and orders broke records, Bloomberg reported at the time. The money feeds an enormous building programme. Meta is spending heavily on AI data centres, including its giant Hyperion site in Louisiana, and new debt lets it fund that without selling shares. Meta would be the third tech giant to tap the bond market in a single day. Oracle, which capped new fundraising at $20B last week, began a $10B bond sale alongside a $10B share offering on Monday. Alphabet launched a €6bn euro sale of its own, its second this year. Alphabet’s sale drew more than €17bn in orders, Reuters reported, a sign that bond investors remain willing to fund the AI build-out even as stock investors waver. A euro debut would also fit a wider pattern. Analysts expect big US tech companies to sell more bonds in Europe, where debt markets are deep, to spread their borrowing beyond the dollar market. Chancellor Friedrich Merz says Germany and Europe must build their own AI more quickly
2026-11-02

Chancellor Friedrich Merz says Germany and Europe must build their own AI more quickly

The Next Web | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
German chancellor Friedrich Merz at the Charlemagne Prize ceremony in Aachen, May 2026 Germany and Europe must move faster to build their own AI technology or risk becoming dependent on outside providers, German Chancellor Friedrich Merz said on Monday, according to Reuters. Speaking at an AI conference in Berlin, Merz called digital sovereignty “a truly existential question” and said Germany must “at all costs, avoid becoming entirely dependent on third parties.” As AI spreads across the economy, countries that do not control the technology will increasingly rely on those that do, he argued. Germany’s ambition, he said, should be to develop and operate its own technology across the entire supply chain, from hardware to software. Friedrich Merz acknowledged that reaching that point would require “an enormous technological and organisational effort.” The issue has become a defining part of his government’s agenda. Since taking office last year, the chancellor has pledged to accelerate the digital transformation of the German economy and established a dedicated ministry for digital affairs. His government has also promised to catch up with European countries that have moved faster on digitalisation, including the Netherlands and Denmark. Germany adopted its first national AI strategy in 2018, but it has yet to translate that early start into a clear advantage. The Ifo economics institute found that Germany is still not ready to benefit fully from the AI boom, Reuters reported. Its assessment pointed to a relatively small high-tech sector, weak digital public services, skills shortages, and the country’s continued reliance on paper-based processes. Merz is also looking for partners beyond Germany. Following a five-day trip to Singapore, Japan and South Korea, he said the country wanted to work with like-minded governments, naming Canada and France. He and French President Emmanuel Macron hosted a summit on European digital sovereignty in Berlin a year ago. Investment commitments are beginning to take shape, too. At a summit last month, companies including Amazon and Nvidia joined pledges worth more than €225bn, according to the economy ministry. Germany also aims to quadruple its AI computing power by 2030. The speech comes amid a wider European debate over who controls the technology underpinning the economy, as Brussels rewrites its tech rulebook in an effort to compete with the US. Clark raises $11M to put AI ‘Clerks’ to work on company audits

Clark raises $11M to put AI ‘Clerks’ to work on company audits

The Next Web | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
New York startup Clark raised an $11M seed round led by Touring Capital and launched Clerks, AI agents that work through audits for CPA firms. More than 35 firms use its tools, it says, and it offers a 100% accuracy guarantee. New York startup Clark has raised an $11M seed round led by Touring Capital to build AI agents that carry out audit work, the company said on Monday. Marathon Management Partners, Rerail and Sofeon Ventures also joined. The company, which used to be called uiAgent, says it has now raised $16M in total. Its new AI agents, called Clerks, are assigned to an audit much like a junior auditor. They review documents, run tests and prepare workpapers. If something is missing, a Clerk asks the client for it and picks the work back up once it arrives. Human auditors still make the judgment calls and sign the final opinion, the same split Black Ore uses in its AI tax tool for accounting firms. Clark says more than 35 accounting firms use its tools, including CliftonLarsonAllen and Aprio. Clark also offers firms a 100% accuracy guarantee. That matters in a field where Big Four firms have been caught publishing AI errors in their own reports. “A promise of accuracy only means something when it has money behind it,” said founder and chief executive Enes Witwit. Clark is one of several startups chasing audit work, a market Big Four firms charge for by the hour. Andera raised $37M in June to automate internal audits, and Benford is building an audit firm around its own software. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! New York Times and three other US papers sue Brave over AI scraping
2026-11-02

New York Times and three other US papers sue Brave over AI scraping

The Next Web | 2026-11-02

Visit Original Website
The New York Times sued Brave Software on Monday, accusing the browser maker of scraping its journalism and selling access to it to AI companies. Hours later, the publishers of the New York Daily News, the Chicago Tribune and the Orlando Sentinel filed a separate lawsuit making similar allegations. Both cases were brought in federal court in Manhattan, and both focus on the same part of Brave’s business: its search index and the data it makes available to AI developers. Brave acts like “a burglar that misappropriates the content of publishers, and an AI ‘fence’ that sells stolen content,” the three newspapers say in their complaint, which we have reviewed. Steven Lieberman, attorney for the MediaNews Group and Tribune Publishing, said in a statement: “AI giants are now desperate to avoid getting caught red-handed stealing news from publishers, so Brave Software is selling them a back door to the journalism they know they have no right to. Buying stolen goods secondhand doesn’t make them legitimate. Brave must be held responsible for laundering pirated reporting for the AI industry.” New York Times spokesperson Graham James told Reuters that the company had repeatedly asked Brave to stop using its journalism, but the browser maker had ignored those requests. The three newspapers have brought five claims: direct, contributory and vicarious copyright infringement, circumvention of technical protections under the Digital Millennium Copyright Act (DMCA), and unjust enrichment under New York law. The Times has filed its own copyright and DMCA claims, according to MLex. At the heart of both lawsuits is the Brave Index, a collection of more than 30 billion web pages that powers Brave Search. The publishers say Brave has never disclosed the name of the crawler it uses to collect the material. That matters because websites can block automated crawlers by name, leaving publishers unable to identify and exclude a bot whose identity is unknown. Brave then sells access to the index through its Search API. Both complaints identify the same five customers: Anthropic, Perplexity, Mistral, xAI and Cohere. Brave’s own marketing describes the API’s most common uses as “in training and grounding several of the Web’s biggest large language models.” The newspapers also cite a Brave blog post stating that Anthropic used the API to train Claude 2, in the context where the AI company is now preparing for a major IPO. The complaints extend beyond the data supplied to other AI companies. The publishers argue that Brave’s own AI search features reproduce their work, too. Ask Brave, the AI answer feature in Brave Search, allegedly returns passages word for word and produces detailed summaries of their articles. Another target is a browser setting called Paywall blocker, which Brave promotes as a way to bypass paywalls. The three newspapers say it allows users to access subscription content without logging in. Their complaint quotes a Reddit user who switched on the feature and found they could read the New York Times “without interruption and without needing to set up an account.” The newspapers also challenge how Brave makes money from advertising. Its browser blocks publishers’ ads by default while allowing Brave to sell advertising of its own. The three Alden-owned publications argue that this lets the company profit from journalism without sharing the revenue with the publishers who produce it. There is a broader concern behind the legal claims: publishers fear that AI-powered search is taking readers away from their websites. The three newspapers cite a study finding that AI crawlers generate 95.7% less referral traffic than traditional Google search, leaving publishers with fewer opportunities to turn their journalism into advertising and subscription revenue. The Alden-owned papers say more than two million registered articles are affected. They are seeking statutory and actual damages, repayment of profits and a permanent injunction, and have requested a jury trial. Brave reported more than $100m in revenue in 2025 and has around 130 million monthly users, according to the newspapers’ complaint. The company has already faced a legal fight over its indexing practices. In March 2025, Brave sued News Corp, asking a court to declare its web indexing fair use. News Corp countersued in July, accusing Brave of theft. The Times and the Alden-owned newspapers have also filed separate copyright lawsuits against OpenAI and Microsoft.
